# Chapter 3  
Mathematical Modeling for the Calculation of Porous Structure Properties: Techniques and Applications

 

## 3.1 INTRODUCTION

Designing porous structures from the bottom up requires a comprehensive approach that integrates pore‐level design with the overall structural framework. Achieving this necessitates a methodology capable of linking microscale features to macroscale behavior. This chapter presents the foundational principles of material properties and microstructure, alongside the historical evolution of methods that ultimately led to the development of the homogenization technique. Recognized for its efficiency, homogenization serves as a versatile and cost‐effective strategy for designing advanced microstructures in diverse applications.

 

## 3.2 OVERVIEW OF COMPUTATIONAL ASSESSMENTS OF PHYSICAL PROPERTIES OF MATERIALS

The foundational tenets of atomic theory form the cornerstone of contemporary chemistry, offering insights into the intricate composition and inherent properties of matter. The evolution of atomic theory spans over two millennia, beginning with the philosophical contemplations of Aristotle and Democritus. Aristotle proposed that matter could be infinitely divisible without altering its fundamental nature, whereas Democritus introduced the notion of an indivisible entity, termed the “atom,” derived from the Greek word “atomos,” signifying indivisibility. This intellectual discourse persisted until the early 19th century when John Dalton, through rigorous experimentation, provided empirical validation for the existence of atoms. Dalton's atomic theory posited that matter is composed of indivisible particles known as atoms, which are neither created nor destroyed. He further asserted that all atoms of a particular element are identical in mass and properties and that compounds are formed by the combination of distinct types of atoms in fixed ratios. While Dalton's model was groundbreaking, it lacked the understanding of subatomic particles such as neutrons and electrons, which are yet to be discovered.

The advancement of atomic theory gained momentum in the late 19th and early 20th centuries, with pivotal contributions from physicists like J.J. Thomson and Ernest Rutherford. Thomson's cathode ray experiments led to the discovery of the electron, a negatively charged subatomic particle, thus challenging Dalton's concept of indivisible atoms. Thomson introduced the plum pudding model, which envisioned electrons embedded within a positively charged matrix. This model was later refuted by Rutherford's gold foil experiment, which uncovered that atoms consist of a dense, positively charged nucleus surrounded by a cloud of electrons. Rutherford's nuclear model demonstrated that the atom is predominantly an empty space, with nearly all of its mass concentrated in the nucleus.

The subsequent refinement of atomic theory was achieved with Niels Bohr's quantized shell model in 1913, which elucidated the stability of electron orbits around the nucleus. Bohr introduced the concept of quantized energy levels, whereby electrons could only occupy specific orbits without emitting energy. This model laid the groundwork for modern quantum mechanics, which offers a more comprehensive understanding of atomic structure and electron behavior.

The exploration of atomic theory extends to the standard model of particle physics, which delineates the fundamental particles and forces constituting matter. The standard model identifies four fundamental forces: the strong force, the weak force, the electromagnetic force, and gravity. The strong and weak forces operate within the atomic nucleus, governing subatomic interactions, while the electromagnetic force and gravity possess infinite ranges but vary significantly in strength. The strong force, the most potent, binds protons and neutrons within the nucleus, whereas the weak force is responsible for radioactive decay. The electromagnetic force, far stronger than gravity, governs interactions between charged particles, while gravity, despite being the weakest force, exerts an infinite‐range influence on all matter.

Understanding atomic structure requires grasping key concepts such as atomic number, mass number, isotopes, and atomic mass. The atomic number, representing the number of protons in an atom, defines the element and remains constant across all isotopes of that element. For instance, carbon always has six protons. In a neutral atom, the number of electrons equals the number of protons, balancing the charges. The mass number, however, is the sum of protons and neutrons in the nucleus and varies among isotopes of the same element. Carbon, for example, has three naturally occurring isotopes: carbon‐12, carbon‐13, and carbon‐14, with mass numbers of 12, 13, and 14, respectively. The average atomic mass of an element, as listed on the periodic table, reflects the weighted average of all its isotopes based on their natural abundance.

Calculating the average atomic mass involves multiplying the mass of each isotope by its relative abundance and summing these values. For instance, copper has two stable isotopes with mass numbers of 63 and 65. If copper‐63 has an abundance of 69.2% and an atomic mass of 62.93 atomic mass units (amu) and copper‐65 has an abundance of 30.8% with an atomic mass of 64.93 amu, the average atomic mass is calculated as: (62.93 amu \* 0.692) + (64.93 amu \* 0.308), resulting in an average atomic mass of approximately 63.55 amu. This value corresponds with the atomic mass listed on the periodic table, demonstrating how the contributions of different isotopes are integrated to represent an element's average atomic mass.

For scientists and engineers, the properties of materials at the atomic and subatomic levels are fundamentally tied to the arrangement and energetic states of atoms. For example, the mechanical strength of a material depends on its specific characteristics. In metallic materials, strength is primarily governed by the freedom of electrons, which defines the metallic bonding and structure.

In solid materials, these bulk properties are often conceptualized through the averaging of electronic configurations and energy levels, considering the spatial dimensions of atomic nuclei. This abstraction is rooted in the understanding that electron behavior functions as the cohesive force binding atoms into a solid structure. Consequently, a core objective of condensed matter theory is to accurately determine the electronic structure (ES) of solids.

ES calculations, grounded in ab initio (first‐principles) methodologies, allow for predictions of the structural and dynamic properties of solids. These calculations are based on fundamental quantum theory, requiring the atomic numbers of constituent atoms and basic structural information as input. Within the framework of density functional theory (DFT), the complex many‐body problem of electron motion is reduced to a single electron moving in an effective potential. This approach has become a standard in computational material science, enabling the analysis and prediction of material properties with remarkable accuracy.

While the formulation of quantum mechanical equations for ESs (which account for all known interactions between electrons and atomic nuclei in solids) is conceptually straightforward, solving these equations in their entirety remains a formidable challenge due to their intrinsic complexity. To address this, various approximations are employed. Despite their simplifications, these approximations often yield thorough solutions.

The computational demands of solving the ES problem are immense, necessitating substantial computational resources. Before the advent of advanced, high‐speed computing, practical ES calculations in solids were exceedingly rare. However, the rapid development and increased availability of computational power as well as numerical methods have revolutionized the field, giving rise to the discipline of computational material science.

Advances in computational power, alongside the development of innovative computational methods and algorithms, have dramatically improved the precision of ES calculations. These advancements enable highly accurate solutions to the one‐electron Kohn–Sham equation for solids, providing unparalleled details at the atomic and electronic scales. This capability not only allows for the simulation of experimental conditions but also facilitates the design of new molecules and materials, predicting their properties prior to synthesis. Computational simulations offer atomic‐scale insights that are often inaccessible through experimental methods, providing a complementary approach to empirical studies. Unlike semi‐empirical methods, which rely on adjustable parameters tailored to the properties of ground‐state structures and may lack transferability to non‐equilibrium configurations, ab initio calculations remain reliable even under non‐equilibrium conditions. This reliability renders them indispensable in multi‐scale modeling of materials, serving two critical functions: (i) addressing scenarios where electronic effects are pivotal and require first‐principles treatment and (ii) generating data for the development of interatomic potentials.

The influence of computational materials science extends beyond merely predicting and understanding material properties. It also enables the design of new materials with customized properties, accelerating discovery and minimizing the need for costly and time‐intensive experimental trials. By harnessing high‐performance computing and advanced algorithms, researchers can explore vast parameter spaces, identifying promising material candidates for a wide range of applications, from electronics to energy storage and beyond.

As theoretical approaches and computational technologies continue to advance, the role of computational methods in materials science is expanding. With an in‐depth understanding of ESs and enhanced computational capabilities, the potential for discovering novel materials and uncovering new physical phenomena is growing.

Polymeric and ceramic materials, like their metallic counterparts, exhibit unique properties rooted in their molecular and atomic structures. Polymers, composed of long, repeating chains of molecules, are characterized by their versatility, lightweight nature, and wide range of mechanical behaviors, which can be tailored through chemical composition and processing techniques. Ceramics, on the other hand, are typically inorganic, non‐metallic materials that are prized for their hardness, thermal stability, and resistance to chemical degradation. Unlike metals, which exhibit ductility and malleability, ceramics are generally brittle, though advancements in ceramic engineering have led to the development of toughened ceramics with enhanced mechanical performance. Both polymeric and ceramic materials play critical roles in modern engineering applications, where their distinct properties are leveraged for specific functional requirements.

The advent of composite materials, which combine two or more distinct phases (typically a matrix and a reinforcement), has revolutionized engineering materials, offering a means to achieve superior properties unattainable by any single material alone. Composites are categorized based on the nature of their constituent materials and the arrangement of the reinforcing phase, leading to classifications such as fiber‐reinforced, particle‐reinforced, and structural composites. The design and optimization of composite structures demand developing numerical methods that deal with the vast range of possible material combinations and configurations, each yielding distinct mechanical properties. These methods are essential for predicting the behavior of composites under various loading conditions, enabling engineers to tailor material properties to specific applications with high precision.

As the complexity of composite structures increases, so too does the necessity for advanced numerical techniques to accurately calculate their mechanical properties. The semi‐infinite combinations of composite materials, coupled with the variability in reinforcement distribution, necessitate a departure from purely empirical approaches. Instead, computational methods have become indispensable for deriving the mechanical properties of composites from the experimentally measured properties of their base materials. This approach allows for the efficient exploration of the material design space, enabling the prediction and optimization of composite performance in a virtual environment before physical prototypes are fabricated.

 

## 3.3 MECHANICAL PROPERTIES OF MICROSTRUCTURE

The development of the design and numerical analysis of composite materials in recent years has been remarkable, leading to the emergence of materials with a variety of new mechanical and chemical properties. These advancements have catalyzed the consideration of composite materials for practical applications in various industrial sectors, particularly as structural members.

Despite the rapid material advancements, progress in understanding the mechanics of these new composites has lagged significantly. This discrepancy is primarily due to an insufficient grasp of the mechanical properties essential for developing structural mechanics tailored to composite materials. For composites to be effectively utilized in structures and buildings, it is imperative to advance not only the fundamental mechanics of composite materials but also their structural mechanics.

Currently, the research focus is heavily skewed toward the synthesis of composite materials and the exploration of basic fracture mechanics. This narrow focus has resulted in a fragile foundational understanding, which is inadequate for the application of composites as structural materials. The disproportionate emphasis on the microscopic aspects of composite materials often leads to the neglect of macroscopic perspectives. A holistic understanding that integrates both scales is crucial for the effective processing and application of composites. The processing of composite materials can only be truly feasible when there is a profound interconnection between macroscopic and microscopic mechanics.

Historically, research has dedicated substantial effort to deriving structural equations for the macroscopic mechanics of composite materials, equations that ostensibly reflect their microscopic structures. However, this approach has often overlooked the reciprocal influence, where macroscopic information could inform and optimize microscopic structures to attain better design. Consequently, this one‐sided focus has stymied the development of a better mechanics framework for processing composite materials. However, the challenge of computationally integrating stress and strain considerations across scales has delayed the evolution of processing mechanics and structural mechanics.

Numerical methods for evaluating composite materials are of immense importance as they not only enable the prediction of composite behavior based on the properties of their constituent materials but also assist in designing new composites with improved performance. Moreover, advancements in designing porous structures for additive manufacturing rely on accurately predicting the behavior of the microstructure—specifically, the pores and their interactions with the overall macrostructure, i.e., the topology of the porous structure.

As discussed in the following chapter, the asymptotic homogenization method, originally developed for evaluating composite materials, is one of the most widely used techniques today. Therefore, we will explore key methods for evaluating composite materials, starting with mixing rules and their limitations and culminating in advanced approaches that integrate varying fields, such as stress and strain analysis at the microscale.

 

### 3.3.1 Mixing Rule

Let us discuss the classical configuration of composite material mechanics which involves alternating materials of A and B in phasis, as depicted in [Figure 3.1](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0001).

![Figure 3.1 Analytical logic for composite material spatial configuration.](images/c03f001.png)

[**Figure 3.1**](#c03.xhtml#R_c03-fig-0001) Analytical logic for composite material spatial configuration.

Let us derive the formula for such a model. Consider plates A and B with thicknesses *t*[a] and *t*[b], respectively, arranged alternately. When stress is applied perpendicularly to the plates, the total strain can be considered as the sum of the strains in each individual plate.

The parameters *α*[a] and *α*[b] represent the volume fractions of materials a and b, respectively, assuming that the areas of the plates are identical.

The classification of stress–strain relationships varies depending on the material configuration. As illustrated in [Figure 3.2](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0002)(a), when the displacement field is continuous across the material phases, the strain fields for both materials, A and B, within the composite are identical. Consequently, the overall stress is the combined contribution of the stresses from materials A and B. This scenario is referred to as the iso‐strain.

![Figure 3.2 The stress–strain relations for composite configurations: (a) Iso‐strain and (b) iso‐stress.](images/c03f002.png)

[**Figure 3.2**](#c03.xhtml#R_c03-fig-0002) The stress–strain relations for composite configurations: (a) Iso‐strain and (b) iso‐stress.

In contrast, the configuration depicted in [Figure 3.2](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0002)(b) exhibits uniform stress across materials A and B. In this case, the displacement and strain fields represent the combined contributions of materials A and B. This scenario is referred to as the iso‐stress.

The rule of mixtures provides a framework to estimate the effective properties of a composite based on the properties and volume fractions of its constituents. When considering the elastic modulus of a composite, two primary conditions—iso‐strain and iso‐stress—are used to establish the upper and lower bounds, respectively.

The iso‐strain condition represents an ideal case where there are perfect load transfer and intimate bonding between the phases. The effective modulus of the composite in this case is a simple weighted average of the moduli of the individual components, proportional to their respective volume fractions. This approach generally predicts the upper bound of the composite's modulus, reflecting the maximum stiffness achievable when the reinforcing phase is aligned with the direction of the applied stress.

Conversely, the iso‐stress condition assumes that the stress is uniformly distributed across the matrix and reinforcement phases. This scenario might occur when there is imperfect bonding or when the load is applied transversely to the reinforcement. Here, the effective modulus is calculated using a harmonic mean, where the volume fractions are inversely related to the moduli of the components. This method typically predicts the lower bound of the composite's modulus, representing the minimum stiffness, which might occur under suboptimal conditions, such as poor load transfer or misalignment of the reinforcement.

For an elastic two‐phase composite, the stiffness bounds provided by the Voigt and Reuss models serve as the main calculated bounds on the Young's modulus based on the volume fractions of the constituent phases. These bounds are applicable regardless of the geometric configuration of the phases within the composite. The Voigt model (upper bound in [Figure 3.3](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0003)), representing the upper bound, assumes a scenario where the strain is uniformly distributed across both phases. The Young's modulus for the Voigt composite is given by

(3.5)![upper E Subscript e q Baseline equals upper E Subscript normal a Baseline a Subscript normal a Baseline plus upper E Subscript normal b Baseline a Subscript normal b](images/c03-math-0005.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <msub> <mi>E</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">eq</mi> </msub> <mo>=</mo> <msub> <mi>E</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi> </msub> <msub> <mi>a</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi> </msub> <mo>+</mo> <msub> <mi>E</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">b</mi> </msub> <msub> <mi>a</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">b</mi> </msub> </math> ![Figure 3.3 Classical Voigt–Reuss upper and lower bounds.](images/c03f003.png)

[**Figure 3.3**](#c03.xhtml#R_c03-fig-0003) Classical Voigt–Reuss upper and lower bounds.

Conversely, the Reuss model (lower bound in [Figure 3.3](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0003)), representing the lower bound, assumes that the stress is uniformly distributed across the phases. The Young's modulus for the Reuss composite is given by

(3.6)![StartFraction 1 Over upper E Subscript e q Baseline EndFraction equals StartFraction a Subscript normal a Baseline Over upper E Subscript normal a Baseline EndFraction plus StartFraction a Subscript normal b Baseline Over upper E Subscript normal b Baseline EndFraction](images/c03-math-0006.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <mfrac> <mn>1</mn> <msub> <mi>E</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">eq</mi> </msub> </mfrac> <mo>=</mo> <mfrac> <msub> <mi>a</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi> </msub> <msub> <mi>E</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi> </msub> </mfrac> <mo>+</mo> <mfrac> <msub> <mi>a</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">b</mi> </msub> <msub> <mi>E</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">b</mi> </msub> </mfrac> </math> 

where the reciprocal relationship reflects the series configuration of the phases, again neglecting Poisson's effects.

The representation of the Voigt–Reuss bounds presents certain limitations, particularly in their sensitivity to loading conditions. This variability introduces significant discrepancies in the calculation of composite material properties, especially within the design space—the region delineated between the upper and lower bounds as depicted in [Figure 3.3](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0003). The inherent uncertainties in this range necessitate the application of substantial safety factors, which are especially critical in high‐risk domains such as aerospace engineering. The imposition of these safety factors often requires an increase in both the volume of the filler material and the overall size of the structural component. This, in turn, leads to a marked increase in weight, which severely compromises performance and can undermine the primary advantage of using composite materials in the first place—namely, their potential for lightweight, high‐strength applications. Consequently, while the Voigt–Reuss bounds offer a foundational framework for understanding the composite behavior, their limitations underscore the need for more precise methods.

To address these challenges, extensive research efforts were undertaken in the United States, funded by the Department of Defense and NASA. These initiatives led to the development of more rigorous and accurate theoretical bounds, particularly for isotropic composites. The work culminated in the establishment of the Hashin–Shtrikman bounds [\[1\]](#c03.xhtml#c03-bib-0001), which offer a more precise estimation of the composite modulus compared to earlier models like Voigt–Reuss model (as illustrated in [Figure 3.4](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0004)). As illustrated in the figure, Hashin–Shtrikman bounds are notably tighter, thereby significantly reducing the safety factor required in engineering designs without compromising the structural integrity and safety of the materials. This advancement marked a critical step forward in optimizing the performance and reliability of composite materials used in high‐stakes applications, particularly in aerospace engineering.

 

### 3.3.2 Need for Homogenization Method

Assume that we apply classical laminate theory to perform stress analysis on the entire composite material. This approach allows us to calculate the strain and stress distribution in each reinforcing plate. The resulting stress values represent the average stress, assuming that the reinforced plate is a homogeneous equivalent rather than a composite material. Consequently, the stress experienced by the reinforcing plate fibers and the matrix will differ significantly.

![Figure 3.4 The Hashin–Shtrikman bounds.](images/c03f004.png)

[**Figure 3.4**](#c03.xhtml#R_c03-fig-0004) The Hashin–Shtrikman bounds.

Even if the average stress has been calculated, what will be the actual stress distribution? It remains uncertain whether the calculated average stress accurately reflects the true conditions. When considering cutting reinforced plates or other material processing, understanding this average stress alone is insufficient for formulating the mechanics of processing. If we cannot determine the actual stress within the matrix or fibers themselves, our analysis is incomplete.

Moreover, the theories mentioned earlier fall short of addressing questions about the impact of changes in fiber cross‐sectional shape. Suppose we consider reinforcing fibers with a circular cross‐section (as shown in [Figure 3.5](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0005)), combining fibers of different sizes. Given the arbitrary nature of their arrangement, classical composite mechanics fall short of fully accounting for the effects of this arrangement on the overall structure.

![Figure 3.5 Same volume fraction with different topological distribution composites.](images/c03f005.png)

[**Figure 3.5**](#c03.xhtml#R_c03-fig-0005) Same volume fraction with different topological distribution composites.

Is there a method that can thoroughly explain the conventional dynamical model while addressing the aforementioned questions? One widely used approach today is homogenization, which has been extensively studied in the field of applied mathematics.

 

### 3.3.3 Microstructure with Periodicity

In general composite materials, replicating a perfectly periodic microstructure is practically impossible due to manufacturing constraints. However, for creating a mathematical dynamic model, we can assume approximate periodicity to construct an idealized model.

To target various composite materials, we can abstractly assume a microstructure with periodicity in the vicinity of arbitrary points. By doing so, we can consider points A and B with slightly different periods and microstructures, allowing for the modeling of composite materials with varying properties at different points.

To express this periodicity and fine structure, we introduce the local coordinate y within the fine structure (as illustrated in [Figure 3.6](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0006)). If the general coordinate representing a composite material structure is denoted as *x*, then the two coordinates *x* and *y* can be expressed using parameters *ω* that represent the scaling for factoring the actual size of the microstructure, establishing the following relationship:

(3.7)![y equals StartFraction x Over omega EndFraction](images/c03-math-0007.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <mi>y</mi> <mo>=</mo> <mfrac> <mi>x</mi> <mi>&#969;</mi> </mfrac> </math> 

The scaling serves to unitarize the problem.

![Figure 3.6 Asymptotic homogenization problem.](images/c03f006.png)

[**Figure 3.6**](#c03.xhtml#R_c03-fig-0006) Asymptotic homogenization problem.

As shown in the example in [Figure 3.6](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0006), when articulated through the unit cell model, the smallest representative and ergodic microstructure can be characterized within a domain of 0.4 mm (400 μm). Specifically, let the macroscopic structure be defined by the coordinate *x* = 400 μm. Correspondingly, the microstructure's local coordinate system, denoted by *y*, is normalized to unity. When the size of the macrostructure is significantly larger than the microstructure, we can consider the limit as *ω* → 0. The homogenization method assumes a periodic fine structure and mathematically explores the behavior of the composite material as *ω* → 0.

 

### 3.3.4 Principles of Virtual Work

To describe composite materials with microstructures, we denote the elastic tensor, displacement, strain, and stress as *E*^ω, *u*^ω, *ε*^ω, and *σ*^ω, respectively. Here, the superscript indices represent the microstructure. The functions required for such a mechanical formulation use the global coordinate *x*, representing the entire structure, as a variable. Simultaneously, due to the fine structure, these functions depend on the coordinate *y* of the unit cell model that represents the fine structure. Therefore, for example, the displacement *u* and the elastic tensor *E* can be expressed as the functions of both *x* and *y*.

(3.8)![StartLayout Enlarged left-brace 1st Row u Superscript normal omega Baseline equals u left-parenthesis x comma y right-parenthesis 2nd Row upper E Superscript normal omega Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals upper E left-parenthesis x comma y right-parenthesis EndLayout](images/c03-math-0008.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <mfenced open="{" close="" separators=""><mtable> <mtr> <mtd> <msup> <mi>u</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#969;</mi> </msup> <mo>=</mo> <mi>u</mi> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow> <mi>x</mi> <mo>,</mo> <mi>y</mi> </mrow> </mfenced> </mtd> </mtr> <mtr> <mtd> <msup> <mi>E</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#969;</mi> </msup> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mi>x</mi> </mfenced> <mo>=</mo> <mi>E</mi> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow> <mi>x</mi> <mo>,</mo> <mi>y</mi> </mrow> </mfenced> </mtd> </mtr> </mtable> </mfenced> </math> 

Since we assume the periodicity of the microstructure, it becomes possible to express the function as follows:

where *Y* is a constant vector that determines the periodic structure.

In the homogenization method, the displacement is decomposed into

(3.15)![u Superscript omega Baseline equals u left-parenthesis x comma y right-parenthesis equals u 0 left-parenthesis x comma y right-parenthesis plus omega u 1 left-parenthesis x comma y right-parenthesis plus omega squared u 2 left-parenthesis x comma y right-parenthesis plus ellipsis](images/c03-math-0015.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <msup> <mi>u</mi> <mi>&#969;</mi> </msup> <mo>=</mo> <mi>u</mi> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow> <mi>x</mi> <mo>,</mo> <mi>y</mi> </mrow> </mfenced> <mo>=</mo> <msub> <mi>u</mi> <mn>0</mn> </msub> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow> <mi>x</mi> <mo>,</mo> <mi>y</mi> </mrow> </mfenced> <mo>+</mo> <mi>&#969;</mi> <msub> <mi>u</mi> <mn>1</mn> </msub> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow> <mi>x</mi> <mo>,</mo> <mi>y</mi> </mrow> </mfenced> <mo>+</mo> <msup> <mi>&#969;</mi> <mn>2</mn> </msup> <msub> <mi>u</mi> <mn>2</mn> </msub> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow> <mi>x</mi> <mo>,</mo> <mi>y</mi> </mrow> </mfenced> <mo form="postfix">+</mo> <mo>&#8230;</mo> </math> 

where *u*[0](*x*, *y*) represents the macroscopic displacement, and *u*[1](*x*, *y*) represents the microscopic fluctuation, scaled by a small parameter *ω*. This decomposition allows the analysis to account for both the global structure and the fine microstructural details.

As illustrated in [Figure 3.7](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0007), this method allows us to analyze the mechanics of the composite material. In order to satisfy the periodicity, it is required that the function have equal values at points *P*¹, *P*², *P*³, …, *P*^ω such that

(3.16)![StartFraction partial-differential upper P Superscript normal omega Baseline Over partial-differential x Subscript normal i Baseline EndFraction equals StartFraction partial-differential Over x Subscript normal i Baseline EndFraction upper P left-parenthesis x comma y right-parenthesis equals StartFraction partial-differential upper P Over partial-differential x Subscript normal i Baseline EndFraction plus StartFraction 1 Over omega EndFraction StartFraction partial-differential upper P Over partial-differential y Subscript normal i Baseline EndFraction](images/c03-math-0016.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <mfrac> <mrow> <mi>&#8706;</mi> <msup> <mi>P</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#969;</mi> </msup> </mrow> <mrow> <mi>&#8706;</mi> <msub> <mi>x</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">i</mi> </msub> </mrow> </mfrac> <mo>=</mo> <mfrac> <mi>&#8706;</mi> <msub> <mi>x</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">i</mi> </msub> </mfrac> <mi>P</mi> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow> <mi>x</mi> <mo>,</mo> <mi>y</mi> </mrow> </mfenced> <mo>=</mo> <mfrac> <mrow> <mi>&#8706;</mi> <mi>P</mi> </mrow> <mrow> <mi>&#8706;</mi> <msub> <mi>x</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">i</mi> </msub> </mrow> </mfrac> <mo>+</mo> <mfrac> <mn>1</mn> <mi>&#969;</mi> </mfrac> <mfrac> <mrow> <mi>&#8706;</mi> <mi>P</mi> </mrow> <mrow> <mi>&#8706;</mi> <msub> <mi>y</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">i</mi> </msub> </mrow> </mfrac> </math> 

Consider a physical property *ψ*(*x*, *y*) of a heterogeneous composite material. The property according to the homogenization theory has oscillation according to the entire macroscale. The oscillation can be expanded using the two‐scale expansion (as illustrated in [Figure 3.8](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0008)).

The evaluation of macroscale properties is performed under the assumption that the microstructural scale is infinitesimal [\[2\]](#c03.xhtml#c03-bib-0002). Due to strong coupling, the property *ψ*(*x*, *y*) can be expressed as an integral over the microstructure, accounting for its periodicity, as shown in Eq. [(3.17)](#c03.xhtml#c03-disp-0017).

[**Figure 3.8**](#c03.xhtml#R_c03-fig-0008) The oscillatory behavior of periodic boundary conditions.

Returning to the problem of the evaluation of elastic tensor, and from the result of Eq. [(3.16)](#c03.xhtml#c03-disp-0016), the zero‐order term *u*[0] in the expansion of *u* does not depend on the microstructure and can be interpreted as the average value of the displacement in the microstructure.

(3.18)![u 0 equals u 0 left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c03-math-0018.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <msub> <mi>u</mi> <mn>0</mn> </msub> <mo>=</mo> <msub> <mi>u</mi> <mn>0</mn> </msub> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mi>x</mi> </mfenced> </math> 

Equation [(3.17)](#c03.xhtml#c03-disp-0017) shows that the first‐order term *u*[1] is expressed as the product of the strain caused by the average displacement and the characteristic displacement in the microstructure. Therefore, χ^(*kl*) corresponds to an eigenfunction. Since χ^(*kl*) corresponds to direct strain in *k* and *l* directions, it shows how the microstructure deforms when uniform direct strain is applied to the unit cell model in the direction of normal stress when *k* = *l*. χ^(*kl*) shows the deformation of the microstructure when shear strain is applied to the unit cell model when *k* ≠ *l*.

Equation [(3.20)](#c03.xhtml#c03-disp-0020) provides the formula for calculating the characteristic displacement, which is a problem for the unit cell model. Generally, χ is determined by numerical methods such as element approximation of a unit cell model.

By solving Eqs. [(3.18)](#c03.xhtml#c03-disp-0018) and [(3.19)](#c03.xhtml#c03-disp-0019), it is also possible to determine the homogenized elastic tensor.

The derivation principle of the homogenization method is employed for the evaluation and design of porous structures, treating voids as materials with infinitesimal properties.

Furthermore, numerical methods used in CAE combined with the homogenization method enable the analysis of microstructures with complex topologies. To provide the reader with a practical understanding of the homogenization method, we will discuss its application with the finite element method for evaluating the mechanical and thermal properties in the following sections.

 

### 3.3.5 Approximation by Finite Element Method

Numerical homogenization seeks to derive macroscopic material properties from microscopic behaviors, thereby facilitating the modeling of porous structures on a practical scale. Central to this process is the use of the finite element method to solve the boundary value problems associated with representative volume elements (RVEs). This approach involves dividing the composite's microstructure into a mesh of finite elements, allowing for the detailed analysis of the material's response to unit test loads.

When using the homogenization method for porous structures, it is necessary to calculate the characteristic displacement in the unit cell model and the average displacement *u* in the homogenized model of the entire structure. This process requires two finite element analyses. If the porous material is not uniform, even in the homogenized model, multiple unit cell models are needed instead of just one.

Approximation using the finite element method on a unit cell model or a homogenized structural model demonstrates convergence, ensuring that the true solution is approached as the elements are further subdivided. Therefore, as long as an appropriate finite element model is used, a solution with high accuracy can be obtained.

Periodic boundary conditions are crucial for accurately representing the behavior of RVEs in numerical homogenization. These types of boundary conditions are employed to simulate an infinite lattice by replicating the unit cell's behavior. Essentially, periodic boundary conditions ensure that the displacements and tractions at the opposite faces of the RVE are identical, thereby mimicking the behavior of an extended ergodic microstructure that makes the material homogeneous.

While the primary application of numerical homogenization has traditionally been to determine the effective elastic tensor of composite materials, the scope of its applicability extends well beyond this. Homogenization techniques can be employed to calculate a variety of physical properties of composites, including thermal conductivity and piezoelectric properties. For instance, in the case of heat conduction, numerical homogenization can be used to derive the effective thermal conductivity tensor by solving heat conduction equations within the RVE and evaluating the resulting heat flux.

Similarly, when analyzing piezoelectric composites, numerical homogenization can be utilized to compute the effective piezoelectric tensor. By applying an electric field and solving the resulting electro‐mechanical problem within the RVE, the effective piezoelectric properties of the composite can be derived.

 

## 3.4 EFFECTIVE ELASTICITY TENSOR FOR POROUS STRUCTURES USING FEM FORMULATION

While conducting the investigation for the evaluation of the effective elastic tensor for two‐dimensional (2D) porous structures, let's start from the assumption that using the homogenization approach, Hooks law in tensor for 2D problems takes the tensor form, as shown in Eq. [(3.22)](#c03.xhtml#c03-disp-0022).

To calculate the effective elastic tensor of the RVE of a volume *V,* Eq. [(3.23)](#c03.xhtml#c03-disp-0024) is used:

where **E**^H is the homogenized elastic tensor, **E**[ijqp] is the elastic tensor of the ingredient materials that consist the RVE, and ![bold epsilon Subscript q p Superscript 0 left-parenthesis italic k l right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0001.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msubsup><mi mathvariant="bold">&#949;</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">qp</mi><mrow><mn>0</mn><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mi mathvariant="italic">kl</mi></mfenced></mrow></msubsup></math> denotes the unit strain test that is linearly independent (as shown in [Figure 3.9](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0009)). ![bold epsilon Subscript q p Superscript asterisk left-parenthesis italic k l right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0002.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msubsup><mi mathvariant="bold">&#949;</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">qp</mi><mrow><mo>&#42;</mo><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mi mathvariant="italic">kl</mi></mfenced></mrow></msubsup></math> represents the periodic characteristic strain, determined by solving Eq. [(3.24)](#c03.xhtml#c03-disp-0024)

where *∂γ*[n] is the arbitrary virtual displacement associated with the unit strain case. Equation [(3.23)](#c03.xhtml#c03-disp-0023) is solved for the three cases of *kl* = 11, 22, and 12, within Eq. [(3.24)](#c03.xhtml#c03-disp-0024).

![Figure 3.9 The three modes of mechanical deformation in a 2D RVE \[3\].](images/c03f009.png)

[**Figure 3.9**](#c03.xhtml#R_c03-fig-0009) The three modes of mechanical deformation in a 2D RVE [\[3\]](#c03.xhtml#c03-bib-0003).

The effective elastic tensor of the microstructure, **E**^H, calculated through the homogenization method as outlined in Eq. [(3.25)](#c03.xhtml#c03-disp-0025), is utilized to derive the elemental elastic tensor of the macroscale, **E**[macro], employing a material interpolation scheme analogous to that of the microstructure system \[[4](#c03.xhtml#c03-bib-0004)\].

### 3.4.1 Development of the Homogenization Approach for Determining the Effective Thermal Conductivity Tensor

Numerical asymptotic homogenization has been employed to determine the effective properties, specifically the homogenized conductivity tensor Λ^H of the microstructure. The microstructure is modeled as the RVE, designed to be statistically homogeneous relative to the macrostructure. The effective properties are influenced by the material distribution within the microstructure and the behavior of the material interface layer.

Here, the thermal conductivity is assumed to be uniformly distributed throughout the material. Additionally, the influence of the interface layer is disregarded, treating the RVE as a single‐phase solid material with voids. The methodology for calculating the thermal conductivity tensor of the material through homogenization will be elaborated in this section. Using the numerical homogenization approach, the effective properties of the microstructure are determined and subsequently utilized to define the finite element model of the macroscale domain, as illustrated in [Figure 3.10](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0010).

![Figure 3.10 A multi‐scale structure incorporating both macro‐ and microlevel design domains \[5\].](images/c03f010.png)

[**Figure 3.10**](#c03.xhtml#R_c03-fig-0010) A multi‐scale structure incorporating both macro‐ and microlevel design domains [\[5\]](#c03.xhtml#c03-bib-0005).

Considering the two‐phase microstructure, the overall thermal conductivity is Λ[*k*], where *k* = 1, 2 which denotes the material's thermal conductivity tensors associated with 2D RVE phases. The general steady‐state conduction heat flow is formulated by Eq. [(3.26)](#c03.xhtml#c03-disp-0026).

(3.26)![nabla period q left-parenthesis upper T right-parenthesis equals minus StartFraction partial-differential Over partial-differential zeta EndFraction left-parenthesis normal upper Lamda StartFraction partial-differential upper T Over partial-differential zeta EndFraction right-parenthesis comma for-all x element-of normal upper Omega](images/c03-math-0026.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <mi>&#8711;</mi> <mo>.</mo> <mi>q</mi> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mi>T</mi> </mfenced> <mo>=</mo> <mo form="prefix">&#8722;</mo> <mfrac> <mi>&#8706;</mi> <mrow> <mi>&#8706;</mi> <mi>&#950;</mi> </mrow> </mfrac> <mspace width="0.2em"/> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#923;</mi> <mfrac> <mrow> <mi>&#8706;</mi> <mi>T</mi> </mrow> <mrow> <mi>&#8706;</mi> <mi>&#950;</mi> </mrow> </mfrac> </mrow> </mfenced> <mo>,</mo> <mspace width="1em"/> <mo>&#8704;</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo>&#8712;</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#937;</mi> </math> 

where *q* is the heat transfer rate, and ![StartFraction partial-differential upper T Over partial-differential zeta EndFraction](images/c03-i0003.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfrac><mrow><mi>&#8706;</mi><mi>T</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>&#8706;</mi><mi>&#950;</mi></mrow></mfrac></math> is the temperature gradient which denotes the material's thermal conductivity constant, with the negative sign indicating the direction of heat flow from regions of higher to lower temperature, in accordance with the second law of thermodynamics. To model the material's physical behavior at the macrostructure level, a periodic and ergodic RVE is considered. The temperature field is then expanded using a Taylor series‐based asymptotic expansion. The temperature field which consists of the global temperature field and the RVE local variation of the temperature field is presented by Eq. [(3.28)](#c03.xhtml#c03-disp-0028).

The parameter Λ represents the thermal conductivity constant of the material, where the negative sign signifies heat flow from areas of higher to lower temperature, consistent with the second law of thermodynamics. To capture the material's physical behavior at the macrostructural level, a periodic and ergodic RVE is utilized. The temperature field is expressed using a Taylor series‐based asymptotic expansion. The overall temperature field, *T*, comprises the global temperature field and the local temperature variation within the RVE, *T*[*k*], as described in Eq. [(3.28)](#c03.xhtml#c03-disp-0028).

(3.27)![upper T left-parenthesis zeta comma xi right-parenthesis equals epsilon 0 ModifyingAbove upper T With bar left-parenthesis zeta right-parenthesis plus sigma-summation Underscript k equals 1 Overscript infinity Endscripts epsilon Subscript k Baseline upper T Subscript k Baseline left-parenthesis zeta comma xi right-parenthesis](images/c03-math-0027.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <mi>T</mi> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow> <mi>&#950;</mi> <mo>,</mo> <mi>&#958;</mi> </mrow> </mfenced> <mo>=</mo> <msub> <mi>&#949;</mi> <mn>0</mn> </msub> <mover accent="true"><mi>T</mi> <mo stretchy="true">&#175;</mo> </mover> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mi>&#950;</mi> </mfenced> <mo>+</mo> <munderover> <mo movablelimits="false">&#8721;</mo> <mrow> <mi>k</mi> <mo>=</mo> <mn>1</mn> </mrow> <mo>&#8734;</mo> </munderover> <msub> <mi>&#949;</mi> <mi>k</mi> </msub> <msub> <mi>T</mi> <mi>k</mi> </msub> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow> <mi>&#950;</mi> <mo>,</mo> <mi>&#958;</mi> </mrow> </mfenced> </math> 

where *ε* represents the characteristic length scale (*ε* = *l*/*L* ≪ 1), which is downscale from macro‐ to microscale, *ξ* = *ζ*/*ε* (as shown in [Figure 3.10](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0010)). The temperature field expansion could be minimized to first order (*k* = 1) such that, The RVE consists of two distinct phases, of which one possesses an infinitesimally small conductivity tensor. Based on the asymptotic expansion of the temperature field, the general form of steady‐state heat transfer is expressed as

As such, *T*[1] will be

Due to the periodicity of the microscale boundary condition and considering *ε* as infinitesimal value, the thermal conductivity constant of the macrostructure can be approximated as the integrated average value of ![normal upper Lamda left-parenthesis 1 minus StartFraction partial-differential chi Over partial-differential xi EndFraction right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0005.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">&#923;</mi><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>&#8722;</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>&#8706;</mi><mi>&#967;</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>&#8706;</mi><mi>&#958;</mi></mrow></mfrac></mrow></mfenced></math> over the periodic microscale domain. This thermal conductivity is referred to as the homogenized thermal conductivity constant **Λ**^H, and it takes the final form of Eq. [(3.34)](#c03.xhtml#c03-disp-0034)

(3.34)![bold upper Lamda Superscript normal upper H Baseline equals StartFraction 1 Over StartAbsoluteValue normal upper Omega Subscript normal m Baseline EndAbsoluteValue EndFraction integral Underscript normal upper Omega Subscript normal m Baseline Endscripts bold upper Lamda left-parenthesis bold upper I minus StartFraction partial-differential chi Over partial-differential xi EndFraction right-parenthesis d normal upper Omega Subscript normal m Baseline](images/c03-math-0034.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <msup> <mi mathvariant="bold">&#923;</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi> </msup> <mo>=</mo> <mfrac> <mn>1</mn> <mfenced open="|" close="|" separators=""><msub> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#937;</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi> </msub> </mfenced> </mfrac> <munder> <mo>&#8747;</mo> <msub> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#937;</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi> </msub> </munder> <mi mathvariant="bold">&#923;</mi> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow> <mi mathvariant="bold">I</mi> <mo>&#8722;</mo> <mfrac> <mrow> <mi>&#8706;</mi> <mi>&#967;</mi> </mrow> <mrow> <mi>&#8706;</mi> <mi>&#958;</mi> </mrow> </mfrac> </mrow> </mfenced> <mspace width="0.5em"/> <mi>d</mi> <msub> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#937;</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi> </msub> </math> 

where |Ω[m]| is the Lebesgue space representation of the microscale domain Ω[m], which is interpolated as the spatial configuration of the design problem (area in the 2D case and volume in the three‐dimensional case). ***I*** represents the identity matrix. The representation of the homogenized thermal properties of the microstructure for the macroscale is illustrated in [Figure 3.11](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0011).

Here ![upper G equals StartFraction 1 Over StartAbsoluteValue normal upper Omega Subscript normal m Baseline EndAbsoluteValue EndFraction integral Underscript normal upper Omega Subscript normal m Baseline Endscripts nabla upper T d normal upper Omega Subscript normal m Baseline](images/c03-i0006.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>G</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mfenced open="|" close="|" separators=""><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">&#937;</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi></msub></mfenced></mfrac><munder><mo>&#8747;</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">&#937;</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi></msub></munder><mi>&#8711;</mi><mi>T</mi><mspace width="0.2em"/><mi>d</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">&#937;</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi></msub></math> represents the microscopic temperature gradient. Calculating the material's thermal conductivity tensor, **G** is imposed on one side of the opposite boundaries such that it consists of the Kronecker delta function of the unity matrix multiplied by the unitary test temperature gradient value.

The periodic boundary conditions are implemented by imposing **G** as a field on the opposite sides of the microscopic domain and then calculating the temperature vector for each case of **G**. The temperature on the boundaries is prescribed as

Here, for Eq. [(3.35),](#c03.xhtml#c03-disp-0035) the representation of *G* in the two‐dimensional case is shown in [Figure 3.12](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0012).

Then, homogenized material's thermal conductivity tensor takes the form of

(3.37)![bold upper Lamda Superscript normal upper H Baseline equals StartFraction 1 Over StartAbsoluteValue normal upper Omega Subscript normal m Baseline EndAbsoluteValue EndFraction sigma-summation Underscript e equals 1 Overscript upper N Subscript normal m Baseline Endscripts integral Underscript normal upper Omega Subscript normal m Baseline Endscripts left-parenthesis bold upper I minus bold upper D upper T right-parenthesis Superscript upper T Baseline bold upper Lamda left-parenthesis bold upper I minus bold upper D upper T right-parenthesis d normal upper Omega Subscript normal m Baseline](images/c03-math-0037.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <msup> <mi mathvariant="bold">&#923;</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi> </msup> <mo>=</mo> <mfrac> <mn>1</mn> <mfenced open="|" close="|" separators=""><msub> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#937;</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi> </msub> </mfenced> </mfrac> <munderover> <mo movablelimits="false">&#8721;</mo> <mrow> <mi>e</mi> <mo>=</mo> <mn>1</mn> </mrow> <msub> <mi>N</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi> </msub> </munderover> <munder> <mo>&#8747;</mo> <msub> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#937;</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi> </msub> </munder> <msup> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow> <mi mathvariant="bold">I</mi> <mo>&#8722;</mo> <mi mathvariant="bold">DT</mi> </mrow> </mfenced> <mi>T</mi> </msup> <mi mathvariant="bold">&#923;</mi> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow> <mi mathvariant="bold">I</mi> <mo>&#8722;</mo> <mi mathvariant="bold">DT</mi> </mrow> </mfenced> <mspace width="0.5em"/> <mi>d</mi> <msub> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#937;</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi> </msub> </math> 

In its finite element form, Eq. [(3.37)](#c03.xhtml#c03-disp-0037) serves as the fundamental equation for conducting concurrent topology optimization at the microscale. It will be employed in inverse homogenization methods to design the microscale structure while simultaneously being used to compute the homogenized heat conductivity of the optimized microstructure, which is then integrated into the macrostructure during each iteration of the optimization process.

# Chapter 4  
Advanced Techniques in Porous Structure Design

 

## 4.1 INTRODUCTION

Nowadays, engineering optimization represents a paradigm shift in engineering design, emerging as a simulation‐driven methodology that has significantly advanced the field. This technique serves as a potent instrument for discerning, evaluating, and refining potential design configurations while efficiently eliminating less viable options during the nascent phases of product development. And with the advancement of computational power, numerical methods and computer‐aided manufacturing optimization have thus become indispensable for manufacturers striving to enhance product designs, generate lightweight and manufacturable solutions, and achieve innovation through cost‐effective strategies. The foundation of structural optimization lies in the principles of mathematical optimization and computational simulations, enabling engineers and designers to exploit mathematical rigor and physical laws to optimize structures and materials with unparalleled precision and efficiency.

The transformative power of structural optimization is particularly evident in its ability to accelerate the design process. Historically, the development of new products was a time‐intensive and iterative endeavor, characterized by the construction, testing, and subsequent modification of prototypes until a satisfactory design was realized. This conventional approach not only consumed substantial time and resources but also generated considerable material waste. In contrast, engineering optimization offers a more streamlined and efficient alternative. By leveraging advanced computer‐aided design (CAD) software and simulation tools, engineers can accurately model the structural behavior of a product under various loading conditions and constraints, thereby efficiently replicating real‐world scenarios within a virtual environment.

The process of numerical optimization is a search algorithm by nature, with optimization algorithms playing a central role in refining the design. These algorithms adjust the design parameters, systematically exploring the design space to identify configurations that meet predefined performance criteria. For instance, a common objective in structural optimization is to minimize the weight of a component while ensuring that it maintains sufficient strength to withstand operational conditions. Other objectives might include reducing material usage, enhancing durability, or improving manufacturability.

In addition to its efficiency, engineering optimization is also highly adaptable and can incorporate multidisciplinary problems, making it applicable across a broad spectrum of industries and design challenges. Whether in aerospace, automotive, civil engineering, or consumer products, optimization can be tailored to meet the specific requirements of each application. For example, in the aerospace industry, where weight reduction is a critical concern, optimization is used extensively to design lightweight components that meet stringent performance and safety standards. Similarly, in the automotive industry, optimization helps engineers design parts that are both lightweight and durable, contributing to improved fuel efficiency and vehicle performance. In civil engineering, structural optimization is applied to the design of bridges, buildings, and other infrastructure projects, ensuring that these structures are both safe and cost‐effective.

The principles of computational simulations that underpin structural optimization are grounded in the mathematical modeling of physical phenomena.

The analysis of physical phenomena often involves discretization methods, where the domain of the phenomenon is divided into smaller, semi‐regular representations. These smaller sections can be solved simultaneously or cluster by cluster, depending on the chosen numerical method, the available computational power, and the specific physics of the problem. Common discretization methods include finite difference methods, finite element methods, boundary element methods, and finite volume methods.

Additionally, there have been attempts to utilize mesh‐free methods, although these approaches face significant challenges. Machine learning has also entered the realm of simulation and analysis due to its mathematical similarities with traditional discretization methods. Neural networks, for instance, share a structural resemblance to numerical methods for solving partial differential equations (PDEs) and have been adapted to tackle such problems effectively.

It is important to note that many of the currently used topological discretization techniques trace their origins back to mathematical discretization methods for physical and mathematical problems, such as series expansions. These foundational methods have shaped the development of modern approaches to modeling and solving complex phenomena.

In this book, the method used to simulate and perform optimization is the finite element method. Finite element analysis (FEA) stands as one of the main cornerstones for numerical techniques in engineering, enabling the detailed examination of complex structures by discretizing them into a mesh of smaller, interconnected elements. Each of these elements serves as a localized domain within which the mathematical equations governing physical phenomena—such as deformation, stress, and strain—are solved. Through this process, FEA can successfully provide a comprehensive simulation of how an entire structure responds to various external fields and boundary conditions, offering engineers a profound understanding of structural behavior under a myriad of scenarios.

The optimization process commences with valid methodology using a finite element model representing the product or structure under consideration. This model is a detailed digital representation encapsulating all critical geometric features and material properties that define the design's essence. The next step involves the articulation of the objective function, a mathematical expression that quantifies the design goals, such as minimizing mass, maximizing stiffness, or enhancing durability. In parallel, engineers define constraints to ensure that the optimization adheres to essential requirements, which may include safety factors, material limits, or manufacturability constraints.

Once the finite element model, objective function, and constraints are established, the optimization process unfolds through an iterative cycle. During this phase, various design parameters—such as dimensions, material distributions, or geometric configurations—are systematically adjusted. Following each adjustment, the finite element model undergoes reanalysis to ascertain the impact of these changes on the overall performance metrics. Optimization algorithms, functioning in tandem with the finite element solver, diligently search for the optimal set of design variables that fulfill the specified objectives while satisfying all imposed constraints. This iterative refinement continues until an optimal design configuration is achieved, one that offers the best possible balance between competing criteria.

The choice of optimization algorithms is diverse, with each type tailored to address different classes of design problems. Among the most commonly employed algorithms are gradient‐based methods, metaheuristic algorithms \[[1](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0001), [2](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0002)\], and biologically inspired methods [\[3\]](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0003). Gradient‐based methods are well‐suited for optimization problems characterized by smooth, continuous design spaces. These algorithms leverage the gradients of the objective function with respect to the design variables, enabling efficient navigation through the design space toward an optimal solution. However, they are most effective in scenarios where the design space is well‐behaved and lacks significant discontinuities or local extrema.

Metaheuristic algorithms somehow are more adept at tackling complex design spaces that may be discrete, highly non‐linear, or riddled with local optima, for example. Genetic algorithms operate on a population of potential solutions, iteratively evolving them through processes analogous to mutation, crossover, and selection. This stochastic approach enables genetic algorithms to explore a wider design space, making them especially useful for problems with discrete design variables or complex, rugged solution landscapes. However, most metaheuristic algorithms require careful tuning of parameters to match the mathematical function being optimized. Additionally, these algorithms can be computationally expensive, which may limit their efficiency in certain applications.

The integration of these optimization algorithms with FEA enables engineers to address a wide array of design challenges with precision and efficiency.

Moreover, the synergy between FEA and optimization extends beyond traditional physical problems to encompass advanced materials and innovative manufacturing techniques. In the realm of additive manufacturing, for example, optimization algorithms are used to design lattice structures that are both lightweight and strong, tailored to the unique capabilities of three‐dimensional (3D) printing technologies.

Engineering optimization proves particularly beneficial in addressing multi‐objective problems, where design criteria often conflict. For instance, in the aerospace sector, engineers may seek to reduce both the weight and drag of an aircraft to boost fuel efficiency. However, these objectives can be at odds with one another, necessitating a careful balance. Structural optimization excels in navigating these complex trade‐offs, enabling the identification of Pareto‐optimal solutions—designs that represent the best possible compromises between competing objectives. This capability to explore and balance multiple design possibilities is a significant advantage, allowing engineers to make informed, strategic decisions about the final design configuration.

Beyond its application in achieving lightweight structures, structural optimization also plays a crucial role in the manufacturing process. While lightweight designs are often advantageous, they can also present manufacturing challenges due to their intricate geometries and the need for specialized materials and techniques. Engineering optimization bridges the gap between design and manufacturing by ensuring that optimized designs are not only efficient and lightweight but also manufacturable. Engineers can integrate manufacturing constraints—such as material thickness limitations, moldability considerations, or specific assembly methods—directly into the optimization process. By doing so, they mitigate the risk of generating designs that, while theoretically optimal, might be impractical or prohibitively expensive to produce.

Optimization also greatly enhances the practice of virtual prototyping, which has become an integral part of modern product development. Traditionally, product development involved creating physical prototypes for testing and validation—a process that could be both time‐consuming and costly. Optimization, however, allows for the creation of virtual prototypes that can be subjected to a comprehensive suite of simulated tests, including structural, thermal, and fluid dynamics analyses. Optimization‐guided virtual prototyping provides a detailed understanding of the design's performance across a range of scenarios, enabling engineers to make informed adjustments well before any physical prototype is built. This approach not only conserves resources but also significantly accelerates the product development cycle, allowing for faster time to market.

As we explore further, we encounter both parametric and non‐parametric approaches within the realm of engineering optimization, especially structural optimization. Parametric optimization relies on specific design parameters and mathematical models to refine a given design, allowing for control over the optimization process and attaining the design according to that parameter. In contrast, non‐parametric optimization employs more generalized methods that can adapt to a wider range of design variables such that all the discretized elements in the design domain are addressed in optimization. Each of these approaches offers unique advantages and is suited to different types of optimization problems. In the following sections, these methodologies are discussed.

 

## 4.2 PARAMETRIC OPTIMIZATION

Parametric optimization is a mathematical approach to optimizing a system or structure by varying one or more parameters within a predefined range to achieve the best performance according to specific objectives. In engineering, this technique is widely applied to enhance the efficiency, strength, or functionality of designs. Parameters may include geometric dimensions, material properties, boundary conditions, or loading configurations. For instance, in structural optimization, parameters such as beam thickness, cross‐sectional area, or material density can be adjusted to minimize weight while maximizing mechanical strength or stiffness. The optimization problem is typically defined by an objective function, such as minimizing stress or maximizing thermal conductivity, subject to constraints like allowable deformation or manufacturing limitations. By systematically analyzing how changes in parameters affect performance, engineers can identify optimal configurations that balance competing requirements, such as cost, durability, and functionality.

Parametric optimization involves finding the optimal values of parameters **p** = {*p*[1], *p*[2],… , *p*[*n*]} that minimize or maximize an objective function *f*(**p**), subjected to the constraints of *g*[*i*](**p**) ≤ 0 and *h*[*i*](**p**) = 0. Mathematically, this can be expressed as

(4.1)![StartLayout 1st Row 1st Column extrema Underscript bold p Endscripts f left-parenthesis bold p right-parenthesis 2nd Column Blank 2nd Row 1st Column subjected to colon 2nd Column g Subscript i Baseline left-parenthesis bold p right-parenthesis less-than-or-equal-to 0 comma i equals 1 comma ellipsis comma m 3rd Row 1st Column Blank 2nd Column h Subscript j Baseline left-parenthesis bold p right-parenthesis equals 0 comma j equals 1 comma ellipsis comma k EndLayout](images/c04-math-0001.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <mtable columnalign="left" displaystyle="true"><mtr> <mtd> <munder> <mtext>extrema</mtext> <mi mathvariant="bold">p</mi> </munder> <mspace width="0.2em"/> <mi>f</mi> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mi mathvariant="bold">p</mi> </mfenced> </mtd> <mtd></mtd> </mtr> <mtr> <mtd> <mtext>subjected&#8201;to:</mtext> <mspace width="0.2em"/> </mtd> <mtd> <mspace width="0.5em"/> <msub> <mi>g</mi> <mi>i</mi> </msub> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mi mathvariant="bold">p</mi> </mfenced> <mo>&#8804;</mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo>,</mo> <mspace width="0.6em"/> <mi>i</mi> <mo>=</mo> <mn>1</mn> <mo>,</mo> <mo>&#8230;</mo> <mspace width="0.19em"/> <mo>,</mo> <mi>m</mi> </mtd> </mtr> <mtr> <mtd></mtd> <mtd> <mspace width="0.5em"/> <msub> <mi>h</mi> <mi>j</mi> </msub> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mi mathvariant="bold">p</mi> </mfenced> <mo>=</mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo>,</mo> <mspace width="0.6em"/> <mi>j</mi> <mo>=</mo> <mn>1</mn> <mo>,</mo> <mo>&#8230;</mo> <mspace width="0.19em"/> <mo>,</mo> <mi>k</mi> </mtd> </mtr> </mtable> </math> 

For example, consider the parametric optimization problem of a cantilever beam. The process typically begins by selecting the initial shape, and then applying optimization to adjust a parameter within the shape. In the case of a rectangular cross‐section cantilever beam, consider a beam subjected to a force *F* at its free end. The objective is to minimize the deflection *δ*, which depends on the beam's thickness *t*. The deflection is given by

(4.2)![delta equals StartFraction italic upper F upper L Over 3 italic upper E upper I EndFraction](images/c04-math-0002.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <mi>&#948;</mi> <mo>=</mo> <mfrac> <mi mathvariant="italic">FL</mi> <mrow> <mn>3</mn> <mi mathvariant="italic">EI</mi> </mrow> </mfrac> </math> 

where *L* is the beam length, and *E* is Young's modulus. *I* is the 2^(nd) moment of area (i.e., the moment of inertia), and it takes the form of

(4.3)![upper I equals StartFraction italic w t cubed Over 12 EndFraction](images/c04-math-0003.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <mi>I</mi> <mo>=</mo> <mfrac> <msup> <mi mathvariant="italic">wt</mi> <mn>3</mn> </msup> <mn>12</mn> </mfrac> </math> 

where *w* is the width of the beam. By varying the thickness *t*, the optimal value can be identified to minimize deflection while adhering to manufacturing or material constraints. This variation in *t* represents a systematic exploration of how deflection behaves as a function of thickness, ultimately allowing us to determine the most effective value. For this example, consider a cantilever beam with a square cross‐section, a length of 10 m, a width of 0.1 m, a Young's modulus of 2 × 10⁹ Pa, and a force of 1 Newton applied at its free end. By varying the thickness from 0.01 to 0.1 m and plotting the resulting deflection, we can observe the behavior of the deflection function. The results of the deflection versus the thickness are depicted in [Figure 4.1](#c04.xhtml#c04-fig-0001).

To replicate this analysis, the reader can execute the accompanying Python code, which generates the results as illustrated in [Figure 4.1](#c04.xhtml#c04-fig-0001). This practical implementation provides insights into the interplay between structural geometry and deflection, a fundamental concept in engineering design.

![](images/c04-gra-0001.png)

The deflection function behavior in [Figure 4.1](#c04.xhtml#c04-fig-0001) shows convexity in the thickness belonging to the interval \[0.01, 0.1\]. This convexity indicates that the optimization process, aimed at minimizing deflection, will achieve a unique global minimum within this specified bound. The best result is attained at a thickness of 0.1 m. Given the convexity of the deflection function within the specified bounds, it is feasible to directly implement optimization techniques to achieve optimal results, bypassing the need for repetitive evaluations and extensive plotting of the entire function. One effective method for this optimization is the Lagrangian approach, which incorporates constraints into the objective function \[[4](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0004), [5](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0005)\]. By introducing Lagrange multipliers, the optimization problem can be reformulated to find the stationary points of the Lagrangian, thus ensuring that the global minimum is attained while satisfying any imposed constraints. This approach significantly enhances computational efficiency and precision in the optimization process. Using this Python code, we implement the optimization, and the results are presented in [Figure 4.2](#c04.xhtml#c04-fig-0002).

![Figure 4.1 The cantilever beam's thickness versus deflection under constant load.](images/c04f001.png)

[**Figure 4.1**](#c04.xhtml#R_c04-fig-0001) The cantilever beam's thickness versus deflection under constant load.

![Figure 4.2 Optimizing cantilever beam deflection: finding the ideal thickness for minimal deformation.](images/c04f002.png)
