## 4.3 NON‐PARAMETRIC OPTIMIZATION

Non‐parametric optimization represents an indispensable dimension of contemporary structural and multiphysics design, driving the evolution of optimal configurations that meet stringent performance criteria while effectively navigating the multifaceted challenges posed by real‐world constraints. Diverging from traditional parametric methodologies, non‐parametric optimization focuses on the intricate manipulation of structural geometry and topology by utilizing the discretization methods. This paradigm shift is exemplified by two principal methodologies: shape optimization and topology optimization. Shape optimization centers on the refinement of a structure's boundaries to satisfy the design problem by updating the element on the boundary shape while maintaining the internal material distribution.

On the other hand, topology optimization addresses the whole elements in the design domain. The transformative potential of non‐parametric optimization lies in its departure from explicit parameterization. Unlike traditional methods, which rely on predefined design parameters, non‐parametric optimization harnesses the intrinsic geometry and topology of the structure as the primary drivers of the optimization process. This approach imbues the resulting designs with fluidity and adaptability that resonates with the complexity of real‐world scenarios, often yielding solutions that surpass the capabilities of parametric counterparts.

To facilitate this optimization, the design domain is discretized using numerical methods such as the finite element method. Discretization converts the continuous structure into a discrete form, comprising a multitude of finite elements, each associated with a design variable. These design variables represent the material density or presence within each element, effectively determining whether the material should be retained, reduced, or eliminated in a particular region. The iterative nature of the topology optimization process involves updating these design variables at each iteration step, guided by the objective function and constraints.

A key aspect of topology optimization is its ability to address complex engineering challenges, such as maximizing structural stiffness, minimizing weight, or optimizing thermal conductivity. The objective function is defined based on the specific performance criterion of interest, and the optimization algorithm seeks to find the optimal material distribution that extremizes this objective while adhering to constraints such as maximum allowable stress, displacement, or volume fraction.

For instance, in a heat maximization problem, the objective function might be to maximize the thermal conductivity of a structure, subject to constraints on material usage and geometric boundaries. The design domain is discretized, and the topology optimization algorithm iteratively adjusts the material distribution to identify the optimal configuration that achieves the desired thermal performance. The resulting design may exhibit a complex and non‐intuitive topology, reflecting the intricate interplay between thermal conductivity and material distribution (as illustrated in [Figure 4.3](#c04.xhtml#c04-fig-0003)).

The power of topology optimization lies in its ability to generate complex, innovative, and efficient designs that are tailored to specific engineering challenges. By allowing the material to be freely redistributed within the design domain, topology optimization can uncover novel structural configurations that would be difficult, if not impossible, to conceive using traditional design methods. These designs often feature intricate internal geometries, optimized load paths, and minimized stress concentrations, all of which contribute to enhanced performance and reduced material usage.

Moreover, the integration of non‐parametric optimization techniques into the design process offers significant advantages in terms of flexibility and adaptability. Unlike parametric methods, which are constrained by predefined design variables and relationships, non‐parametric optimization allows for a more organic and unconstrained exploration of the design space. This flexibility is particularly valuable in scenarios where the design objectives and constraints are complex or where the optimal solution is not readily apparent.

![Figure 4.3 The topology optimization designing process for the heat maximization problem \[6\] / with permission from Elsevier.](images/c04f003.png)

[**Figure 4.3**](#c04.xhtml#R_c04-fig-0003) The topology optimization designing process for the heat maximization problem [\[6\]](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0006) / with permission from Elsevier.

Historically, topology optimization originated as a conceptual framework introduced by J. Maxwell [\[7\]](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0007), who laid the foundational ideas of discretizing the structure as domains to small segments using the energy principle. This discretization method may not be considered new, as this era has witnessed significant advancements in the use of series to solve engineering problems. However, the pioneering work of Maxwell opened the door to variational principles, which laid the foundation for the development of widely used computational methods such as the finite element and finite volume methods. Furthermore, the discretization approach, later known as Maxwell's lemma, intrigued both mathematicians and engineers, prompting its application in optimizing complex structures that would otherwise take an extensive amount of time to solve using series. It was not until the groundbreaking contributions of A.G.M. Michell [\[8\]](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0008) that the field experienced a significant advancement. Michell's seminal work established a practical methodology for applying mathematical optimization principles to the design of structural architectures, effectively bridging the gap between theoretical concepts and practical engineering applications. His approach provided a systematic and quantifiable means of determining the most efficient structural layouts, minimizing material usage while maximizing performance. Michell's innovative techniques catalyzed further research, leading to the evolution and refinement of topology optimization methods. These advancements have since been integrated into a wide spectrum of engineering and design disciplines, yielding highly efficient and optimized structural systems. The foundational work of Michell has inspired a myriad of subsequent studies, each contributing to the expansion and enhancement of topology optimization, solidifying its role as a critical tool in modern engineering design \[[9](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0009)–[12](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0012)\],

The advent of modern topology optimization was fundamentally shaped by the integration of homogenization techniques, which facilitated the confluence of advanced mathematical concepts and contemporary computational methods. This integration was exemplified by the development of the discretized continuous optimality criterion method [\[13\]](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0013), which subsequently laid the groundwork for the solid isotropic material with penalization (SIMP) approach. The SIMP method has been notably applied to the maximization of thermal performance, as demonstrated in the research conducted by Joo et al. [\[14\]](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0014). Their investigation focused on optimizing heat sink designs using the SIMP approach, addressing the challenge of local minima by incorporating a surrogate model to enhance the optimization efficacy.

In contrast, Koga et al. [\[15\]](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0015) employed an unconventional approach to heat sink designs by focusing on the internal channel configuration within a metallic block. Their methodology utilized a steady‐state flow objective function and incorporated Stokes flow principles to optimize the internal structure, thus providing an innovative alternative to traditional heat sink design approaches.

Their study, employing the SIMP method, considered both steady‐state conduction and convective heat transfer from side surfaces, thereby presenting a holistic approach to thermal management. This body of research underscores the ongoing refinement and application of topology optimization techniques in developing highly efficient and sophisticated thermal management solutions.

 

## 4.4 MULTIPHYSICS TOPOLOGY OPTIMIZATION FUNDAMENTALS

Topology optimization shares conceptual underpinnings with traditional parametric optimization methodologies. Optimization, in essence, involves identifying extrema within a mathematically formulated problem, which encompasses variables, constraints, and an objective function. Variables determine the structure of the objective function, the choice of optimization techniques, and any constraints applied. Constraints, when incorporated into the optimization framework, delineate the feasible solution space, guiding the optimization process toward feasible solutions. The objective function, assuming that it exhibits a smooth behavior over the design space, is subjected to the extremum search process.

In optimization, the goal is to explore the design space defined by the objective function using various search algorithms to locate the extrema of the function concerning the design variables. These design variables are integral to shaping the objective function and thus directly influence the optimization outcome.

In contrast, non‐parametric optimization methods, such as topology optimization, employ discretized representations that mitigate the impact of changes in the objective function on the design's topological flexibility. This is due to the implicit nature of how topology is represented within the objective function, allowing for a broader range of design alterations without significantly constraining the topological freedom of the solution space.

Here, two separate design variables are used to characterize the macro‐ (x[M]) and microscales (**x**[m]). The extrema of the objective function are considered global when the design variables satisfy the following condition:

Greater convexity typically increases the likelihood of identifying global extrema. Optimality smoothness is critical for achieving effective sensitivity analysis and robust solutions, which can be realized by approximating the problem within a smooth mathematical framework. Such an approximation is widely employed in modeling engineering challenges. Securing a global extremum presents significant difficulties, as the function's trajectory is often constrained by local extrema (as illustrated in [Figure 4.4](#c04.xhtml#c04-fig-0004) for the case of multiple minima). For instance, in minimization problems, the goal is to locate the global minimum, as illustrated in [Figure 4.5](#c04.xhtml#c04-fig-0005), but the presence of strong local minima increases the likelihood of converging to a local minimum. To address this, the random multi‐start method which is a practical approximation technique is used to explore different regions of the solution space, enhancing the probability of finding the global minimum. Consequently, the minimum value is typically obtained after “sufficient trials,” with the number of trials being proportional to the convexity of the function.

![Figure 4.5 Mathematical optimization with multiple minima.](images/c04f005.png)

Here, **H**(**x**(Δ)) is widely known as the Hessian matrix. If the Hessian matrix is positive definite at all points within the selected test set and the gradient of the function is zero, the point in question is identified as a local minimum of a strong nature. The Hessian matrix is instrumental in identifying extrema; however, its use can significantly affect computational efficiency due to the increased volume of calculations required. Consequently, this study avoids employing second‐order derivatives to mitigate computational costs. To find the extremum of a function, direct search algorithms are commonly utilized, commencing from an initial point characterized by estimated strong convexity. The general criterion for optimality in extremum problems can be expressed as follows:

close="&#8214;" separators=""><mrow> <msup> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mi>i</mi> </msup> <mo>&#8722;</mo> <msup> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mrow> <mi>i</mi> <mo>&#8722;</mo> <mn>1</mn> </mrow> </msup> </mrow> </mfenced> <mspace width="2.2em"/> <mo>&#60;</mo> <msub> <mi>&#949;</mi> <mn>1</mn> </msub> </mtd> </mtr> <mtr> <mtd></mtd> <mtd columnalign="left"><mfenced open="&#8214;" close="&#8214;" separators=""><mrow> <mi>&#8711;</mi> <mi>f</mi> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><msup> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mi>i</mi> </msup> </mfenced> </mrow> </mfenced> <mspace width="3.1em"/> <mo>&#60;</mo> <msub> <mi>&#949;</mi> <mn>2</mn> </msub> </mtd> </mtr> <mtr> <mtd></mtd> <mtd> <mfenced open="&#8214;" close="&#8214;" separators=""><mrow> <mi>f</mi> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><msup> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mi>i</mi> </msup> </mfenced> <mo>&#8722;</mo> <mi>f</mi> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><msup> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mrow> <mi>i</mi> <mo>&#8722;</mo> <mn>1</mn> </mrow> </msup> </mfenced> </mrow> </mfenced> <mo>&#60;</mo> <msub> <mi>&#949;</mi> <mn>3</mn> </msub> </mtd> </mtr> </mtable> </math> 

where ε[1 → 3] are larger than zero.

To ensure robustness, constraints are typically employed to delineate the search domain. These constraints, whether defined by equality or inequality conditions, are crucial for restricting the design to a “feasible search space.” Search algorithms initiate from a designated starting point, progressing with a controlled step size (*ε*), as illustrated in [Figure 4.5](#c04.xhtml#c04-fig-0005).

Optimizing a function under specific constraints necessitates achieving what is termed as the feasibility of the design problem. Constraints on the extremum can be effectively managed by reformulating the problem into an unconstrained optimization framework through the application of penalty functions. This approach allows for the transformation of constrained optimization into a more manageable form by penalizing deviations from the constraints within the objective function.

(4.13)![StartLayout 1st Row 1st Column Extremum 2nd Column f left-parenthesis bold x right-parenthesis 2nd Row 1st Column normal s period normal t period 2nd Column g left-parenthesis bold x right-parenthesis EndLayout right-brace right-arrow Extremum upper P left-parenthesis bold x right-parenthesis equals f left-parenthesis bold x right-parenthesis plus sigma-summation beta g squared left-parenthesis bold x right-parenthesis](images/c04-math-0013.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <mfenced open="" close="}" separators=""><mtable> <mtr> <mtd> <mtext>Extremum</mtext> <mspace width="0.06em"/> </mtd> <mtd> <mi>f</mi> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> </mfenced> </mtd> </mtr> <mtr> <mtd> <mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi> <mo>.</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">t</mi> <mo>.</mo> <mspace width="0.01em"/> </mtd> <mtd> <mi>g</mi> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> </mfenced> </mtd> </mtr> </mtable> </mfenced> <mo>&#8594;</mo> <mtext>Extremum</mtext> <mspace width="0.2em"/> <mi>P</mi> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> </mfenced> <mo>=</mo> <mi>f</mi> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> </mfenced> <mo form="postfix">+</mo> <mo>&#8721;</mo> <mi>&#946;</mi> <msup> <mi>g</mi> <mn>2</mn> </msup> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> </mfenced> </math> 

The penalty parameter, denoted as (*β*), plays a critical role in the optimization process. Selecting appropriate penalty parameters is essential for the problem's formulation. Additionally, the Lagrangian function is an effective approach for addressing constraint problems, achieved by introducing Lagrange multipliers, denoted as (ℓ).

(4.14)![StartLayout 1st Row Extremum f left-parenthesis bold x right-parenthesis 2nd Row normal s period normal t period Cond equals StartLayout Enlarged left-brace 1st Row g left-parenthesis bold x right-parenthesis equals 0 2nd Row h left-parenthesis bold x right-parenthesis equals 0 EndLayout EndLayout right-brace right-arrow Extremum upper P left-parenthesis bold x comma normal script l right-parenthesis equals f left-parenthesis bold x right-parenthesis plus sigma-summation normal script l Cond](images/c04-math-0014.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <mfenced open="" close="}" separators=""><mtable> <mtr> <mtd> <mtext>Extremum</mtext> <mspace width="1.16em"/> <mi>f</mi> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> </mfenced> </mtd> </mtr> <mtr> <mtd> <mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi> <mo>.</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">t</mi> <mo>.</mo> <mspace width="0.48em"/> <mtext>Cond</mtext> <mo>=</mo> <mfenced open="{" close="" separators=""><mtable> <mtr> <mtd> <mi>g</mi> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> </mfenced> <mo>=</mo> <mn>0</mn> </mtd> </mtr> <mtr> <mtd> <mi>h</mi> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> </mfenced> <mo>=</mo> <mn>0</mn> </mtd> </mtr> </mtable> </mfenced> </mtd> </mtr> </mtable> </mfenced> <mo>&#8594;</mo> <mtext>Extremum</mtext> <mspace width="0.2em"/> <mi>P</mi> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mo>,</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#8467;</mi> </mrow> </mfenced> <mo>=</mo> <mi>f</mi> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> </mfenced> <mo form="postfix">+</mo> <mo>&#8721;</mo> <mspace width="0.2em"/> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#8467;</mi> <mtext>Cond</mtext> </math> 

The constraint type plays an important role in solving the optimization problem. In general, the constraints are classified as: equality constraints (*g*(**x**) = 0) and non‐equality constraints (*g*(**x**) *<* 0). In this work, the Lagrange method with equality constraints is adopted as the main method of updating the design variables. Optimizing with search Lagrangian is considerably fast, but the search speed will degrade with increasing the problem resolution (i.e., the number of elements in both the macro \[Ω[*dM*]\] and micro \[Ω[*dm*]\] design domains).

Several techniques exist to accelerate the process by adaptively adjusting the search steps based on statistical measures, although this falls outside the scope of this paper. Additionally, metaheuristic optimization methods [\[17\]](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0017) do not require sensitivity analysis. However, for topology optimization, the metaheuristic approach incurs significant computational costs, making it unsuitable for multi‐scale and multiphysics topology optimization problems.

In general, multi‐objective optimization problems yield multiple solutions. Addressing a specific multi‐objective function formulation often necessitates satisfying conditions for Pareto optimality. Ideally, one might identify a dominant solution, which is defined as being superior to all other solutions with respect to at least one objective function while being at least as good as the remaining solutions concerning the other objectives. When a solution fulfills these criteria, it is termed a Pareto optimal solution. The general formulation of multi‐objective optimization is represented in Eq. [(4.15)](#c04.xhtml#c04-disp-0015).

where ![ModifyingAbove f With bar left-parenthesis bold x right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0003.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mover accent="true"><mi>f</mi><mo stretchy="true">&#175;</mo></mover><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi></mfenced></math> is the multi‐objective function of the n^(th) sub‐functions, and the design variable **x** is searched with in the feasible region (A ⊂ ℝ). The feasibility is bounded by suitable constraints *g*[*i*](**x**).

A prevalent approach in multi‐objective optimization is to reformulate the problem as a summation of individual objective functions. In this study, the multi‐objective problem is simplified to a single composite function representing the weighted sum of the various objectives. The weights for this summation are predetermined prior to the optimization process.

(4.16)![f overbar equals sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript 2 Endscripts w Subscript i Baseline f Subscript i](images/c04-math-0016.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <mover accent="true"><mi>f</mi> <mo stretchy="true">&#175;</mo> </mover> <mo>=</mo> <munderover> <mo movablelimits="false">&#8721;</mo> <mrow> <mi>i</mi> <mo>=</mo> <mn>1</mn> </mrow> <mn>2</mn> </munderover> <msub> <mi>w</mi> <mi>i</mi> </msub> <msub> <mi>f</mi> <mi>i</mi> </msub> </math> 

The solution's fitness and prioritization are governed by the weight assigned to the objective function (*w*[*i*]). A higher weight indicates greater importance for that objective function compared to others with smaller weights, as illustrated in [Figure 4.6](#c04.xhtml#c04-fig-0006).

In practical scenarios, achieving feasible design variables for all objective functions may not always be possible. This limitation implies that the Pareto front, which represents the set of non‐dominated solutions in multi‐objective optimization, may not be accurately represented across all objectives. Consequently, the resulting solutions could fail to satisfy the criteria for a proper Pareto optimal solution. To address this issue, it is essential to first establish a clear definition of what constitutes a proper Pareto optimal solution. The proper Pareto front contains the solution **x** such that for each sub‐objective function *f*[*i*], there exists at least one *f*[*j*] such that

where *M* is a scalar larger than 0. To effectively attain an optimal trade‐off in multi‐objective optimization, various modification techniques are employed to reformulate the problem. Among these, normalization stands out as a particularly reliable approach. This technique ensures that no single objective function dominates the optimization process solely due to its initial scale or value. In this study, we utilize the goal programming method, which seeks to minimize the deviation of the solution vector from the ideal values of each objective function. The goal programming approach identifies solutions along the Pareto front that best satisfy the specified weighting criteria. The knee point on this front, which is the closest to the ideal or utopian solution, is deemed the most preferable. The normalization process for the goal programming method is expressed as follows:

## 4.5 TOPOLOGY OPTIMIZATION METHODOLOGIES

Topology optimization has seen considerable progress in recent years and remains a vibrant research field, largely propelled by advancements in CAD and the rapid development of additive manufacturing technologies. This optimization approach allows for the use of mathematical search algorithms to iteratively refine the design domain and identify the optimal configuration. A variety of methodologies for topology optimization have been proposed in the literature, with the SIMP method, the evolutionary structural optimization (ESO) method, and the metaheuristic structure binary‐distribution (MSB) method being the most widely discussed. Each of these methods will be explored in detail.

 

### 4.5.1 Solid Isotropic Materials with Penalization Method

The SIMP method for topology optimization involves discretizing the design domain to identify a smooth, optimal structure. Derived from homogenization theory [\[18\]](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0018), the SIMP method assumes that the material consists of isotropically distributed density fields, referred to as artificial densities. To enforce binarization of the design domain—essentially determining whether a material exists at a given location—these artificial densities must be penalized using a power law. This penalty encourages the design domain to adopt discrete material states. Consequently, material properties are interpolated based on the penalized density field within the discretized domain. The optimization problem is then formulated as maximizing or minimizing the objective function, which incorporates the density field through the interpolation scheme, with respect to the design variables of the density field, as shown in Eq. [(4.19)](#c04.xhtml#c04-disp-0019)

(4.19)![Extremum upper X left-parenthesis normal x right-parenthesis vertical-bar normal x Superscript p](images/c04-math-0019.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <mtext>Extremum</mtext> <mspace width="0.24em"/> <mfenced open="" close="|" separators=""><mrow> <mi>X</mi> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi> </mfenced> </mrow> </mfenced> <mspace width="0.2em"/> <msup> <mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi> <mi>p</mi> </msup> </math> 

where *X*(x)| is the cost function, and x is the design variables that represent SIMP as the artificial isotropic density. As previously noted, the SIMP method is favored for its robustness in structural optimization. However, excessively high penalization values can compromise the convexity of the objective function, potentially leading the optimization process to converge on local minima rather than the global optimum. In the current framework, several challenges arise, notably checkerboard patterns and non‐existence issues. The latter, which pertains to mesh dependency, often results in the introduction of zero‐density elements, a phenomenon driven by the algorithm's attempt to minimize the objective function.

To address this, the relaxation principle is employed. This principle involves adjusting the density function to incorporate a so‐called gray region, thereby mitigating the occurrence of zero‐density elements and improving solution robustness. (x ∈ (0, 1\]). The application of heuristic algorithms with appropriate constraints is often employed to address the challenges of topology optimization, particularly in mitigating issues such as checkerboarding. Checkerboarding arises as a consequence of discretization and is inherently linked to the heuristic approach used to identify optimal solutions. This issue manifests when design elements are constrained to binary states—either fully present or completely absent—resulting in discontinuous sensitivity variations across the finite element mesh. Consequently, small changes in the presence of an element can lead to abrupt changes in the objective function values due to the discretization process.

Checkerboarding is especially prevalent with low‐order finite elements, where the coarse resolution exacerbates the problem. While increasing the order of the elements, that is, the number of nodes per element, might alleviate checkerboarding, it incurs a substantial increase in computational cost. Alternatively, enhancing the resolution of the design domain by increasing the number of elements can reduce the checkerboard patterns significantly, yet this also results in a considerable rise in computational demand.

To address these issues more effectively, one approach is to implement sensitivity filtering techniques. By averaging the sensitivities of neighboring elements—those in proximity to the element in question—through mesh‐independent filtering schemes, the continuity of sensitivity variations is improved, thereby reducing the appearance of checkerboarding. This method helps balance the trade‐offs between solution accuracy and computational efficiency.

 

### 4.5.2 Evolutionary Structural Optimization Method

The optimization process in topology design is inherently influenced by the initial configuration of design variables, which promotes the adoption of binarization techniques. In these approaches, the design domain is initially defined with binary values—either 0 or 1—and remains updated throughout the optimization process until the final design is achieved. This strategy aligns with the practice of binarized topology optimization methods, such as the ESO method, which has gained significant traction in recent years \[[19](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0019)–[21](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0021)\].

ESO operates on the principle of binarizing the initial design domain, wherein design variables are set to binary states from the outset. The method utilizes a procedure that involves progressively zeroing out the central element values within a periodic structure. This approach effectively triggers an inverse homogenization process, where the material is systematically removed based on the performance criteria. The overarching doctrine of ESO is to achieve a clear delineation between materials and voids, optimizing the structure by removing inefficient material regions while maintaining the overall structural integrity. This binarization process facilitates a straightforward and computationally efficient means of arriving at optimized structural designs \[[22](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0022), [23](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0023)\].

Implementing ESO under these specified conditions results in a uniform design distribution while mitigating the risk of local minima. ESO achieves this by progressively addressing nearly the entire design domain and striving to minimize the volume to identify an optimal binary topology.

The fundamental principle of ESO revolves around the direct elimination of “inefficient materials” to progressively shape the structure into an optimal design. Originally introduced by Min and Steven \[[19](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0019)\], ESO leverages the sensitivity of the cost function to guide updates to the design variables. This approach systematically refines the material distribution based on its effectiveness, ensuring that the final design is optimized for performance and material efficiency [\[24\]](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0024). Updating depends on the element's sensitivity number *α*[*i*], as shown in Eq. [(4.20):](#c04.xhtml#c04-disp-0020)

(4.20)![alpha Subscript i Baseline equals StartLayout Enlarged left-brace 1st Row 1st Column italic d upper X Subscript i Baseline 2nd Column for normal x Subscript i Baseline equals 1 2nd Row 1st Column 0 2nd Column for normal x Subscript i Baseline equals x Subscript min Baseline EndLayout](images/c04-math-0020.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <msub> <mi>&#945;</mi> <mi>i</mi> </msub> <mo>=</mo> <mfenced open="{" close="" separators=""><mtable> <mtr> <mtd> <msub> <mi mathvariant="italic">dX</mi> <mi>i</mi> </msub> <mspace width="0.5em"/> </mtd> <mtd> <mtext>for</mtext> <mspace width="0.36em"/> <msub> <mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi> <mi>i</mi> </msub> <mo>=</mo> <mn>1</mn> </mtd> </mtr> <mtr> <mtd> <mn>0</mn> <mspace width="0.5em"/> </mtd> <mtd> <mtext>for</mtext> <mspace width="0.36em"/> <msub> <mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi> <mi>i</mi> </msub> <mo>=</mo> <msub> <mi>x</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">min</mi> </msub> </mtd> </mtr> </mtable> </mfenced> </math> 

In essence, ESO focuses on eliminating redundant materials, identified by their low sensitivity values. This methodology is constrained to the removal of materials only, meaning that once materials are discarded, they cannot be reintroduced. Consequently, an initially oversized design domain is crucial to guarantee that the final design encompasses all potential configurations.

To address this limitation, the bi‐directional evolutionary structural optimization (BESO) method was developed [\[25\]](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0025). BESO enhances the robustness of ESO by allowing both the addition and removal of materials within the design domain. This bi‐directional approach improves the overall flexibility of the optimization process, thereby reducing the likelihood of converging on local extrema and ensuring a more comprehensive exploration of the design space.

 

### 4.5.3 Metaheuristic Structure Binary‐Distribution Method

The MSB method, introduced by Al‐Ali et al. \[[6](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0006)\], is designed to expedite the resolution of topology optimization problems while mitigating the risk of encountering local minima. This methodology integrates YUKI's hybrid metaheuristic optimization with gradient descent techniques. To provide clarity, we will first present a concise overview of the YUKI algorithm, followed by a detailed exposition of the MSB method's formulations.

The YUKI algorithm is a novel metaheuristic optimization method designed to find optimal solutions with minimal computational effort. It achieves this through three key features. The first characteristic of the YUKI optimization algorithm is its use of the best solutions from a given iteration (*i*) as the starting point for finding improved solutions in the subsequent iteration (*i* + 1).

The YUKI algorithm integrates the notion of “Best Points” to represent the personal best solutions within the optimization process. By harnessing the collective intelligence of all points within the swarm, each individual point effectively navigates the search space and refines its best solutions. This collective approach enhances the global search capability, allowing the swarm to continuously adapt and improve. In each iteration, the algorithm evaluates the population of solutions and utilizes a vector to monitor the values associated with the optimal fitness criteria—such as minimizing thermal compliance in the current context. The Best Points accumulated throughout the iterations are then employed to determine a singular representative point, termed the “MeanBest.” This MeanBest point serves as the centroid of the cluster of optimal solutions, with its value computed as the average of the identified Best Points. This method ensures a balanced and accurate representation of the optimal solution, thereby improving the efficacy of the optimization process.

The YUKI algorithm initiates by defining a local search domain centered around the optimal solution identified up to that point. The extent of this search domain is dictated by the distance between this solution and the MeanBest point. This methodology encompasses several distinctive attributes:

1.  **1.** **Adaptive Search Domain**: As solutions progress toward the optimal, the local search domain contracts, reflecting increased convergence and refinement in the search.
2.  **2.** **Maintained Global Coverage**: The distance between Best Points is preserved sufficiently large, ensuring comprehensive global search coverage and mitigating the risk of premature convergence to local minima.
3.  **3.** **Dynamic Adjustment**: The search domain size is dynamically adjusted based on the discovery of new optimal solutions within the local area, allowing for expansion of the search space as needed.
4.  **4.** **Continuous Update**: Throughout the optimization process, if no new optimal solution is identified, the MeanBest is regularly updated, facilitating the generation of various search domain sizes and maintaining search dynamism.
5.  **5.** **Variable Sensitivity**: The algorithm's effectiveness is contingent on the global search space size rather than individual design variables. Consequently, the local search domain may exhibit significant variation in size across different dimensions, reflecting the sensitivity of each variable and ensuring that the algorithm adapts to the distinct characteristics of each design variable.

The local boundaries are calculated by the following expressions:

(4.21)![StartLayout 1st Row 1st Column upper D 2nd Column equals StartAbsoluteValue bold x Subscript italic best Baseline minus bold x Subscript italic MeanBest Baseline EndAbsoluteValue EndLayout](images/c04-math-0021.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <mtable> <mtr> <mtd> <mi>D</mi> </mtd> <mtd> <mo>=</mo> <mfenced open="|" close="|" separators=""><mrow> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mtext mathvariant="italic">best</mtext> </msub> <mo>&#8722;</mo> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mtext mathvariant="italic">MeanBest</mtext> </msub> </mrow> </mfenced> </mtd> </mtr> </mtable> </math> (4.22)![StartLayout 1st Row 1st Column italic upper L upper B 2nd Column equals bold x Subscript italic best Baseline plus upper D EndLayout](images/c04-math-0022.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <mtable> <mtr> <mtd> <mi mathvariant="italic">LB</mi> </mtd> <mtd> <mo>=</mo> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mtext mathvariant="italic">best</mtext> </msub> <mo>+</mo> <mi>D</mi> </mtd> </mtr> </mtable> </math> (4.23)![StartLayout 1st Row 1st Column italic upper L upper T 2nd Column equals bold x Subscript italic best Baseline minus upper D EndLayout](images/c04-math-0023.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <mtable> <mtr> <mtd> <mi mathvariant="italic">LT</mi> </mtd> <mtd> <mo>=</mo> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mtext mathvariant="italic">best</mtext> </msub> <mo>&#8722;</mo> <mi>D</mi> </mtd> </mtr> </mtable> </math> 

where **x**[*best*] represents the variable value associated with the best fitness value discovered to date. **x**[*MeanBest*] denotes the average of the “Best Points” vector. *LT* and *LB* refer to the local upper and lower boundaries of the search space, respectively.

The second characteristic of the YUKI algorithm is the division of the search space (Ω). The population (i.e., the design variables) is divided into two segments. One segment is tasked with exploring areas outside the local search region (Ω^(*EXP*)), while the other focuses on searching within the local search region (Ω^(*Local*)).

The population size within each segment of the search space is adjusted in a linear fashion. Initially, the algorithm allocates a substantial proportion of the population to exploration, facilitating a broad search of the design space. As iterations advance, the focus gradually shifts toward intensifying the search within the local vicinity of promising solutions. This transition is governed by a probabilistic mechanism: A random value is generated between 0 and 1 and compared against a threshold value derived from a specific expression. This comparison determines the extent of focus on exploration versus exploitation within the local search area, thereby optimizing the balance between global exploration and local refinement [\[26\]](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0026).

(4.24)![Evaluate left-parenthesis bold x right-parenthesis StartLayout Enlarged left-brace 1st Row bold x element-of normal upper Omega Superscript italic upper E upper X upper P Baseline comma italic r a n d less-than italic upper E upper X upper P 2nd Row bold x element-of normal upper Omega Superscript italic Local Baseline comma italic r a n d greater-than italic upper E upper X upper P EndLayout comma for-all bold x element-of normal upper Omega](images/c04-math-0024.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <mtext>Evaluate</mtext> <mspace width="0.24em"/> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> </mfenced> <mspace width="0.2em"/> <mfenced open="{" close="" separators=""><mtable> <mtr> <mtd> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mo>&#8712;</mo> <msup> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#937;</mi> <mi mathvariant="italic">EXP</mi> </msup> <mo>,</mo> <mspace width="0.5em"/> <mi mathvariant="italic">rand</mi> <mo>&#60;</mo> <mi mathvariant="italic">EXP</mi> </mtd> </mtr> <mtr> <mtd> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mo>&#8712;</mo> <msup> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#937;</mi> <mtext mathvariant="italic">Local</mtext> </msup> <mo>,</mo> <mspace width="0.3em"/> <mi mathvariant="italic">rand</mi> <mo>&#62;</mo> <mi mathvariant="italic">EXP</mi> </mtd> </mtr> </mtable> </mfenced> <mo>,</mo> <mspace width="1.2em"/> <mo>&#8704;</mo> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mo>&#8712;</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#937;</mi> </math> 

where *rand* is the random value between 0 and 1, and *EXP* is the exploration parameter of the YUKI algorithm, which assigns a percentage of the population to search outside the local search area. In contrast to other metaheuristic optimization algorithms, the YUKI algorithm excels in efficiently managing computational resources. This is achieved by concentrating efforts on areas with the most promising solutions while concurrently maintaining a broad scan of other regions.

A distinctive feature of the YUKI algorithm is its ability to conduct a comprehensive examination of the focus area through extensive exploration. Initially, it generates a random distribution of points within the local search domain. These points are then employed to probe potential solutions beyond the confines of the local search area. The exploration is strategically directed toward regions further from the MeanBest, a reference point defined by the position of the MeanBest, as specified in Eq. [(4.25)](#c04.xhtml#c04-disp-0025). This approach ensures that the search dynamically adapts, enhancing the likelihood of discovering optimal solutions.

(4.25)![upper E Superscript i Baseline equals bold x Subscript italic l o c Superscript i Baseline minus bold x Subscript italic best Superscript i](images/c04-math-0025.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <msup> <mi>E</mi> <mi>i</mi> </msup> <mo>=</mo> <msubsup> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mi mathvariant="italic">loc</mi> <mi>i</mi> </msubsup> <mo>&#8722;</mo> <msubsup> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mtext mathvariant="italic">best</mtext> <mi>i</mi> </msubsup> </math> 

where ![bold x Subscript italic l o c Superscript i](images/c04-i0006.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msubsup><mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi><mi mathvariant="italic">loc</mi><mi>i</mi></msubsup></math> denotes a position of the selected point to be directed toward exploration, and ![bold x Subscript italic best Superscript i](images/c04-i0007.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msubsup><mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi><mtext mathvariant="italic">best</mtext><mi>i</mi></msubsup></math> is the best position found by this point during the optimization process (i.e., historical best solution). This distance *E*^(*i*) is used to set the reach of the exploration range for this particular point. Then, the new solutions are calculated by the following equation:

(4.26)![bold x Subscript italic n e w Superscript i Baseline equals bold x Subscript italic l o c Superscript i Baseline plus upper E Superscript i](images/c04-math-0026.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <msubsup> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mi mathvariant="italic">new</mi> <mi>i</mi> </msubsup> <mo>=</mo> <msubsup> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mi mathvariant="italic">loc</mi> <mi>i</mi> </msubsup> <mo>+</mo> <msup> <mi>E</mi> <mi>i</mi> </msup> </math> 

For the rest of the population that wasn't selected for exploration, they will be sent to look around the search center. This is expressed by the following equation:

(4.27)![StartLayout 1st Row 1st Column upper F Superscript i 2nd Column equals bold x Subscript italic l o c Superscript i Baseline plus bold x Subscript italic best EndLayout](images/c04-math-0027.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <mtable> <mtr> <mtd> <msup> <mi>F</mi> <mi>i</mi> </msup> </mtd> <mtd> <mo>=</mo> <msubsup> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mi mathvariant="italic">loc</mi> <mi>i</mi> </msubsup> <mo>+</mo> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mtext mathvariant="italic">best</mtext> </msub> </mtd> </mtr> </mtable> </math> (4.28)![StartLayout 1st Row 1st Column bold x Subscript italic n e w Superscript i 2nd Column equals bold x Subscript italic l o c Superscript i Baseline minus italic r a n d times upper F Superscript i EndLayout](images/c04-math-0028.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <mtable> <mtr> <mtd> <msubsup> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mi mathvariant="italic">new</mi> <mi>i</mi> </msubsup> </mtd> <mtd> <mo>=</mo> <msubsup> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mi mathvariant="italic">loc</mi> <mi>i</mi> </msubsup> <mo form="postfix">&#8722;</mo> <mi mathvariant="italic">rand</mi> <mo>&#215;</mo> <msup> <mi>F</mi> <mi>i</mi> </msup> </mtd> </mtr> </mtable> </math> 

Here, the same random value *rand* is used for all design variables. *F*^(*i*) is the distance from the selected local point to the absolute best solution. And for each solution, the same random value is used for all design variables.

## 4.6 SHAPE OPTIMIZATION

Shape optimization is a subset of non‐parametric optimization that focuses on modifying the boundaries of the design domain to achieve an optimal configuration. Unlike parametric optimization, which relies on predefined variables, shape optimization updates the geometry directly, allowing for greater design flexibility. Key methods in this field include the level‐set method, the H¹ gradient method, and the phase‐field method (PFM). These methods are discussed in detail in the following subsections.

 

### 4.6.1 Level‐Set Method

The level‐set method, akin to ESO and MSB, employs binarized design variables. Originally conceived by Dervieux and Thomasset [\[27\]](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0027) for studying multi‐phase flows, this methodology is fundamentally a non‐parametric technique that implicitly represents shapes. Its formulation and application were initially explored in the context of fluid interfaces \[[28](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0028), [29](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0029)\]. The term “level set” itself was coined later by Osher and Sethian [\[30\]](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0030), who introduced complex topological notations to the method.

The level‐set method has found extensive applications in segmentation and object shape identification within the realm of computer vision [\[31\]](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0031). Initially constrained by the computational limitations of its time, due to its implicit nature and significant resource demands, the method's viability was restricted. However, advancements in computational power have alleviated these constraints, allowing for the method's application to more computationally intensive problems.

Subsequent developments led to the successful use of the level‐set method in stress minimization problems by Sethian and Wiegmann [\[32\]](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0032), marking a significant shift toward its application in moderately computationally demanding tasks such as structural strain energy minimization \[[33](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0033), [34](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0034)\]. This evolution underscores the method's adaptability and efficacy in addressing complex optimization challenges.

In shape optimization, the level set provides an implicit representation of the transformation of the decision variables x, which are part of the cost function, within a domain defined by the level‐set function *φ*. This representation specifically addresses a level ℓ, at which the function has a neutral sign. Values above this level yield a positive sign, while those below it result in a negative sign for the design variable.

(4.29)![normal x right-arrow from bar upper X left-parenthesis normal x right-parenthesis equals left-brace normal x element-of partial-differential upper Omega bar phi left-parenthesis upper X left-parenthesis normal x right-parenthesis right-parenthesis equals normal script l right-brace for-all normal x element-of normal double struck upper R squared](images/c04-math-0029.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi> <mo>&#8614;</mo> <mi>X</mi> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi> </mfenced> <mo>=</mo> <mfenced open="{" close="}" separators=""><mrow> <mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi> <mo>&#8712;</mo> <mi>&#8706;&#937;</mi> <mo>&#8739;</mo> <mi>&#966;</mi> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow> <mi>X</mi> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi> </mfenced> </mrow> </mfenced> <mo>=</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#8467;</mi> </mrow> </mfenced> <mspace width="0.36em"/> <mo>&#8704;</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi> <mo>&#8712;</mo> <msup> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#8477;</mi> <mn>2</mn> </msup> </math> 

As shown in [Figure 4.7](#c04.xhtml#c04-fig-0007), the representation of the curve outline changes at the intersection of the measuring level of the level set and the functional surface.

The level‐set method offers a transformative approach to representing shapes within cost functions, generalizing the shape representation such that perturbations at specific points above or below the level set do not significantly impact the overall shape. This robustness addresses numerical instabilities inherent in earlier formulations, particularly in the abstract form of the level‐set method where shape derivatives are considered without relying on parametrized bi‐directional updates or topology‐derivative regulation factors [\[35\]](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0035).

In the context of topology optimization using the level‐set method, the problem is typically framed as a single material‐void scenario. The level‐set representation is effectively captured by the signed distance function, which is articulated mathematically in Eq. [(4.30)](#c04.xhtml#c04-disp-0030).

</mfenced> <mo>&#8712;</mo> <msup> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#8477;</mi> <mn>2</mn> </msup> <mo>,</mo> <mo>&#8704;</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi> <mo>&#8713;</mo> <msub> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#937;</mi> <mtext mathvariant="italic">material</mtext> </msub> </mtd> </mtr> </mtable> </mfenced> </math> 

where ℓ is the level plane that the function is evaluated around. Here the intersection boundaries of the function and the level plane are the boundaries to be evaluated for. The topology optimization problem necessitates the binary material representation to have 1 for the material existence and 0 for the void. This representation can ease the choice of ℓ to be 0, and this complies with Osher notation [\[30\]](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0030).

![Figure 4.7 Level‐set representations of the boundary for a single function.](images/c04f007.png)

[**Figure 4.7**](#c04.xhtml#R_c04-fig-0007) Level‐set representations of the boundary for a single function.

In general, level set‐based topology optimization does not operate directly on the cost function *X* but instead focuses on the function *φ*. Since *φ* represents a surface, it simplifies the evolution process from a mathematical standpoint. However, as previously mentioned, the implicit representation of *φ* incurs additional computational costs, requiring updates to all decision variables x in ℝ².

Thus, the level‐set function is constructed under the premise of evolving the curve through the orthogonal progression of the design variable along the level‐set plane. The evolution proceeds in the form of

(4.34)![StartFraction partial-differential phi Over partial-differential t EndFraction equals v StartAbsoluteValue nabla phi EndAbsoluteValue](images/c04-math-0034.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <mfrac> <mrow> <mi>&#8706;</mi> <mi>&#966;</mi> </mrow> <mrow> <mi>&#8706;</mi> <mi>t</mi> </mrow> </mfrac> <mo>=</mo> <mi>v</mi> <mfenced open="|" close="|" separators=""><mrow> <mi>&#8711;</mi> <mi>&#966;</mi> </mrow> </mfenced> </math> 

The optimization procedure is carried out to assess the sensitivity of the level set by advancing along the functional boundaries, ultimately leading to the Hamilton–Jacobi equation. The boundaries are subsequently updated using optimization methods, such as the forward Euler scheme. This method incorporates the Heaviside function, which assigns a value of one when the level‐set function is positive or zero, and a value of zero in void regions.

While the volumetric representation *V* of the function with respect to the level set takes the form of

(4.39)![upper V equals integral Underscript normal upper Omega Endscripts upper H left-parenthesis phi left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis right-parenthesis d normal upper Omega](images/c04-math-0039.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <mi>V</mi> <mo>=</mo> <msub> <mo>&#8747;</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#937;</mi> </msub> <mi>H</mi> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow> <mi>&#966;</mi> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mi>x</mi> </mfenced> </mrow> </mfenced> <mspace width="0.2em"/> <mi>d</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#937;</mi> </math> 

The level‐set method has been introduced as a technique that avoids mesh‐dependent issues such as checkerboard patterns [\[36\]](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0036) and grayscale artifacts [\[37\]](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0037). However, as an evolutionary approach, the function parameters are problem‐dependent.

In other words, it is important to select a suitable initial value of *φ* and the normal velocity *v*. The evolution of boundaries in topology optimization via the level‐set method may exhibit insufficient sensitivity to local variations within the optimization loop, potentially leading to the convergence toward local minima.

This limitation arises from the method's inherent constraint in dynamically creating new boundaries within solid regions, as well as its challenge in accommodating changes distant from the initial boundaries. Several strategies have been proposed to address these limitations, including the incorporation of topological derivatives, parameterization of the level‐set function, and the application of regularized Heaviside functions to mitigate numerical instabilities and avoid singularities in the conductivity matrix. The Heaviside function, which assumes a value of one for positive values of the level‐set function and zero for void regions, plays a crucial role in these adjustments. In the current study, the focus remains solely on shape derivatives. Notably, the level‐set method has been extensively applied in multi‐scale topology optimization for both static and dynamic mechanical problems by various researchers \[[38](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0038)–[40](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0040)\].

 

### 4.6.2 H¹ Gradient Method

The H¹ gradient method is used as a shape optimization technique to refine the microstructure's detailed shape. Originally introduced by Azegami to address domain optimization challenges in boundary value problems [\[41\]](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0041), this method was later extended by Shimoda et al. through numerical analysis using general‐purpose finite element software [\[42\]](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0042). It has since been applied to various structures, including shell frame and fiber orientation \[[43](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0043), [44](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0044)\] problems. More recently, the H¹ gradient method has been adapted for shape optimization in multi‐scale structures. For instance, Fujioka et al. proposed a shape optimization approach for microstructures aimed at maximizing the stiffness of a macrostructure in a two‐dimensional elastic body composed of porous materials under area constraints [\[45\]](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0045). Additionally, they developed methods focused on enhancing structural strength through shape optimization, specifically for designing porous structures with high strength [\[46\]](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0046).

 

### 4.6.3 Phase‐Field Method

The PFM has emerged as a powerful approach for addressing shape optimization challenges in engineering and design. Initially developed for simulating phase transitions in materials, PFM has been adapted to a broad spectrum of applications, including shape optimization. In this method, the geometry of a design is represented by a continuous phase‐field variable, *φ*(**x**) ∈ \[0, 1\], which smoothly transitions between distinct phases. Typically, *φ*(**x**) = 1 denotes the material inclusion, while *φ*(**x**) = 0 represents the surrounding void, with a diffuse interface between these regions. This representation eliminates the need for explicit boundary tracking, simplifying the modeling of complex geometries and topological changes. PFM frames the problem as the minimization (or maximization) of an objective functional *J*(*φ*, *χ*), where *χ* represents state variables governed by physical laws. The evolution of the phase‐field variable is governed by a PDE, which ensures smooth boundary transitions and allows for topological flexibility. The objective functional is augmented with a regularization term based on the Ginzburg–Landau energy to maintain numerical stability and ensure well‐posedness \[[47](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0047)–[49](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0049)\].

 

## 4.7 POROUS STRUCTURAL DESIGN

Over the past few decades, numerous approaches have evolved in the design of porous structures to efficiently achieve robust solutions that meet specific requirements. Prior to the advancements in additive manufacturing, many designs were constrained by the limitations of traditional manufacturing methods, where cost and manufacturability were major factors. This lack of design freedom significantly hindered the development of porous structures, and the concept of designing the pores themselves was often considered impractical. However, the emergence of 3D‐printing technologies has revolutionized this paradigm, offering unprecedented freedom in design and fabrication. As a result, new methods for porous structure design have emerged, enabling more sophisticated and optimized solutions. The following section will explore these innovative methodologies in porous structure design.

 

### 4.7.1 Biologically Inspired

The design of porous structures inspired by cellular biological systems draws upon the extraordinary efficiency and functionality observed in nature's lattice architectures. Natural porous structures, such as the trabecular bone in human anatomy or the intricate wings of a dragonfly, exemplify optimized strength‐to‐weight ratios, with resilience to impact. These biological models serve as a valuable source of inspiration for developing engineered porous materials that seek to replicate the advantages found in these natural systems.

The modern process of designing bio‐inspired porous structures often begins with a detailed analysis of existing natural models. A critical step involves extracting microscale design features using advanced imaging techniques such as computed tomography (CT) scans. These scans provide detailed cross‐sectional images of natural porous structures, capturing the intricate micro‐architecture with high precision. The data from CT scans are then used to create a 3D representation of the porous structure, often saved in a format such as stereolithography (STL) files. Once the 3D model is established, the microstructure is chosen and evaluated (as illustrated in [Figure 4.8](#c04.xhtml#c04-fig-0008)). The advent of 3D‐printing technology has revolutionized the production of these intricate porous structures.

![Figure 4.8 Biologically inspired microstructure process.](images/c04f008.png)

[**Figure 4.8**](#c04.xhtml#R_c04-fig-0008) Biologically inspired microstructure process.

With 3D printing, researchers can fabricate complex, variable‐porosity designs with high precision and accuracy. This technology allows for the creation of structures that would be challenging to achieve using traditional manufacturing methods. The ability to print detailed and customized porous structures directly from digital models significantly enhances the flexibility and capability of material design.

 

### 4.7.2 Parametric Design of Porous Structures

Designing porous structures through the parametric optimization of cellular structures, particularly those utilizing triply periodic minimal surfaces (TPMS), represents a significant advancement in material science and structural engineering \[[3](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0003), [48](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0048),[50](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0050)–[53](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0053)\]. TPMS, such as the gyroid, and diamond surfaces (as depicted in [Figure 4.9](#c04.xhtml#c04-fig-0009)), offer an advantage due to their periodicity in three independent spatial directions and their characteristic mean curvature of zero at every point on the surface. These surfaces, often used in the design of porous materials, provide a versatile framework for modeling various geometric configurations. Their intrinsic properties allow for the creation of highly complex and varied porous structures through the manipulation of parametric models [\[54\]](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0054).

A key benefit of using TPMS in porous structure design lies in their ability to be parametrically adjusted to achieve different geometries. For instance, a gyroid surface, a specific type of TPMS, is characterized by its periodic and minimal surface properties which make it particularly amenable to changes in structural parameters \[[55](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0055)–[57](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0057)\]. By adjusting these parameters, designers can precisely control the size and shape of the apertures within the porous material. This flexibility is illustrated in [Figure 4.8](#c04.xhtml#c04-fig-0008), where different porosity gradient distributions are achieved by varying the parameters of the TPMS model.

The fundamental principle behind these adjustments involves the implicit function expressions that define the TPMS. The gyroid surface, for example, is described by an implicit function that encodes the periodic surface features. By altering parameters within this function, one can fine‐tune the pore sizes and distribution, allowing for the customization of the porous material's structural properties to meet specific engineering requirements.

![Figure 4.9 Examples of the TPMS model.](images/c04f009.png)

[**Figure 4.9**](#c04.xhtml#R_c04-fig-0009) Examples of the TPMS model.

 

### 4.7.3 Non‐Parametric Design of Porous Structures

 

#### 4.7.3.1 Mono‐Scale

Mono‐scale topology optimization is a powerful approach in the design of porous structures. Unlike multi‐scale modeling, which relies on the assumption of distinct separation between different length scales, mono‐scale optimization operates under the principle that material distribution can be optimized within a single scale. This approach is grounded in the discretization of the design domain into a finite‐sized mesh, where each element of the mesh represents a portion of the material. As the mesh resolution increases, the resulting structures can exhibit intricate, fine‐scale features that are essential for optimizing stiffness and other mechanical properties.

The process of mono‐scale topology optimization involves the allocation of materials within the design domain to achieve optimal structural performance under specified loading conditions. By iteratively adjusting the material distribution, the optimization algorithm seeks to minimize or maximize an objective function, such as structural compliance, while adhering to constraints on material volume or other design parameters.

One of the key advantages of mono‐scale topology optimization is its ability to generate highly efficient structural designs without the need to delve into the complexity of multi‐scale modeling. As the mesh resolution becomes finer, the optimized structures can naturally evolve to exhibit multi‐scale characteristics. The successful application of mono‐scale topology optimization to the design of porous structures requires meticulous attention to several critical factors. Among these, the continuation technique employed during the optimization process plays a pivotal role. Continuation techniques involve the gradual variation of specific parameters within the optimization algorithm to guide the search toward a globally optimal solution. Without these strategies, the algorithm is prone to premature convergence to suboptimal solutions, particularly in complex design spaces characterized by numerous local optima. Equally important is the local control of material distribution within the design domain. Two primary strategies for achieving this control are pattern repetition and local volume constraints. In the pattern repetition strategy, the design domain is divided into multiple subdomains. Each subdomain undergoes refinement to allow for detailed material distribution, with the layout constrained to be identical across all subdomains. This approach ensures periodic patterns throughout the structure, making it particularly advantageous for applications requiring uniformity and regularity, such as biomedical implants and filtration devices.

For applications necessitating graded or varying material distributions, subdomains can be grouped, with each group sharing a common material layout. This method enables the creation of structures exhibiting periodic patterns along one axis while allowing material variation along another axis. Such flexibility is essential for designing structures that must perform optimally under varying load conditions or environmental influences. The implementation of these local control strategies often relies on templates that establish links between subdomains within the design domain. These templates serve as references for material layout, where the sensitivity of each template point is computed as the aggregate sensitivity of the corresponding points in all linked subdomains. This approach effectively reduces the optimization problem's solution space, as the algorithm only needs to determine the material layout for a single subdomain or a small group of subdomains. Despite this reduction, the optimization and analysis are conducted on the full mesh, ensuring that the final structure is optimized at the resolution of the entire design domain. However, the computational power needed is phenomenally expensive for topology optimization.

 

#### 4.7.3.2 Multi‐scale Approach

Multi‐scale topology optimization for designing porous structures represents an advanced lightweight engineering structure, characterized by its ability to optimize material distribution across multiple scales, thereby achieving superior performance with low computational cost. This optimization process is inherently mathematically complex due to the need to manage interactions across different scales, from the macroscale down to the microscale, and vice versa where material behavior and structural performance must be simultaneously considered. The challenge lies in the appropriate representation and optimization of the unit cells, the fundamental building blocks of the structure, which dictate the overall physical properties through their geometric and material characteristics.

As the restrictions on unit cell design increase, the flexibility in the parameterization of these cells decreases. In highly restricted approaches, unit cells are parameterized by a single parameter, such as density, which significantly simplifies the optimization process but also limits the achievable structural performance. This trade‐off between design flexibility and computational efficiency is a key consideration in multi‐scale topology optimization. The optimization process in these cases typically involves predefined cellular structures, where the effective material properties of the unit cells are evaluated using homogenization techniques. Homogenization is a powerful tool in multi‐scale modeling, allowing for the computation of effective material properties by averaging the microscale behavior over a representative volume element. This process is computationally efficient because it can be performed offline, prior to the actual optimization, thereby reducing the computational burden during the design process.

The use of predefined unit cells in multi‐scale topology optimization offers several advantages, particularly in the context of additive manufacturing, where these cells can be directly translated into 3D‐printed components. The optimization of such structures often requires minimal adaptation of traditional mono‐scale optimization techniques, as the repeating unit cells can be treated as materials whose distribution is optimized across the structure. This approach is particularly attractive for designing components with uniform infill patterns, which are commonly used in industries such as aerospace and biomedical engineering. The simplicity of this method lies in its ability to reduce the solution space of the optimization problem, often to a single dimension, thereby facilitating the construction of differentiable functions that map the reduced parameters to the homogenized properties of the material.

However, the use of predefined unit cells also imposes certain limitations, particularly when dealing with anisotropic materials. Most mono‐scale optimization techniques are traditionally designed for isotropic materials, where the material properties are uniform in all directions. In contrast, anisotropic materials exhibit direction‐dependent properties, which require modifications to the stiffness matrices used in FEA. These modifications ensure that the homogenized elasticity tensor accurately reflects the anisotropic nature of the material, thereby preserving the integrity of the optimization results. The challenge is further compounded when the anisotropic microstructures are not aligned with the local principal stress directions, which can lead to suboptimal performance and structural inefficiencies.

To address these challenges, advanced multi‐scale topology optimization approaches have been developed that allow for greater flexibility in unit cell parameterization. These approaches include hierarchical and concurrent optimization methods, where the microstructures are represented by multi‐variable density fields. In hierarchical optimization, the structure is sequentially optimized at different scales, with the microscale optimization feeded into the macroscale design. This allows for the creation of structures that are optimized for both global and local performance criteria. Concurrent optimization, on the other hand, treats the structure as a unified entity, where the optimization is performed simultaneously across all scales. This approach is computationally more demanding but offers the potential for achieving truly optimal multi‐scale designs.

Mono‐scale topology optimization concentrates on optimizing the parameterized aspects of microstructures, such as material distribution, within a single, uniformly discretized scale, typically through a finely meshed design domain. While this approach allows for the emergence of fine‐scale features as the mesh resolution increases, it is inherently limited by the need to balance the trade‐offs between resolution and computational cost. As the mesh becomes finer, the computational resources required for optimization grow exponentially, often leading to prohibitive computational expenses, especially when addressing the complex interactions that occur at both the macro‐ and microscale levels in porous structures.

In contrast, multi‐scale topology optimization offers a more sophisticated approach by explicitly considering the separation of scales, thereby optimizing material distribution across multiple distinct scales simultaneously. This method significantly reduces computational cost while maintaining a high level of detail in the design of porous structures. By employing homogenization techniques and hierarchical modeling, multi‐scale optimization effectively decouples the macro‐ and microscale problems, allowing for the efficient computation of effective material properties at the microscale, which can then be utilized in the macroscale optimization. This not only reduces the dimensionality of the problem but also enables the design of structures with intricate microscale porosity embedded within a robust macroscale framework.

Moreover, the ability of multi‐scale optimization to craft highly detailed structures at both scales is particularly advantageous in applications requiring precise control over material properties, such as in biomedical implants or advanced filtration systems. The method's capacity to seamlessly integrate fine‐scale features into a global structure without overwhelming computational demands positions it as a superior choice for designing porous materials. While mono‐scale optimization may excel in generating detailed structures when computational resources are ample, multi‐scale topology optimization emerges as the better approach overall, particularly for large‐scale, complex designs where computational efficiency and the ability to address interactions across scales are paramount.
