# Chapter 5  
Practical Examples and Case Studies

 

## 5.1 INTRODUCTION

This chapter presents a series of case studies focused on the design of porous structures for a range of applications. These include lightweight heat dissipators, multiphysics problems requiring high mechanical stiffness and low heat absorptivity, as well as the design of soft robotics utilizing single‐ and multi‐materials. Additionally, the chapter introduces the multiphysics principles of heat‐harvesting mechanisms and grippers. The chapter encompasses both mathematical modeling and practical case studies to demonstrate how the final design of porous structures depends on the initial design of both the micro‐ and macrostructures. Notably, the details of the multiphysics model for high mechanical stiffness and low heat absorptivity will be discussed further in [Chapter 6](#c06.xhtml).

 

## 5.2 POROUS HEAT SINK DESIGNS WITH NON‐PARAMETRIC OPTIMIZATION

Thermally conductive solid structures, such as passive thermal solid‐state components, are extensively integrated into industrial systems, particularly as the exigency for high‐frequency data transmission intensifies with the advent of technologies like 5G cellular communication and autonomous vehicles. Traditional designs of thermal dissipation components often occupy significant physical space and impose excessive weight on printed circuit boards (PCBs), potentially leading to long‐term degradation of the printed wiring. This degradation arises from mechanical stress, including bending due to the component's weight or from resonant vibrations interacting with cooling fins, which can also alter the high‐frequency impedance characteristics. Consequently, the development of a lightweight, efficient heat dissipater becomes a highly sought‐after solution to mitigate these challenges \[[1](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0001)–[8](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0008)\].

In the context of non‐parametric optimization for the thermal conductivity of lightweight structures, shape and topology optimization strategies often focus on minimizing thermal compliance. This approach aims to reduce stored thermal energy in favor of enhancing heat conduction efficiency. While thermal compliance has been effectively employed as an objective function in the design of high‐conductivity solid structures, achieving an optimal design necessitates meticulous consideration of factors such as the discretization of the design domain and the intricacies of the optimization process \[[9](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0009)–[12](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0012)\]. In terms of designing thermal solutions, heat compliance is a major thermal performance metric used in optimization problems, particularly for heat sink designs, representing the system's resistance to heat flow and thermal energy distribution. So, with high thermal compliance, the design tends to store heat energy, while with low thermal compliance, heat dissipation is maximized, making it ideal for heat sink applications.

In the realm of thermal compliance minimization, researchers initially reported tree‐like configurations as effective design solutions. However, when the finite element resolution within the design domain exceeds a million elements, the emergence of spike‐like geometries, as observed by Wadbro et al., [\[13\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0013) becomes prevalent. This shift underscores the complexity inherent in optimizing thermal structures. Yan et al. [\[14\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0014) provided an in‐depth exploration of the thermal compliance optimization challenge and the characteristics of tree‐like designs. Their study benchmarks various study cases, highlighting how the selection of design variable interpolation schemes, conditions, and the initial design domain can profoundly influence the achievement of optimal solutions. Notably, their findings challenge the conventional wisdom that tree‐like structures are optimal for maximizing thermal conductivity.

Building on this foundation, we propose that by concurrently optimizing both the micro‐ and macrostructures of a material, one can achieve a superior performance‐to‐weight ratio under stringent design constraints. Specifically, optimizing the material layout within the representative volume element (RVE) as a secondary design domain, nested within the macroscale, can significantly enhance the overall performance of the structure. This approach suggests that by refining the microstructural arrangement, materials can achieve an extremum in performance metrics, resulting in a markedly higher performance‐to‐weight ratio, thereby pushing the boundaries of current thermal optimization paradigms. In this part, we address a critical gap in the design methodology for achieving fin‐like structures, as opposed to conventional tree‐like thermal structures, through the application of concurrent multi‐scale topology optimization. The primary objective of this work is to develop and rigorously examine the methodologies and formulations necessary for effective concurrent multi‐scale optimization in the context of thermal conductivity. This involves the exploration of the interpolation method that links the macro‐ and microscales within the design system, thereby enabling the seamless integration of multi‐scale design elements. Moreover, this part introduces a predetermined initial design domain scheme, which is pivotal in the pursuit of a robust three‐dimensional (3D) printable porous structure with enhanced thermal conductivity.

 

### 5.2.1 Simultaneous Multi‐scale Formulation of Thermal Conductivity Optimization for Porous Heat Sink Using Topological Methods

The simultaneous optimization of macro‐ and microsystems was conducted through concurrent topology optimization, aiming to minimize heat compliance at both scales, i.e., the micro (**x**[*M*])‐ and macroscale (**x**[*m*]). The macro‐ and microscale design domains were discretized using separate finite element systems, with bilinear structured meshes employed for both. In this framework, an elemental design variable (**x**) of 1 indicates solid material, while 0 represents a void, as expressed in Eq. [(5.1)](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0001).

(5.1)![bold x Subscript upper M comma m Baseline equals StartLayout Enlarged left-brace 1st Row 1 Design material 2nd Row 0 Void EndLayout](images/c05-math-0001.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mrow> <mi>M</mi> <mo>,</mo> <mi>m</mi> </mrow> </msub> <mo>=</mo> <mfenced open="{" close="" separators=""><mtable> <mtr> <mtd> <mn>1</mn> <mspace width="0.9em"/> <mtext>Design&#8201;&#8201;material</mtext> </mtd> </mtr> <mtr> <mtd> <mn>0</mn> <mspace width="0.9em"/> <mtext>Void</mtext> </mtd> </mtr> </mtable> </mfenced> </math> 

The concurrent design of multi‐scale problems necessitates the adoption of homogenization methods, which serve two critical purposes. The first is the determination of effective material properties, which are essential for the accurate modeling and analysis of the macrostructure.

The second purpose involves the utilization of inverse homogenization as a sophisticated tool for the concurrent design of both microstructure and macrostructure. In this context, inverse homogenization facilitates the tailored design of the microstructure to achieve desired macroscopic properties, enabling the optimization process to address both scales simultaneously. This dual application of homogenization methods ensures that the microstructural design is not only compatible with, but also enhances, the overall performance of the macrostructure, thereby achieving an optimized multi‐scale design that meets stringent thermal conductivity requirements.

 

### 5.2.2 Homogenization Approach of Linking the Multi‐scale for Heat Conduction Problem

Homogenization has been employed numerically to assess the effective properties, encapsulated by the homogenized conductivity tensor Λ^H, of the microstructural design domain. By definition, this micro design domain represents an RVE that is statistically homogeneous when compared to the larger macroscale domain. The effective properties derived from this homogenization process are influenced by several factors, including the material distribution within the RVE, the intrinsic behavior of the properties under consideration within the material, and the interfacial behavior at the boundaries between different material phases.

In the context of this study, it is assumed that thermal conductivity is uniformly distributed throughout the material. Additionally, the effects of the interface layer between different material phases have been neglected, simplifying the RVE to a single‐phase infinitesimal solid material interspersed with voids. This simplification allows for a more tractable numerical analysis while still capturing the essential characteristics of the material's thermal behavior.

By applying the asymptotic homogenization method, the effective properties of the microstructure are determined and subsequently utilized to construct the finite element model of the macroscale domain. Specifically, this approach considers a two‐phase microstructure, where the overall thermal conductivity is represented by Λ[*k*], *k* = 1, 2, denoting the thermal conductivity tensors associated with the respective phases of the RVE. The general steady‐state heat conduction within this system is governed by Eq. [(5.2)](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0002).

This formulation serves as the foundation for understanding and predicting the thermal behavior of the material system across multiple scales, thereby enabling the concurrent optimization of both microstructural and macrostructural properties.

where *q* is the heat transfer rate and ![StartFraction partial-differential normal upper T Over partial-differential zeta EndFraction](images/c05-i0001.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfrac><mrow><mi>&#8706;</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">T</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>&#8706;</mi><mi>&#950;</mi></mrow></mfrac></math> is the temperature gradient. In the context of multi‐scale thermal problems, Λ denotes the thermal conductivity constant of the material. For macroscale analysis, the heat conductivity is represented by a homogenized tensor derived from microscale properties. This effective heat conductivity tensor at the macroscale is calculated using Eq. [(5.3)](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0003), which integrates the microscale thermal characteristics to provide a representative value for larger‐scale thermal behavior.

### 5.2.3 Sensitivity Analysis and Optimization Method

Sensitivity analysis is a crucial component in the optimization process, instrumental in identifying the global optimal solution. For each iteration of the optimization algorithm, a first‐order sensitivity analysis is conducted using Eq. [(5.11)](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0011).

(5.11)![ModifyingAbove upper C With dot Subscript normal upper H Baseline equals ModifyingAbove bold upper P With dot Superscript upper T Baseline bold upper T plus bold upper P Superscript upper T Baseline ModifyingAbove bold upper T With dot](images/c05-math-0011.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <msub> <mover accent="true"><mi>C</mi> <mo>&#729;</mo> </mover> <mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi> </msub> <mo>=</mo> <msup> <mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="bold">P</mi> <mo>&#729;</mo> </mover> <mi>T</mi> </msup> <mi mathvariant="bold">T</mi> <mo>+</mo> <msup> <mi mathvariant="bold">P</mi> <mi>T</mi> </msup> <mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="bold">T</mi> <mo>&#729;</mo> </mover> </math> 

where ![ModifyingAbove bold upper T With dot](images/c05-i0004.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="bold">T</mi><mo>&#729;</mo></mover></math> can be obtained by differentiating Eq. [(5.4)](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0004) and arranging and separating the variables which gives

Now, substituting Eq. [(5.12)](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0012) into [(5.13)](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0013) gives

(5.13)![ModifyingAbove upper C With dot Subscript normal upper H Baseline equals 2 ModifyingAbove bold upper P With dot Superscript upper T Baseline bold upper T minus bold upper T Superscript upper T Baseline ModifyingAbove bold upper K With dot bold upper T](images/c05-math-0013.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <msub> <mover accent="true"><mi>C</mi> <mo>&#729;</mo> </mover> <mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi> </msub> <mo>=</mo> <mn>2</mn> <msup> <mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="bold">P</mi> <mo>&#729;</mo> </mover> <mi>T</mi> </msup> <mi mathvariant="bold">T</mi> <mo>&#8722;</mo> <msup> <mi mathvariant="bold">T</mi> <mi>T</mi> </msup> <mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="bold">K</mi> <mo>&#729;</mo> </mover> <mi mathvariant="bold">T</mi> </math> 

In the context of the current problem, a fixed thermal load condition is imposed on the design domain, rendering the problem independent of variable heat loads. This implies that the thermal response of the system is not influenced by fluctuations or changes in the applied heat load. Consequently, the design and optimization process can focus exclusively on material distribution and structural configuration to optimize thermal conductivity, without the need to account for varying thermal loads. This simplifies the analysis and allows for a more streamlined approach to achieving optimal thermal performance within the defined design domain. This will lead to eliminating the first term of Eq. [(5.13)](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0013), which reduces the first‐order sensitivity of the heat compliance to

The optimization process in this study employs the solid isotropic material with penalization (SIMP) method. To update design variables, the optimality criteria method (OCM) is utilized. To ensure the existence of solutions to the topology optimization problem and to prevent the occurrence of checkerboard patterns, a sensitivity filter is implemented. This filter modifies the sensitivities ![ModifyingAbove upper C With dot Subscript upper H Baseline left-parenthesis bold x Subscript upper M Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0006.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>C</mi><mo>&#729;</mo></mover><mi>H</mi></msub><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><msub><mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi><mi>M</mi></msub></mfenced></math> and ![ModifyingAbove upper C With dot Subscript upper H Baseline left-parenthesis bold x Subscript m Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0007.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>C</mi><mo>&#729;</mo></mover><mi>H</mi></msub><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><msub><mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi><mi>m</mi></msub></mfenced></math>, enhancing the robustness and reliability of the optimization results \[[15](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0015)–[17](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0017)\].

### 5.2.4 Numerical Investigations of Concurrent Multi‐ Versus Macroscale Optimization

The numerical investigation presented in this section focuses on comparing macroscale optimization with multi‐scale optimization approaches. Initially, we consider a macro design domain consisting of 200 × 200 elements along the x and y directions, with a specified volume fraction of 0.5, corresponding to a 50% reduction in material weight. A uniformly distributed heat load of 0.1 is applied across the entire area, while a heat sink is positioned in the middle of the left boundary on the upper left side (as illustrated in [Figure 5.1](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0001)(a)). The remaining outer boundaries are assumed to be adiabatic, thus preventing heat transfer across them.

![Figure 5.1 Design domain in (a) macro‐ and (b) microscale.](images/c05f001.png)

[**Figure 5.1**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0001) Design domain in (a) macro‐ and (b) microscale.

When optimizing for heat compliance at the macroscale level, the resulting design is depicted in [Figure 5.2](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0002)(a). This optimization, constrained by the initial 50% volume fraction, produces a structure that effectively balances material distribution with thermal performance. However, when the volume fraction is further reduced to 0.25, representing a 75% weight reduction, the final heat compliance significantly increases due to the excessive reduction in material, as shown in [Figure 5.2](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0002)(b). This outcome highlights the trade‐off between weight reduction and thermal efficiency when relying solely on macroscale optimization.

To address this challenge, we employ a concurrent optimization strategy that simultaneously optimizes both the macro‐ and microscales of the design domain. By integrating multi‐scale considerations, volume reduction is achieved across both scales, resulting in a design that maintains thermal performance despite significant material reduction. The microstructural optimization, illustrated in [Figure 5.1](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0001)(b), is destined to play an important role not only in weighing minimization but also in enhancing the overall thermal conductivity of the structure, compensating for the reduced material at the macroscale. This approach demonstrates the superior efficacy of multi‐scale optimization in achieving lightweight designs with optimized thermal properties, surpassing the limitations observed in single‐scale optimization.

Therefore, by assuming that each element in the design domain corresponds to a volume equivalent to that of the microscale, the multi‐scale optimization approach—achieving a 50% weight reduction at both the macro‐ and microscales—effectively results in a 75% total volume reduction. The results of this multi‐scale optimization, depicted in [Figure 5.2](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0002)(c), demonstrate superior performance in minimizing heat compliance compared to the equivalent 75% total volume reduction achieved solely through macroscale optimization.

![Figure 5.2 Optimization for (a) a 50% volume fraction at the macroscale, (b) a 25% volume reduction at the macroscale, and (c) a total 25% volume reduction across both the macro‐ and microscale.](images/c05f002.png)

[**Figure 5.2**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0002) Optimization for (a) a 50% volume fraction at the macroscale, (b) a 25% volume reduction at the macroscale, and (c) a total 25% volume reduction across both the macro‐ and microscale.

 

### 5.2.5 Numerical Investigation of Concurrent Multi‐scale Optimization with Predetermined Initial Design Domain

By introducing predetermined branched design domains for the problem discussed in [Section 5.2.4](#c05.xhtml#c05-sec-0006), we achieved a fin‐like structure in the final design (as illustrated in [Figure 5.3](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0003)). The performance of the design was significantly enhanced by optimizing the multi‐scale problem with an initial design domain that incorporated an increased number of branches. As demonstrated in [Figure 5.3](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0003)(b)–(d), this approach led to improved heat compliance minimization compared to the multi‐scale design problem presented in [Section 5.2.4](#c05.xhtml#c05-sec-0006).

![Figure 5.3 Optimization for (a) a fully distributed initial design domain, (b) a prescribed initial design domain with 5 branches, (c) a prescribed initial design domain with 9 branches, and (d) a prescribed initial design domain with 20 branches.](images/c05f003.png)

[**Figure 5.3**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0003) Optimization for (a) a fully distributed initial design domain, (b) a prescribed initial design domain with 5 branches, (c) a prescribed initial design domain with 9 branches, and (d) a prescribed initial design domain with 20 branches.

The introduction of initial branched structures allowed for more efficient heat dissipation pathways, resulting in a more effective thermal management solution. This improvement highlights the importance of initial design considerations in multi‐scale topology optimization for thermal problems \[[18](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0018), [19](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0019)\].

 

### 5.2.6 3D Concurrent Topology Optimization of Heat Dissipater

The 3D cases were optimized using the SIMP method, as illustrated in [Figure 5.4](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0004). In these examples, the design domains are uniformly heated at all nodes. For case (a), a heat sink is positioned at the center of the upper surface, while in case (b), it is located at the upper corner, both maintained at a temperature of 0°. The weight reduction for both the micro‐ and macroscales is set to 50%, with a uniform heat load of 0.01 applied to the macroscale nodes.

![Figure 5.4 The multi‐scale design domains of the 3D cases (a) Central point with zero temperature and internal heat generation, (b) Corner with zero temperature and internal heat generation, and (c) Two sides subjected to heat load with zero temperature boundary conditions \[20\].](images/c05f004.png)

[**Figure 5.4**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0004) The multi‐scale design domains of the 3D cases (a) Central point with zero temperature and internal heat generation, (b) Corner with zero temperature and internal heat generation, and (c) Two sides subjected to heat load with zero temperature boundary conditions [\[20\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0020).

The microscale design domain begins with an initial rectangular hole shape that extends through all surfaces. Structured hexahedral elements were employed for the finite element modeling of both the macro‐ and microscale domains. The microscale design domain utilized 8,000 hexahedral elements, while the macroscale domain used 144,000 elements. [Table 5.1](#c05.xhtml#c05-tbl-0001) presents the optimized topologies of the macro‐ and microstructures for the two cases depicted in [Figure 5.4](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0004).

It is important to note that 3D finite element analysis demands significantly higher computational resources compared to simpler two‐dimensional (2D) models. Consequently, a lower mesh resolution was used, resulting in voxelized designs, as shown in the first two columns of [Table 5.1](#c05.xhtml#c05-tbl-0001). Increasing the number of elements can enhance the precision and sharpness of the design but comes with a significant computational cost. To address this challenge and improve the visualization of 3D designs while maintaining low mesh resolution, we employed a volume imaging approach using the Isosurface technique [\[21\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0021). This method enables the creation of high‐quality design representations, as shown in the third and fourth columns of [Table 5.1](#c05.xhtml#c05-tbl-0001). The Isosurface technique effectively generates detailed images from voxelized data with minimal computational requirements, ensuring both accuracy and efficiency in representing spatial configurations \[[22](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0022), [23](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0023)\]. This technique is widely used in medical imaging [\[24\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0024) and fluid dynamics [\[25\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0025). The objective history of the case (a) is presented in [Figure 5.5](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0005).

To demonstrate the practicality of using Isosurface rendering for creating robust 3D‐printed models, we opted for a smaller and simpler model to optimize and manufacture. Cases (a) and (b) involved relatively large numbers of elements, which would significantly increase in size when meshed microstructures are introduced for each macrostructural element. Therefore, we selected a smaller problem, with 150 elements in the macroscale domain, where heat loading is applied to the lower side and a heat sink is placed at the central part of the upper side (as depicted in [Figure 5.4](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0004)(c)).

The multi‐scale optimization was performed using a microstructure design domain similar to those in cases (a) and (b). The resulting designs for both the macro‐ and microscales are presented in [Table 5.1](#c05.xhtml#c05-tbl-0001), case (c). Using Isosurface rendering, the 3D model of the multi‐scale design was converted into STereoLithography (STL) format for 3D printing (as illustrated in [Figure 5.6](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0006)).

To validate the feasibility of manufacturing the refined model, we fabricated the design illustrated in [Figure 5.6](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0006). Acrylonitrile butadiene styrene (ABS) was utilized as the material. The preparation and printing of the model were carried out using a Stratasys F170 3D printer, as shown in [Figure 5.7](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0007).

 

### 5.2.7 Numerical Investigation of the Effect of Microstructure Dependency on Multi‐scale Design

![Table 5.1  The outcomes of concurrent multi‐scale topology optimization for 3D cases, including both voided and enhanced designs \[20\].](images/c05t001a.png)

[**Table 5.1**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-tbl-0001)  The outcomes of concurrent multi‐scale topology optimization for 3D cases, including both voided and enhanced designs [\[20\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0020).

![Figure 5.5 Iteration histories of the normalized heat compliance of 3D case (a).](images/c05f005.png)

[**Figure 5.5**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0005) Iteration histories of the normalized heat compliance of 3D case (a).

![Figure 5.6 The final multi‐scale design of case (c) \[20\].](images/c05f006.png)

[**Figure 5.6**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0006) The final multi‐scale design of case (c) [\[20\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0020).

![Figure 5.7 The ABS‐printed specimen of the 3D multi‐scale optimized case (c).](images/c05f007.png)

[**Figure 5.7**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0007) The ABS‐printed specimen of the 3D multi‐scale optimized case (c).

This section explores the complex relationship between macroscopic and microscopic design domains and their influence on initial microstructure through a concurrent multi‐scale optimization framework. Utilizing topology optimization with the SIMP method, we aimed to reduce the solid design domain's weight by 25% through equal 50% reductions in both solid structure and microstructure weights. The numerical model, depicted in [Figure 5.7](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0007), features a 150 mm × 150 mm macro design domain discretized into 1 mm² elements, with an initial design value of X[*M*] = 0.5 to meet the SIMP criteria. The micro design domain, measuring 1 mm × 1 mm, employs periodic boundary conditions for numerical homogenization and is discretized into 104 elements for effective microscale analysis and optimization. This approach allows for a comprehensive investigation of the intricate interplay between scales in the optimization process. Three distinct examples of the initial micro design domain have been investigated. The first example within our study introduces a central circular sub‐design domain Ω[2] characterized by an initial value of 0.5, while the remainder of the micro design domain Ω[1] maintains a consistent initial value of 1.

This variation in the central sub‐design domain, as opposed to the surrounding areas, serves multiple scientific purposes. Primarily, this differentiation is employed to encourage achieving optimal design outcomes through the optimality criteria, all while significantly reducing computational cost [\[26\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0026). Additionally, it aids in the minimization of grayscale regions within the microcell \[[20](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0020), [27](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0027)\]. The investigation expands to include two additional microscale configurations, each featuring distinct central subdomains. The second system incorporates a square subdomain, while the third employs a subdomain of unspecified shape, both with side lengths of 50 elements. These designs are purposefully selected to examine how different geometric forms affect microstructural optimization. Importantly, the initial values assigned to these subdomains are kept consistent with those used in the previously studied circular subdomain case.

This approach, exploring various subdomain shapes while maintaining consistent initial conditions, enables a comprehensive analysis of how both geometry and starting parameters influence the optimization process. The study aimed to shed light on the intricate interplay between subdomain configuration and initial settings in shaping the outcomes of microstructural optimization.

We have selected initial design values of Ω[1] = 1 and Ω[2] = 0.5 for the purpose of conducting multi‐scale optimization across three distinct initial microstructures, as depicted in [Figure 5.8](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0008)(a)–(d). The analysis, summarized in [Table 5.2](#c05.xhtml#c05-tbl-0002), reveals varying outcomes across different microstructures in multi‐scale topology optimization for heat compliance reduction. The square sub‐design domain proved to be the least effective in this regard. In contrast, the circular sub‐design domain demonstrated a notable 22% enhancement in performance. The most promising results were obtained with the rhombus sub‐design domain, which achieved a remarkable 25% decrease in heat compliance compared to the square configuration. This significant improvement, illustrated in [Figure 5.9](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0009), underscores the rhombus domain's superior efficacy in this particular optimization scenario. These findings highlight the crucial role that sub‐design domain geometry plays in determining the effectiveness of multi‐scale topology optimization for heat compliance minimization.

![Table 5.2  A comparative analysis of the performance of different microstructure sub‐design domains in multi‐scale topology optimization aimed at minimizing heat compliance.](images/c05t002a.png)

[**Table 5.2**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-tbl-0002)  A comparative analysis of the performance of different microstructure sub‐design domains in multi‐scale topology optimization aimed at minimizing heat compliance.

![Figure 5.8 Simultaneous optimization of design domains: (a) a square macro design with a left‐central heat sink and (b–d) unit cell design domains featuring central circular, square, and rhombus sub‐designs, respectively.](images/c05f008.png)

[**Figure 5.8**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0008) Simultaneous optimization of design domains: (a) a square macro design with a left‐central heat sink and (b–d) unit cell design domains featuring central circular, square, and rhombus sub‐designs, respectively.

![Figure 5.9 The influence of microstructure design on the performance of heat compliance optimization.](images/c05f009.png)

[**Figure 5.9**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0009) The influence of microstructure design on the performance of heat compliance optimization.

The data in Table offer insights into how the initial microscale central sub‐design domain affects concurrent multi‐scale optimization, especially regarding the enhancement of heat conductivity. To gain an in‐depth understanding of the role of subdomains at the microscale in the optimization process, we conducted a detailed analysis using a rhombus‐shaped central sub‐design domain as a case study. This investigation involved varying the parameters of the sub‐design domain and categorizing the resulting configurations into four distinct groups. The first group consists of a central rhombus‐shaped sub‐design domain, as depicted in [Figure 5.8](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0008)(d), with the corresponding results summarized in [Table 5.3](#c05.xhtml#c05-tbl-0003).

 

### 5.2.8 Multiphysics Optimization Aimed at Maximizing Heat Conductivity and Mechanical Stiffness

In real‐world applications, the design problem must satisfy multiple physical aspects. These problems are referred to as multiphysics problems. The constituent physical aspects have their own formulations and necessitate distinct discretization to satisfy these formulations and associated conditions. The involved physics may exhibit complementary relationships or may be distinct and separate. In this section, the multiphysics problem of designing a high‐stiffness heat dissipator is addressed.

![Table 5.3  Effect of sub‐design domain configurations on heat conductivity maximization––central rhombus sub‐design domain.](images/c05t003a.png)

[**Table 5.3**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-tbl-0003)  Effect of sub‐design domain configurations on heat conductivity maximization––central rhombus sub‐design domain.

In order to maximize heat conductivity, heat compliance *C*[*H*] is minimized. Using the same analogy, mechanical stiffness maximization is achieved by minimizing mechanical compliance *C*[*mech*]. The generalized form of the main mechanical objective function takes the form of Eq. [(5.22):](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0022)

</msub> <mo>&#8804;</mo> <msub> <mi>v</mi> <mi>M</mi> </msub> <mo>,</mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo>&#60;</mo> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mi>M</mi> </msub> <mo>&#60;</mo> <mn>1</mn> <mspace width="0.84em"/> <mo>&#8704;</mo> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mi>M</mi> </msub> <mo>&#8712;</mo> <msub> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#937;</mi> <mi mathvariant="italic">dM</mi> </msub> </mtd> </mtr> <mtr> <mtd></mtd> <mtd> <mspace width="1.0em"/> <munder> <mo>&#8747;</mo> <msub> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#937;</mi> <mi mathvariant="italic">dm</mi> </msub> </munder> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mi>m</mi> </msub> <mi>d</mi> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mi>m</mi> </msub> <mo>&#8804;</mo> <msub> <mi>v</mi> <mi>m</mi> </msub> <mo>,</mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo>&#60;</mo> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mi>m</mi> </msub> <mo>&#60;</mo> <mn>1</mn> <mspace width="0.84em"/> <mo>&#8704;</mo> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mi>m</mi> </msub> <mo>&#8712;</mo> <msub> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#937;</mi> <mi mathvariant="italic">dm</mi> </msub> </mtd> </mtr> </mtable> </math> 

Topology optimization requires sensitivity analysis capable of addressing the multiphysics aspects of the problem to guide the search algorithm toward identifying the extremum point. For the function presented in Eq. [(5.24)](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0024), given that the denominator remains constant with respect to the objective function, the sensitivity of the function can be expressed as

(5.25)![d f overbar equals StartFraction w 1 italic d f Subscript c Baseline Over f Subscript c Baseline Superscript italic i n i Baseline minus f Subscript c Baseline Superscript asterisk Baseline EndFraction plus StartFraction w 2 italic d f Subscript kappa Baseline Over f Subscript kappa Baseline Superscript italic i n i Baseline minus f Subscript kappa Baseline Superscript asterisk Baseline EndFraction](images/c05-math-0025.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <mi>d</mi> <mover accent="true"><mi>f</mi> <mo stretchy="true">&#175;</mo> </mover> <mo>=</mo> <mfrac> <mrow> <msub> <mi>w</mi> <mn>1</mn> </msub> <msub> <mi mathvariant="italic">df</mi> <mi>c</mi> </msub> </mrow> <mrow> <msup> <msub> <mi>f</mi> <mi>c</mi> </msub> <mi mathvariant="italic">ini</mi> </msup> <mo>&#8722;</mo> <msup> <msub> <mi>f</mi> <mi>c</mi> </msub> <mo>&#42;</mo> </msup> </mrow> </mfrac> <mo>+</mo> <mfrac> <mrow> <msub> <mi>w</mi> <mn>2</mn> </msub> <msub> <mi mathvariant="italic">df</mi> <mi>&#954;</mi> </msub> </mrow> <mrow> <msup> <msub> <mi>f</mi> <mi>&#954;</mi> </msub> <mi mathvariant="italic">ini</mi> </msup> <mo>&#8722;</mo> <msup> <msub> <mi>f</mi> <mi>&#954;</mi> </msub> <mo>&#42;</mo> </msup> </mrow> </mfrac> </math> 

As presented in Eq. [(5.25)](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0025), the objective function depends on the macro and micro design variables, so the sensitivity has to be driven with respect to the variational densities (**x**[*M*], **x**[*m*]). In the case of mechanical compliance, the sensitivity takes the form of

(5.26)![ModifyingAbove upper C With dot Subscript italic mech Baseline left-parenthesis bold x Subscript upper M Baseline comma bold x Subscript m Baseline right-parenthesis equals ModifyingAbove bold upper F With dot Subscript upper M Superscript upper T Baseline bold upper U Subscript upper M Baseline plus bold upper F Subscript upper M Superscript upper T Baseline ModifyingAbove bold upper U With dot](images/c05-math-0026.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <msub> <mover accent="true"><mi>C</mi> <mo>&#729;</mo> </mover> <mtext mathvariant="italic">mech</mtext> </msub> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mi>M</mi> </msub> <mo>,</mo> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mi>m</mi> </msub> </mrow> </mfenced> <mo>=</mo> <msubsup> <mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="bold">F</mi> <mo>&#729;</mo> </mover> <mi>M</mi> <mi>T</mi> </msubsup> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">U</mi> <mi>M</mi> </msub> <mo>+</mo> <msubsup> <mi mathvariant="bold">F</mi> <mi>M</mi> <mi>T</mi> </msubsup> <mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="bold">U</mi> <mo>&#729;</mo> </mover> </math> 

![ModifyingAbove bold upper U With dot](images/c05-i0013.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="bold">U</mi><mo>&#729;</mo></mover></math> is obtained by differentiating (**K**(**x**[*M*], **x**[*m*])**U** = **F**) and separating the variables which gives

The second part (i.e., the heat compliance) is evaluated with respect to **x**[*m*] as is given in Eq. [(5.17)](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0017).

The derivative of the homogenized material's thermal conductivity tensor with respect to the micro design variable ![StartFraction d bold upper Lamda Superscript normal upper H Baseline left-parenthesis bold x Subscript m Baseline right-parenthesis Over d bold x Subscript m Baseline EndFraction](images/c05-i0016.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfrac><mrow><mi>d</mi><msup><mi mathvariant="bold">&#923;</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi></msup><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><msub><mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi><mi>m</mi></msub></mfenced></mrow><mrow><mi>d</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi><mi>m</mi></msub></mrow></mfrac></math> has also been derived previously in Eq. [(5.18)](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0018).

The optimization process can be summarized in the following steps:

1.  **Step 1:** Specify the design domain parameters for both the macro‐ and microscale. The input parameters include the discretization resolution (number of elements along the x and y axes), the desired volume fraction, and the material properties of the base materials used for developing the microstructure design in conjunction with the macrostructure design.
2.  **Step 2:** The design domain and finite element analysis are structured to ensure the creation of a specialized multi‐scale finite element model tailored to each physical phenomenon within the domain. In this approach, a single physical model treats every element of the macro‐scale design domain as a unit cell, which is further discretized using a microscale framework. By employing the homogenization method, the effective macroscopic properties of the RVE are determined, accounting for the distinct and well‐defined phases of the materials at the microscale [\[28\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0028). In this context, robustness refers to ensuring the ergodicity of the mechanical properties of the selected RVE for the macrostructure. If the RVE size is too small, it may exclude certain material phases from consideration, compromising the accuracy of the representation [\[29\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0029). Therefore, the size of the RVE relative to its sub‐constructive phases must be sufficiently large to guarantee numerical stability when applying periodic boundary conditions and to accurately represent the effective properties of the selected macrostructure [\[30\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0030).
3.  **Step 3:** Calculate the initial design values for mechanical and thermal compliances (*f*[*c*]^(*ini*) and *f*[*κ*]^(*ini*), respectively) is essential as a preliminary step in constructing the scalarized form of the normalized multi‐objective function.
4.  **Step 4:** Calculate the optimized results for each physical model to identify the values of *f*[*c*]^(\*) and *f*[*κ*]^(\*).
5.  **Step 5:** Calculate the objective function of the macro‐ and microscale ([Eq. 5.24](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0024)). Because the summation of the weights is 1, the weighing can be written in terms of a general weight (*η*). As such, the weights are rewritten as *w*[1] = *η* and *w*[2] = (1 − *η*).
6.  **Step 6:** Perform the sensitivity analysis and evaluate the design domains with the optimality criteria, and update the design domain.
7.  **Step 7:** Assess the design variables with respect to the constraint functions to ensure compliance with the weight minimization criteria for both the macro‐ and micro design variables.
8.  **Step 8:** Establish the design domain by assigning the macro design variables **x**[*M*] as 1 for solid elements and associate it with micro design variables **x**[*m*] that have the value of 1 for solids and 0 for the voids of the microscale design domain. As a result, the layout of the macrostructure and the microstructure is updated simultaneously. This will construe into a porous macrostructure with the highest possible heat conductivity and high stiffness with high weight reduction.
9.  **Step 9:** Iterate through steps 5–8 until the structural layout meets the specified constraints.

The overall algorithm for concurrent multi‐scale and multiphysics topology optimization, aimed at maximizing heat conductivity and mechanical stiffness, is depicted in [Figure 5.10](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0010).

![Figure 5.10 Flowchart of concurrent multi‐scale heat and stiffness optimization \[31\].](images/c05f010.png)

[**Figure 5.10**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0010) Flowchart of concurrent multi‐scale heat and stiffness optimization [\[31\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0031).

The first model features a rectangular domain comprising 200 elements along the *x*‐axis and 90 elements along the *y*‐axis. It incorporates a periodic square microstructure with an edge length of 100 elements. Both share a volume fraction reduction of 50%. In the mechanical model, the left edge is fixed, and a downward load is applied at a point located in the third quarter of the right edge, measured from the top (refer to [Figure 5.11](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0011)(a)). For the heat conduction model, the temperature at the center of the left edge is set to zero ([Figure 5.11](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0011)(b)), while all other boundaries are treated as adiabatic, ensuring no heat transfer, following the Bejan method \[[19](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0019), [32](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0032), [33](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0033)\]. Heat generation was set to be constant and homogeneous throughout the domain of 0.01, which is represented in a finite element, another discretization with the same size as for the mechanical model (i.e., with 200 × 90 elements in the x and y directions, respectively, for macroscale and 100 × 100 elements for the microscale with periodic boundary conditions to comply with the numerical homogenization evaluation scheme). Regarding the initial micro design domain, it is set to have the value of 0.5 except the central circular domain of the radius equal to 50 elements. This central sub‐design domain was set to have a quarter value of the initial value than for the other design domains. This is to encourage achieving a design by the optimality criteria with less computational cost [\[26\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0026) as well as minimizing the grayscale area of the microcell [\[27\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0027). The optimization results for the concurrent multi‐scale dimension are shown in [Figure 5.12](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0012). The influence of the trade‐off of the multi‐objective multi‐scale problem is shown in the change of the macro‐ and microstructures. The microstructure is trying to adapt to the stronger physics while balancing the micro design variables with the overall objective of decreasing the value of the general weighing factor *η*. The Pareto front shows that the best result (knee point) for the first example is for the weighing factor (*η* = 0.6). The history of the multi‐objective function is shown in Figures [5.12](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0012) and [5.13](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0013).

![Figure 5.11 The spatial layout of numerical examples: (a) mechanical loading of a rectangular cantilever beam, (b) heat boundary conditions and loading of a rectangular cantilever beam, (c) mechanical loading of a short beam, and (d) heat loading of a short beam \[31\].](images/c05f011.png)

[**Figure 5.11**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0011) The spatial layout of numerical examples: (a) mechanical loading of a rectangular cantilever beam, (b) heat boundary conditions and loading of a rectangular cantilever beam, (c) mechanical loading of a short beam, and (d) heat loading of a short beam [\[31\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0031).

![Figure 5.12 Pareto front of the concurrent multiphysics optimization of example 1 \[31\].](images/c05f012.png)

[**Figure 5.12**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0012) Pareto front of the concurrent multiphysics optimization of example 1 [\[31\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0031).

The second example's mechanical model ([Figure 5.11](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0011)(c)) features a square macro design domain with a side length of 150 elements. The left side is fixed, and a load is applied at the center of the right edge, directed along the positive y‐axis. The solid heat conduction model retains properties similar to the first example but differs in the placement of the heat sink ([Figure 5.11](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0011)(d)).

![Figure 5.13 The multi‐objective history of the concurrent optimization for example 1 with η = 0.6 \[31\].](images/c05f013.png)

[**Figure 5.13**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0013) The multi‐objective history of the concurrent optimization for example 1 with *η* = 0.6 [\[31\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0031).

![Figure 5.14 Pareto front of the concurrent multiphysics optimization of the second example \[31\].](images/c05f014.png)

[**Figure 5.14**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0014) Pareto front of the concurrent multiphysics optimization of the second example [\[31\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0031).

The Pareto front for macro‐ and microscale optimization is illustrated in [Figure 5.14](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0014). The transition in macrostructure layouts between the two cases is notably reflected in the microscale design, particularly in case 2. This transition directly corresponds to the combined effects of the physical models. Material distribution changes in response to the boundary conditions of each physical model. In the first example ([Figure 5.11](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0011)(a) and (b)), the boundary conditions guide the macro design to extend toward the middle of the left side, except in the mechanical model, where it shifts slightly downward. The microscale design adapts by distributing material diagonally for the heat conduction model, with a concentrated mass at the center to facilitate thermal energy transfer within the microstructure.

For the mechanical strain energy model, the microscale layout distributes energy along the edges and diagonally, forming a branching pattern. This includes a triangular truss arrangement around the central circular void of the microstructure. [Table 5.4](#c05.xhtml#c05-tbl-0004) summarizes the optimal results of the multi‐scale optimization for both examples.

![Table 5.4 The best results of multi‐scale optimization on the Pareto front of the multi‐objective function \[31\].](images/c05t004a.png)

[**Table 5.4**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-tbl-0004) The best results of multi‐scale optimization on the Pareto front of the multi‐objective function [\[31\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0031).

 

## 5.3 APPLICATION OF ROBOTICS

Robotics are increasingly employed in a wide range of applications, encompassing various forms and categories. These include welding robots in automotive manufacturing plants, automated domestic vacuum cleaners, and even assistive systems in modern vehicles, such as steering assistance and auto‐parking technologies.

Robotics leverages compliant mechanisms to achieve high precision and reliability by utilizing elastic deformation for motion and force transmission. Unlike traditional mechanisms with joints and bearings, compliant mechanisms minimize friction, backlash, and wear, enabling smooth and repeatable movements. This makes them ideal for applications requiring micrometer‐level accuracy, such as surgical robotics, precision assembly, and soft robotics. By integrating flexibility and structural efficiency, compliant mechanisms enhance the performance and versatility of robotic systems, driving innovation in high‐precision tasks.

A common necessity for all robotic systems is minimizing energy consumption, which can be achieved through several approaches. One of the primary strategies is weight reduction. Designing porous robotic structures facilitates the creation of lightweight robots, significantly contributing to energy efficiency. Furthermore, advancements in 3D‐printing technology enable the efficient and precise fabrication of porous robotic structures, making this approach accessible for both enthusiasts and industrial‐scale manufacturing. This section introduces several applications aimed at achieving porous robotics. These applications are discussed in detail, with a focus on optimization and simulation techniques.

 

### 5.3.1 Porous Soft Robotic Design with Hybrid Topology Optimization

Soft robotics has been the subject of strong interest in the last few years, both from researchers and industry. Roboticist is considered a potentially whole new class of machines that perform better in the real world and are more versatile \[[34](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0034)–[37](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0037)\]. Soft robotics aims to develop robots that are flexible and compliant, mimicking the properties of biological organisms. Similar to natural tissues, the primary structure of a soft robot distributes energy to perform actions, deforming in response to external forces rather than relying solely on rigid linkages and gears as in conventional robotics. In essence, soft robotics seeks to extend the field beyond the constraints of dynamic rigid bodies.

This study focuses on designing an ultra‐lightweight, stiff, soft robotic gripping hand capable of achieving precise gripping with a single actuation point. The approach involves two key strategies: first, incorporating a structural design that redistributes strain energy to achieve controlled, precise displacements; second, employing a porous structure to minimize weight while maintaining superior performance. Additionally, the pores are optimized to maximize material properties, such as resistance to axial and shear stresses.

To achieve this design, the study employs non‐parametric optimization, specifically topology optimization, which is one of the most effective methods for generating optimal designs that meet user‐defined objectives. This technique involves discretizing the design space and applying conventional optimization algorithms to determine the optimal topology of the structure. For static loading scenarios, the optimization process requires defining load steps, design variables, responses, constraints, and objectives to solve the problem effectively \[[4](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0004), [5](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0005), [7](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0007), [8](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0008)\]. Associated with additive manufacturing, topologically optimized structures have shown robust design and good performance \[[38](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0038)–[40](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0040)\]. Topology optimization has revolutionized the design of lightweight and highly functional structures. By optimizing the distribution of materials within a given design domain, this approach maximizes the structural performance‐to‐weight ratio while meeting various design constraints. One of the pioneering methods in continuum topology optimization for creating compliant systems is the homogenization approach. This technique simplifies complex structural optimization problems by reformulating them as efficient multi‐scale optimization tasks, using a material density function for each discretized element. These elements are conceptualized as containing an infinite number of randomly distributed voids.

Homogenization theory plays a critical role in determining the mechanical properties of such materials. There are two primary strategies for incorporating microstructures: rank laminate composites and hollow microcell‐based designs. In the case of rank laminate composites, the homogenization equations can often be solved analytically. Conversely, for hollow microcells, numerical methods are typically employed to solve the homogenization problem. With the homogenization method, theoretical structural performance can be mathematically constrained [\[41\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0041). Ananthasuresh et al [\[42\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0042) extended the homogenization to perform compliant mechanism designs. However, because the resulting mechanisms are not flexible enough, the results appear to be a mean compliance design rather than a compliant mechanism design. As a result, Nishiwaki et al. [\[43\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0043) developed a homogenization‐based topology optimization method for the design of compliant mechanisms that includes flexibility. In their method and in order to effectively describe flexibility, the creation of a multi‐objective function using mutual mean compliance was used. The SIMP method has been used to design compliant mechanisms as a direct descendant of the homogenization method \[[44](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0044)–[47](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0047)\]. Additionally, the evolutionary structural optimization (ESO) method was developed on the straightforward tenet of gradually removing wasteful material from a structure in order to get the optimum structure conceivable. The fundamental tenet of ESO is the elimination of the so‐called inefficient material directly from the building in order to create the best design, and it was first introduced by Min and Steven [\[48\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0048). The cost function sensitivity is used to update the decision variables \[[20](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0020), [49](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0049)\]. Updates are based on the element sensitivity number that is generated by differentiating the objective function so that the elemental sensitivity and zero are identical for solid and soft elements, respectively. And as for the SIMP method, the ESO method was investigated for designing compliant mechanisms \[[50](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0050)–[53](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0053)\]. Due to the challenges in creating robust designs, the multi‐scale compliant mechanism has received little attention from researchers. In addition, due to the fluctuating effective properties of the grayscale elements at the start of the optimization process, the grayscale nature of such a problem, when it is optimized using the SIMP method, significantly restricts attaining extrema. The research of Sivapuram et al. [\[54\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0054) suggested an improved compliant mechanism optimization using the level‐set method. The binary element idea in the zero‐level‐set method was shown to be effective for robust soft‐compliant mechanism design.

 

#### 5.3.1.1 The Mathematical Modeling of Concurrent Multi‐scale Hybrid Topology Optimization

In order to simultaneously optimize the objective function on both the macro‐**x**[M] and microscales **x**[m], concurrent multi‐scale topology optimization was carried out. Two distinct finite element systems are used to discretize the macro‐ and microscale design domains. For both systems in this paper, we used a bilinear structured mesh. According to Eq. [(5.34)](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0034), when it is equal to 1, the corresponding element is a solid, while when it is zero, the element represents a void.

(5.34)![bold x Subscript upper M Baseline comma bold x Subscript m Baseline equals StartLayout Enlarged left-brace 1st Row 1 solid material 2nd Row 0 Void EndLayout](images/c05-math-0034.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mi>M</mi> </msub> <mo>,</mo> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mi>m</mi> </msub> <mo>=</mo> <mfenced open="{" close="" separators=""><mtable> <mtr> <mtd> <mn>1</mn> <mspace width="2.04em"/> <mtext>solid&#8201;&#8201;material</mtext> </mtd> </mtr> <mtr> <mtd> <mn>0</mn> <mspace width="2.04em"/> <mtext>Void</mtext> </mtd> </mtr> </mtable> </mfenced> </math> 

The homogenization approach is essential for the concurrent design of multi‐scale problems for two primary reasons. First, it enables the determination of the effective properties of the macrostructure. Second, it allows the microscale objective function to employ inverse homogenization, facilitating the simultaneous design of both the macrostructure and microstructure. This process begins with an investigation to evaluate the effective elastic tensor, based on the assumption of Hooke's law.

where **E** is the element's elastic tensor and **B** is the strain displacement matrix. The elastic modulus of the microstructure **E**[*ijqp*] that consists of void and solid materials is connected to the elastic tensor of the based solid material **E**[0] as follows:

The effective elastic tensor of the microstructure **E**^H that is calculated by implementing Eq [(5.36)](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0036) is used to make the macroscale of the elemental elastic tensor **E**[*macro*] with a similar material interpolation scheme but with the difference of adopting the relaxed penalization of the macro design variables. The relaxation and penalization principles are utilized in SIMP in order to solve many problems that faced its early version, such as the non‐existence and grayscale \[[56](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0056), [57](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0057)\]. However, these modifications are serious limitations for attaining design with global extrema of the traditional SIMP method \[[14](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0014), [58](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0058), [59](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0059)\]. This issue will be discussed later in the discussion section, so returning to the formulation part of the research, the elastic tensor **E**[*macro*] is formulated by penalizing the macro design variable **x**[*M*] to power ![left-parenthesis p greater-than-or-equal-to max left-bracket StartFraction 2 Over 1 minus upsilon EndFraction comma StartFraction 4 Over 1 plus upsilon EndFraction right-bracket right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0024.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mi>p</mi><mo>&#8805;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">max</mi><mfenced open="\[" close="\]" separators=""><mrow><mfrac><mn>2</mn><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>&#8722;</mo><mi>&#965;</mi></mrow></mfrac><mo>,</mo><mfrac><mn>4</mn><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>&#965;</mi></mrow></mfrac></mrow></mfenced></mfenced></math> [\[60\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0060). Here, *υ* is the Poisson ratio of the solid materials. In this research, *p* is chosen to be 3. The elastic modulus of the macrostructure takes the final form of

where *α* is an infinitesimal value. By assuming that the actuator is subject to linear strain limits, a spring of stiffness K[in], and a force F[in] at the input point A, the generalized model of the mechanically activated compliant mechanism problem is linearly implemented. As shown in [Figure 5.15](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0015), the goal is to maximize the displacement at the output point B.

[**Figure 5.15**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0015) The design challenge of the soft robotic gripper mechanism: (a) static response and (b) dynamic response.

The goal is to attain a porous ultra‐lightweight soft gripper while maximizing the porous robustness to withstand the different loading conditions. As such, two cases are investigated for maximizing the bulk modulus, of the microstructure. Therefore, the optimization is also addressing the maximization of **E**^H. The concurrent multi‐scale optimization algorithm will be

Here, *N*[*M*] and *N*[*m*] stand for the macro‐ and microscale structures' corresponding element numbers, respectively. *v*[*M*] and *v*[*m*] are the volume fractions of the design variables **x**[*M*] and **x**[*m*] within the macro and micro design domains (Ω[*dM*] and Ω[*dm*], respectively).

 

#### 5.3.1.2 Sensitivity Analysis and Optimization Method

The sensitivity analysis is given in [Eq. 5.44](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0044), taking into account that the mechanical loading vector **F** is design‐independent in our linear analysis.

mathvariant="normal">&#937;</mi> <mi>m</mi> </msub> </math> 

In this work, the macrostructure is optimized using the SIMP approach, while the microstructure is optimized with the ESO method. This hybrid optimization approach enables the generation of robust and clear design concepts, significantly reducing computational costs. The OCM is also employed to update the design variables \[[5](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0005), [62](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0062)\]. To ensure the existence of solutions to the topology optimization problem and to prevent the emergence of checkerboard patterns, a sensitivity filter is applied.

 

#### 5.3.1.3 Numerical Examples and Discussion

In this section, we will investigate several examples of soft grippers. The first model has 80 and 80 mm in the x and y directions (as shown in [Figure 5.16](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0016) (a)). The microstructure has 100 × 100 elements in the x and y directions. The volume fraction condition for the microscale is 0.3 and 0.4 for the macroscale. For all cases, the elastic tensor for solid material (**E**[0]) is given in Eq [(5.47)](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0047):

(5.47)![bold upper E 0 equals Start 3 By 3 Matrix 1st Row 1st Column 3 2nd Column 1 3rd Column 0 2nd Row 1st Column 1 2nd Column 3 3rd Column 0 3rd Row 1st Column 0 2nd Column 0 3rd Column 1 EndMatrix](images/c05-math-0047.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">E</mi> <mn>0</mn> </msub> <mo>=</mo> <mfenced open="\[" close="\]" separators=""><mtable> <mtr> <mtd> <mn>3</mn> </mtd> <mtd> <mn>1</mn> </mtd> <mtd> <mn>0</mn> </mtd> </mtr> <mtr> <mtd> <mn>1</mn> </mtd> <mtd> <mn>3</mn> </mtd> <mtd> <mn>0</mn> </mtd> </mtr> <mtr> <mtd> <mn>0</mn> </mtd> <mtd> <mn>0</mn> </mtd> <mtd> <mn>1</mn> </mtd> </mtr> </mtable> </mfenced> </math> 

The objective is maximizing the bulk modulus [\[63\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0063) of the microscale (![bold upper E 11 Superscript normal upper H Baseline plus bold upper E 22 Superscript normal upper H](images/c05-i0027.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msubsup><mi mathvariant="bold">E</mi><mn>11</mn><mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi></msubsup><mo>+</mo><msubsup><mi mathvariant="bold">E</mi><mn>22</mn><mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi></msubsup></math> for 2D case). The microscale design is shown in [Figure 5.16](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0016)(b) and (c). The macroscale design is shown in [Figure 5.16](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0016)(d).

![Figure 5.16 A case study on concurrent multi‐scale hybrid design for soft gripper optimization (a) The initial problem, (b) a single microscale design, (c) a 4 × 4 microscale design, and (d) the macroscale design.](images/c05f016.png)

[**Figure 5.16**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0016) A case study on concurrent multi‐scale hybrid design for soft gripper optimization (a) The initial problem, (b) a single microscale design, (c) a 4 × 4 microscale design, and (d) the macroscale design.

![Figure 5.17 The number of iterations for (a) the hybrid topology optimization and (b) the SIMP method.](images/c05f017.png)

[**Figure 5.17**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0017) The number of iterations for (a) the hybrid topology optimization and (b) the SIMP method.

To assess the computational cost of the proposed hybrid (SIMP‐ESO) topology optimization method in comparison to the SIMP method, we performed multi‐scale concurrent SIMP topology optimization for the same given example (i.e., [Figure 5.16](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0016)(d)). The number of iterations for both the SIMP and hybrid (SIMP‐ESO) methods is presented in [Figure 5.17](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0017). The results indicate that the hybrid method achieves a robust design in a shorter time, with the hybrid topology optimization method being nearly three times faster than the SIMP method.

The output displacement (**U**[*out*]) of our hybrid multi‐scale concurrent topology optimization (as shown in [Figure 5.18](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0018)(a)), also showed better results (1.2 times better) than using the SIMP method alone (as shown in [Figure 5.18](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0018)(b)).

It is important to note that Bendsoe and Sigmund [\[64\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0064), after conducting a series of investigations with discrete optimality criteria optimization (DOCO) and the SIMP method, concluded that modeling‐compliant mechanisms using linear analysis—the approach used in this research for all cases—often results in poor or ineffective designs. They further emphasized that, in the best‐case scenario, linear analysis provides erroneous results, while in the worst‐case scenario, the outcomes are completely unsuitable for designing a compliant mechanism. As noted by Bendsoe and Sigmund, designing compliant mechanisms using topology optimization requires the use of geometrically non‐linear finite element modeling [\[64\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0064). The numerical examples presented by Bendsoe and Sigmund were developed with a simple gradient descent algorithm in mind, specifically the OCM. One limitation of the OCM is its tendency to converge to local extrema, which necessitates careful selection of the gradient descent parameters to avoid this issue \[[14](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0014), [20](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0020)\]. Later, several researchers made use of the method of moving asymptotes (MMAs) \[[65](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0065), [66](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0066)\]. This is a versatile algorithm capable of handling various types of optimization problems. It relies on a convex approximation suitable for topology optimization; however, the effectiveness of the method is significantly influenced by the asymptote and move limitations [\[67\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0067). Due to their inherent characteristics, the OCM and MMA methods are single‐point search algorithms, making them prone to falling into local minima [\[7\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0007), particularly as the penalization power of the SIMP method increases. These limitations highlight the need for further exploration of the role of non‐linear analysis. Through extensive investigations, our research confirms the first part of Bendsoe and Sigmund's statement, showing that linearized modeling aimed at maximizing displacement as an objective function for designing compliant mechanisms can lead to questionable results. Additionally, prior studies have successfully applied non‐linear finite element modeling to the design of a piezoelectric gripper [\[7\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0007). This supports the second part of the statement by Bendsoe and Sigmund. However, in this study, we demonstrate a robust design with well‐formed hinges ([Figure 5.18](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0018)(a)) in the case of hybrid topology optimization. From the results, we conclude that the issue of not achieving optimal hinge formation in compliant mechanism design is not due to linear analysis. Furthermore, we extend our observation to non‐linear analysis, where the consideration of non‐linearity mitigates the significant changes in design variables caused by penalization, and the mesh‐independent filters are less severe. The Young's modulus of elasticity for the SIMP method is a third‐order variation, which influences in this case, badly influencing a higher nodal displacement value within the context of linear elastic finite element analysis, particularly when using bilinear elements. In this approach, rigid elements (where the design variables are set to unity) tend to concentrate around the hinges, which are expected to be present. However, in non‐linear analysis, iterative methods are highly sensitive to coupled analysis, causing small fields (due to the low values of penalized design variables) to become more pronounced. In contrast, our hybrid multi‐scale topology optimization begins with high values for the elastic tensors, which are then gradually reduced across all elements. This approach places the macro design variables in a situation akin to the modified rational approximation of material properties (RAMP) optimization. As a result, the solution avoids falling into local minima. Based on these findings, we conclude that the primary issue with the SIMP method in displacement control problems lies within the method itself, rather than the objective or finite element analysis. However, the SIMP method can yield excellent results when combined with problem‐specific modifications and parameterizations, such as using ESO to concurrently design the microscale in this section.

![Figure 5.18 Comparison of output displacement between (a) the SIMP method and (b) the proposed hybrid topology optimization.](images/c05f018.png)

[**Figure 5.18**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0018) Comparison of output displacement between (a) the SIMP method and (b) the proposed hybrid topology optimization.

 

### 5.3.2 Multi‐material Porous Soft Robotics

Multi‐material structures represent an advanced engineering paradigm that integrates multiple materials within a single system to exploit their complementary properties, thereby optimizing the functionality of the structure. This methodology reflects the philosophy of strategically positioning materials within the spatial configuration of a structure to achieve superior performance metrics that cannot be realized with a single material alone.

While the fundamental philosophy of multi‐material structures aligns with that of composite materials—leveraging multiple physical materials to benefit from diverse properties—there is a key distinction. Rather than creating a "new material," multi‐material structures utilize the distinct physical responses of different materials in specific regions of the system to collectively achieve the desired structural performance.

Historically, the implementation of multi‐material structures was limited by high costs, despite their potential utility in applications such as suspension bridges (e.g., steel cables supporting concrete slabs and steel girders) and rocket engines. However, with advancements in 3D printing technologies, it has become feasible to fabricate structures comprising multiple materials with distinct physical properties. This innovation significantly simplifies the integration process, enabling the realization of optimized performance with greater ease.

In robotics, a critical requirement is minimizing energy consumption, which is often addressed through weight reduction. Designing porous robotic structures further enhances this objective by creating lightweight systems that improve energy efficiency. This section explores and discusses the design of multi‐material porous soft robotics, supported by numerical examples to illustrate their optimization and performance.

 

#### 5.3.2.1 Mathematical Formulation for Hybrid Topology Optimization in Concurrent Multi‐material and Multi‐scale Systems

This section addresses the complexities of multi‐material systems by decomposing the problem into a series of individual single‐material sub‐problems. This strategy enables the development of a generalized SIMP interpolation model specifically designed for optimizing the topology of multi‐material‐compliant mechanisms. For instance, the interpolation model for the elastic modulus in a three‐phase material system can be expressed as follows:

(5.48)![bold upper E left-parenthesis bold x Subscript normal upper M Baseline right-parenthesis equals sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript q Endscripts bold x Subscript i Superscript p Baseline bold upper E Subscript i](images/c05-math-0048.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <mi mathvariant="bold">E</mi> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">M</mi> </msub> </mfenced> <mo>=</mo> <munderover> <mo movablelimits="false">&#8721;</mo> <mrow> <mi>i</mi> <mo>=</mo> <mn>1</mn> </mrow> <mi>q</mi> </munderover> <msubsup> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mi>i</mi> <mi>p</mi> </msubsup> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">E</mi> <mi>i</mi> </msub> </math> 

where **E**[*i*] represents the unchanging stiffness tensor associated with the *i*^(th) phase when dealing with a particular material distribution denoted by xi. In this research, p is chosen to be 3. The microscale is designed using the MSB method.

By incorporating the multi‐material representation for the objective function of single‐material soft robotics that was introduced in Eq. [(5.43),](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0043) we attain the multi‐material objective function as follows:

Here, *v*[M] and *v*[m] are the volume fractions of the design variable, **x**[M] is the design variable set of the macroscale, and **x**[m] is the design variable of the *i*^(th) material phase of the microscale. Ω[*dM*] and Ω[*dm*] are the macro and micro design domains, respectively. The initial model used in this study had dimensions of 80 mm along the x‐axis and 160 mm along the y‐axis, as illustrated in [Figure 5.19](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0019). The microstructure was represented using a grid of 100 × 100 elements in both the x and y directions. Volume fraction conditions were set at 0.5 for all microscale materials and 0.5 for the macroscale, leading to an overall weight reduction of 75% (corresponding to a 0.25 volume fraction). For all scenarios analyzed, the elastic tensors of the solid materials were consistent: the red material had a Young's modulus of 3, the green material had a Young's modulus of 2, and the blue material had a Young's modulus of 1. The case study revolves around the objective of maximization of the bulk modulus (denoted as ![upper E 11 Superscript normal upper H Baseline plus upper E 22 Superscript normal upper H](images/c05-i0028.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mspace width="0.2em"/><msubsup><mi>E</mi><mn>11</mn><mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi></msubsup><mo>+</mo><msubsup><mi>E</mi><mn>22</mn><mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi></msubsup></math>) within the aforementioned problem framework. This endeavor results in the microscale design configurations for the three constitutive materials, as depicted in [Figure 5.19](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0019)(a)–(d). The macroscale representation of these designs can be observed in [Figure 5.19](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0019)(e). The historical evolution of case (a), as delineated in [Figure 5.19](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0019), is elucidated in [Figure 5.20](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0020).

![Figure 5.19 Concurrent multi‐scale, multi‐material design using hybrid SIMP‐MSB: (a) initial design domain, (b–d) microstructures of different materials, and (e) final macroscale multi‐material design.](images/c05f019.png)
