### 5.3.3 Heat‐Harnessing Porous Robotic Systems

In the previous section, the design of porous robotics was introduced. The activation of such robotic systems relies on the application of external forces at specific, predetermined points. These forces are typically generated through external actuation mechanisms, such as electric actuators, which derive their energy from an electrical power source. This energy is often produced via thermal energy conversion processes, such as those used in thermal power plants. However, energy conversion processes inherently involve losses, making it desirable to minimize power losses by directly harvesting activation energy.

In this section, a methodology for designing heat‐harvesting porous robotic systems is introduced, accompanied by multiple examples. Additionally, the additive manufacturability of these systems is addressed, highlighting their feasibility and potential for efficient production.

 

#### 5.3.3.1 Mathematical Modeling of Thermo‐elastic Loading of Multiphysics‐Compliant Mechanism

Concurrent multi‐scale topology optimization is performed by establishing two independent representations of the design problem, i.e., macro‐ and microscale domains. The design variables related to discretization for topology optimization using two separate finite element systems are **x**[M] and **x**[m], respectively, for macro‐ and microscale design domains. The macroscale domain is statistically homogeneous with respect to the microscale structure, which is regarded as the RVE. The effective characteristics of the microstructure are estimated using the numerical homogenization approach. In this study, the elastic tensor **E**^H and the effective conductivity tensor **Λ**^H are employed as the base material constant tensors for the macroscale. The thermoelastic loading formulations, homogenization theory, and the concurrent multi‐scale optimization of the multiphysics‐compliant mechanism will be discussed in the following subsections.

 

##### *5.3.3.1.1 Thermoelastic Problem Formulation*

The phenomenon of thermal deformation is the property of a substance to change the volume with temperature gradient within the structure. It's measured in terms of the coefficient of thermal expansion (**α**[*ij*]). When a temperature gradient (*ΔT*) exists, it causes thermal loads in the continuum structures due to thermal expansion. The linear thermal strain (**ε**[*th*]) caused by thermal deformation can be expressed by

(5.50)![bold epsilon Subscript italic t h Baseline equals bold alpha Subscript italic i j Baseline normal upper Delta upper T](images/c05-math-0050.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">&#949;</mi> <mi mathvariant="italic">th</mi> </msub> <mo>=</mo> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">&#945;</mi> <mi mathvariant="italic">ij</mi> </msub> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#916;</mi> <mi>T</mi> </math> 

In the analysis of material properties, the RVE framework employs a set of simplifying principles and analytical techniques. This model assumes a linear relationship in heat transfer and adopts small strain theory for elastic tensors, maintaining the analysis within the linear elastic domain. Numerical homogenization is achieved through finite element methods, with periodic boundary conditions applied to the RVE edges. The effective material characteristics are then determined by averaging field values. To ensure periodicity, the finite element model links opposite boundaries with equivalent field functions, interpolating across discretized segments. This methodology facilitates a thorough yet computationally manageable examination of intricate material attributes and responses. The direct coupling method presents significant challenges, particularly due to the complexity involved in achieving the multi‐scale sensitivity analysis required for topology optimization. Alternatively, the sequence coupling method provides computational benefits by utilizing specialized solvers tailored to individual fields. Each field is analyzed using numerical techniques and solvers best suited to its specific properties, simplifying implementation and reducing computational costs. This approach is especially advantageous for complex systems involving multiple interacting fields.

The decision to use sequence coupling or direct coupling depends on several factors, such as the complexity of the coupled system, the desired accuracy, and available computational resources. It is essential to balance accuracy and computational efficiency when selecting the most suitable coupling method. In this study, the sequence coupling method was adopted to address the thermo‐elastic coupling problem. Structural analysis of the thermal response was treated as quasi‐static, with the interaction of thermal and elastic fields represented by the superposition of displacements in the finite element framework, as shown in Eq. [(5.51)](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0051). This method enables efficient and accurate analysis of thermo‐elastic coupling while maintaining manageable computational costs.

(5.51)![bold upper K upper U equals bold upper F plus bold upper F Subscript italic t h](images/c05-math-0051.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <mi mathvariant="bold">KU</mi> <mo>=</mo> <mi mathvariant="bold">F</mi> <mo>+</mo> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">F</mi> <mi mathvariant="italic">th</mi> </msub> </math> 

where **K** is the global elastic stiffness matrix, **U** is the global nodal displacement, **F** is the elastic field nodal force, and **F**[*th*] represents the thermally induced nodal forces. **F**[*th*] is induced by the deformation in the structure due to temperature difference and takes the form of

(5.52)![bold upper F Subscript italic t h Baseline equals integral Underscript normal upper Omega Endscripts bold upper B Superscript upper T Baseline bold upper E alpha upper Delta bold upper T Superscript upper T Baseline d normal upper Omega](images/c05-math-0052.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">F</mi> <mi mathvariant="italic">th</mi> </msub> <mo>=</mo> <msub> <mo>&#8747;</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#937;</mi> </msub> <msup> <mi mathvariant="bold">B</mi> <mi>T</mi> </msup> <mi mathvariant="bold">E&#945;</mi> <mi>&#916;</mi> <msup> <mi mathvariant="bold">T</mi> <mi>T</mi> </msup> <mi>d</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#937;</mi> </math> 

where **B** is the strain displacement matrix, and **E** is the elastic tensor of the material. Here, the thermal gradient is calculated from a dedicated finite element model to describe the phenomena of heat transfer in solids.

 

##### *5.3.3.1.2 Effective Elastic and Thermal Conductivity Tensors for the Microscale*

By definition, the microscale structure is the RVE that is statistically homogeneous with respect to the macroscale domain. The effective characteristics of the microstructure are estimated using the numerical homogenization approach. In this work, the effective elastic tensor **E**^H and the effective heat conductivity tensor **Λ**^H are chosen as the basic material constant tensors at the macroscale. Additionally, with regard to the periodic micro design domain, the related thermal expansion effective property **α**^H is also calculated. In this study, the interface layer between the material and the void is ignored, and the heat conductivity is assumed to be evenly distributed throughout the material. The RVE model incorporates several simplifying assumptions and methodologies. It presumes linear heat transmission and applies small deformation theory to elastic tensors, situating the structure within the ideal elastic range. The model employs numerical homogenization through finite element analysis, utilizing periodic boundary conditions on the RVE borders and deriving effective characteristics from average field calculations. To satisfy these periodic conditions, the cell finite element model connects opposing edge borders using identical field functions, which interpolate the field based on discretization segments. This approach enables a comprehensive yet computationally efficient analysis of complex material properties and behaviors [\[68\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0068). The following part provides a succinct explanation and the basic equation for multi‐scale topology optimization.

Let's begin with the evaluation of the effective elastic tensor. The displacement field *χ* at the macrostructure of a linear elastic material can be represented as an asymptotic expansion form, as shown in Eq. [(5.53):](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0053)

(5.53)![chi left-parenthesis zeta comma xi right-parenthesis equals chi 0 left-parenthesis zeta right-parenthesis plus sigma-summation Underscript k equals 1 Overscript infinity Endscripts omega Subscript k Baseline chi Subscript k Baseline left-parenthesis zeta comma xi right-parenthesis](images/c05-math-0053.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <mi>&#967;</mi> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow> <mi>&#950;</mi> <mo>,</mo> <mi>&#958;</mi> </mrow> </mfenced> <mo>=</mo> <msub> <mi>&#967;</mi> <mn>0</mn> </msub> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mi>&#950;</mi> </mfenced> <mo>+</mo> <munderover> <mo movablelimits="false">&#8721;</mo> <mrow> <mi>k</mi> <mo>=</mo> <mn>1</mn> </mrow> <mo>&#8734;</mo> </munderover> <msub> <mi>&#969;</mi> <mi>k</mi> </msub> <msub> <mi>&#967;</mi> <mi>k</mi> </msub> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow> <mi>&#950;</mi> <mo>,</mo> <mi>&#958;</mi> </mrow> </mfenced> </math> 

where *ω* is the characteristic length scale (*ω* = *l*/*L* ≪ 1), which downscales the macroscale to the microscale (*ξ* = *ζ*/*ω* as shown in [Figure 5.21](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0021)). For RVE to represent two phases, the displacement field expansion could be reduced to first order (k=1).

![Figure 5.21 Macro and micro design domains in a multi‐scale structure.](images/c05f021.png)

[**Figure 5.21**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0021) Macro and micro design domains in a multi‐scale structure.

The link between the effective properties and the local strain generated by thermal expansion is obtained via asymptotic expansion of the thermoelastic equation using perturbation theory. The nature of the thermal expansion properties **α** is similar to the strain vector; therefore, in order to calculate the thermal expansion coefficient, the tensor of homogenized thermal stress due to thermal expansion **β** is needed. And it can be evaluated as follows:

(5.54)![bold beta Superscript normal upper H Baseline equals StartFraction 1 Over StartAbsoluteValue upper V EndAbsoluteValue EndFraction integral Underscript normal upper Omega Subscript normal m Baseline Endscripts bold upper E Subscript italic ijqp Baseline left-parenthesis bold alpha Subscript italic p q Baseline minus bold epsilon Subscript italic p q Superscript italic t h Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript upper T Baseline left-parenthesis bold epsilon Subscript italic i j Superscript 0 Baseline minus bold epsilon Subscript italic i j Superscript asterisk Baseline right-parenthesis italic d upper V](images/c05-math-0054.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <msup> <mi mathvariant="bold">&#946;</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi> </msup> <mo>=</mo> <mfrac> <mn>1</mn> <mfenced open="|" close="|" separators=""><mi>V</mi> </mfenced> </mfrac> <munder> <mo>&#8747;</mo> <msub> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#937;</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi> </msub> </munder> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">E</mi> <mtext mathvariant="italic">ijqp</mtext> </msub> <msup> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">&#945;</mi> <mi mathvariant="italic">pq</mi> </msub> <mo>&#8722;</mo> <msubsup> <mi mathvariant="bold">&#949;</mi> <mi mathvariant="italic">pq</mi> <mi mathvariant="italic">th</mi> </msubsup> </mrow> </mfenced> <mi>T</mi> </msup> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow> <msubsup> <mi mathvariant="bold">&#949;</mi> <mi mathvariant="italic">ij</mi> <mn>0</mn> </msubsup> <mo>&#8722;</mo> <msubsup> <mi mathvariant="bold">&#949;</mi> <mi mathvariant="italic">ij</mi> <mo>&#42;</mo> </msubsup> </mrow> </mfenced> <mspace width="0.25em"/> <mi mathvariant="italic">dV</mi> </math> 

where ![bold epsilon Subscript italic p q Superscript italic t h](images/c05-i0029.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msubsup><mi mathvariant="bold">&#949;</mi><mi mathvariant="italic">pq</mi><mi mathvariant="italic">th</mi></msubsup></math> is the induced strain vector by thermal expansion. The homogenized thermal expansion can be calculated by multiplying the homogenized stress vector with the homogenized elastic tensor as follows:

(5.55)![bold alpha Superscript normal upper H Baseline equals StartFraction bold beta Superscript normal upper H Baseline Over bold upper E Superscript normal upper H Baseline EndFraction](images/c05-math-0055.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <msup> <mi mathvariant="bold">&#945;</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi> </msup> <mo>=</mo> <mfrac> <msup> <mi mathvariant="bold">&#946;</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi> </msup> <msup> <mi mathvariant="bold">E</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi> </msup> </mfrac> </math> 

In this section, the physics of heat convection is also considered. To address this, the surface boundaries are divided into discrete elements or nodes, where the heat transfer equations are computed. Heat convection represents a key mechanism by which thermal energy is exchanged between a solid surface and its surrounding environment \[[69](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0069)–[71](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0071)\]. The rate of heat transfer (**Q**[*Conv*]) through convection is dependent on several factors, including the temperature difference between the solid surface (*T*[*s*]) and the surrounding (*T*[∞]) (as shown in [Figure 5.22](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0022)). This relationship is described by the convection governing equation, which takes the form of

(5.56)![bold upper Q Subscript italic Conv Baseline equals upper A bold k Subscript italic Conv Baseline left-parenthesis bold upper T Subscript s Baseline minus bold upper T Subscript infinity Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-math-0056.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">Q</mi> <mtext mathvariant="italic">Conv</mtext> </msub> <mo>=</mo> <mi>A</mi> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">k</mi> <mtext mathvariant="italic">Conv</mtext> </msub> <mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">T</mi> <mi>s</mi> </msub> <mo>&#8722;</mo> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">T</mi> <mo>&#8734;</mo> </msub> </mrow> </mfenced> </math> 

where **k**[*Conv*] is the heat convection coefficient, and *A* is the solid surface that is in contact with the void filled with air.

![Figure 5.22 Heat transfer modeling of conduction–convection problem.](images/c05f022.png)

[**Figure 5.22**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0022) Heat transfer modeling of conduction–convection problem.

Therefore, at the free surfaces of the solid materials Ω[*s*], the convections take place, so the element on the solid's boundary needs to be updated during the optimization process using the term ![bold upper K Subscript italic Conv Superscript i](images/c05-i0030.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msubsup><mi mathvariant="bold">K</mi><mtext mathvariant="italic">Conv</mtext><mi>i</mi></msubsup></math>, which is given in Eq. [(5.57):](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0057).

(5.57)![bold upper K Subscript italic Conv Superscript i Baseline equals integral Underscript normal upper Omega Subscript s Baseline Endscripts bold upper N Superscript upper T Baseline bold upper N k Subscript italic Conv Baseline d normal upper Omega Subscript s Baseline](images/c05-math-0057.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <msubsup> <mi mathvariant="bold">K</mi> <mtext mathvariant="italic">Conv</mtext> <mi>i</mi> </msubsup> <mo>=</mo> <msub> <mo>&#8747;</mo> <msub> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#937;</mi> <mi>s</mi> </msub> </msub> <msup> <mi mathvariant="bold">N</mi> <mi>T</mi> </msup> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">Nk</mi> <mtext mathvariant="italic">Conv</mtext> </msub> <mi>d</mi> <msub> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#937;</mi> <mi>s</mi> </msub> </math> 

where **N** is the shape function matrix for the heat distribution field. Moreover, the corresponding heat flux vector load update is formulated as follows:

(5.58)![bold upper Q Subscript italic Conv Superscript i Baseline equals integral Underscript normal upper Omega Subscript s Baseline Endscripts bold upper N Superscript upper T Baseline bold k Subscript italic Conv Baseline bold upper T Subscript infinity Baseline d normal upper Omega Subscript s Baseline](images/c05-math-0058.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <msubsup> <mi mathvariant="bold">Q</mi> <mtext mathvariant="italic">Conv</mtext> <mi>i</mi> </msubsup> <mo>=</mo> <msub> <mo>&#8747;</mo> <msub> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#937;</mi> <mi>s</mi> </msub> </msub> <msup> <mi mathvariant="bold">N</mi> <mi>T</mi> </msup> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">k</mi> <mtext mathvariant="italic">Conv</mtext> </msub> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">T</mi> <mo>&#8734;</mo> </msub> <mi>d</mi> <msub> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#937;</mi> <mi>s</mi> </msub> </math>  

#### 5.3.3.2 The Topology Optimization for Multiphysics‐Compliant Mechanisms

Designing a compliant mechanism through topology optimization focuses on determining the optimal shape to achieve a specific deformation pattern (illustrated in [Figure 5.23](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0023)). The elastic structure demonstrates compliant behavior by producing a displacement, represented as (*u*[*out*]), at a designated output port [\[72\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0072). The goal of this optimization problem is to identify the optimal topology of the elastic structure that maximizes the output displacement while ensuring compliance with the equilibrium equations and adhering to a specified material volume constraint.

![Figure 5.23 Thermo‐elastic compliant mechanism design problem for the thermally actuated model.](images/c05f023.png)

[**Figure 5.23**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0023) Thermo‐elastic compliant mechanism design problem for the thermally actuated model.

Thermal expansion resulting from variations in temperature distribution induces deformation in heat‐activated compliant mechanisms. The objective function is updated based on the form of Eq. [(5.59)](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0059), which is expressed as follows:

(5.59)![StartLayout 1st Row 1st Column max Underscript bold x Endscripts colon 2nd Column u Subscript italic out 2nd Row 1st Column normal s period normal t period colon 2nd Column StartLayout Enlarged left-brace 1st Row bold upper K upper U equals bold upper F Subscript italic t h Baseline 2nd Row integral bold x d normal upper Omega less-than-or-equal-to v comma bold x element-of left-parenthesis 0 comma 1 right-bracket for-all bold x element-of normal upper Omega EndLayout EndLayout](images/c05-math-0059.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"> <mtable columnalign="left" displaystyle="true"><mtr> <mtd> <munder> <mi mathvariant="normal">max</mi> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> </munder> <mtext>:</mtext> </mtd> <mtd> <mspace width="1.0em"/> <msub> <mi>u</mi> <mtext mathvariant="italic">out</mtext> </msub> </mtd> </mtr> <mtr> <mtd> <mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi> <mo>.</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">t</mi> <mo>.</mo> <mtext>:</mtext> </mtd> <mtd> <mspace width="1.0em"/> <mfenced open="{" close="" separators=""><mtable> <mtr> <mtd> <mspace width="0.2em"/> <mi mathvariant="bold">KU</mi> <mo>=</mo> <msub> <mi mathvariant="bold">F</mi> <mi mathvariant="italic">th</mi> </msub> </mtd> </mtr> <mtr> <mtd> <mo>&#8747;</mo> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mi>d</mi> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#937;</mi> <mo>&#8804;</mo> <mi>v</mi> <mo>,</mo> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mo>&#8712;</mo> <mfenced open="(" close="\]" separators=""><mrow> <mn>0</mn> <mo>,</mo> <mn>1</mn> </mrow> </mfenced> <mspace width="0.84em"/> <mo>&#8704;</mo> <mi mathvariant="bold">x</mi> <mo>&#8712;</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">&#937;</mi> </mtd> </mtr> </mtable> </mfenced> </mtd> </mtr> </mtable> </math> 

where N is the number of elements in the design domain. For the macro‐**x**[M] and microscale **x**[m] of the design domain, the modeling of the thermoelastic problem necessitates a dedicated finite element model for each physics (i.e., a model for thermal and mechanical physics), as shown in [Figure 5.24](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0024).

![Figure 5.24 Finite element modeling of the coupled multi‐scale and multiphysics of thermo‐elastic problem.](images/c05f024.png)

[**Figure 5.24**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0024) Finite element modeling of the coupled multi‐scale and multiphysics of thermo‐elastic problem.

As such, the concurrent multi‐scale topology optimization problem formulation is presented in Eq. [(5.60):](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0060)

To provide a summary, the concurrent model comprises a macrostructure with predefined boundary conditions, as depicted in [Figure 5.25](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0025)(a) and (b). The design domain Ω[M] consists of periodically replicated RVEs, with a designated design domain Ω[m] (as presented in [Figure 5.25](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0025) (c)).

Each system, namely the macro‐ and microscale, is discretized using dedicated finite elements that correspond to the design variables.

![Figure 5.25 Multi‐scale design problem: (a) macrostructure; (b) periodic cellular structure; (c) microstructure. Al Ali et al., (2023) / Elsevier.](images/c05f025.png)

[**Figure 5.25**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0025) Multi‐scale design problem: (a) macrostructure; (b) periodic cellular structure; (c) microstructure. Al Ali et al., (2023) / Elsevier.

 

#### 5.3.3.3 Numerical Implementations

This section presents a range of case studies exploring the multiphysics multi‐scale thermoelastic formulation through various numerical examples. The investigations are structured into two main categories, each focusing on different aspects of the optimization process.

The first category examines the design of a reciprocal displacement actuator. We outline the optimization strategies employed and provide a comprehensive analysis of the outcomes achieved. This investigation sheds light on the intricacies of designing such actuators and the effectiveness of our optimization approach.

The second category focuses on a heat‐activated clipper mechanism. We detail the optimization techniques applied to this mechanism and offer an in‐depth discussion of the results and their implications. This study provides valuable insights into the design considerations and potential applications of heat‐activated clippers.

Through these detailed examinations, we aim to enhance our understanding of both the reciprocal displacement actuator and the heat‐activated clipper mechanism, contributing to the broader field of thermoelastic design optimization.

 

##### *5.3.3.3.1 Reciprocal Heat‐Activated Displacement Actuator Optimization*

To begin with, we'll explore the development of displacement actuators that respond to heat, considering designs both with and without heat convection at single and multiple scales. These actuators are engineered to generate linear movement by converting thermal energy into physical displacement.

Our initial focus was on the multiphysics topology optimization of a displacement actuator under thermal loading, excluding convection effects. We constructed a 2D macro design domain at a single scale, featuring insulation along the upper and lower boundaries (illustrated in [Figure 5.26](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0026)(a) and (c), respectively). This domain utilized bilinear finite element discretization (shown in [Figure 5.26](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0026)(b)) for both elastic and solid heat transfer physics simulations.

![Figure 5.26 Single‐scale macromodel of the heat‐activated displacement actuator under (a) heating, (b) the constitutive bi‐linear element and (c) cooling \[73\].](images/c05f026.png)

[**Figure 5.26**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0026) Single‐scale macromodel of the heat‐activated displacement actuator under (a) heating, (b) the constitutive bi‐linear element and (c) cooling [\[73\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0073).

In the numerical simulations conducted in this study, material properties such as Young's modulus, thermal expansion coefficient, and heat convection tensor are assumed to have a unit value. This simplification enables a focused analysis of the specific phenomenon under investigation while also streamlining computational processes. During the topology optimization, the properties of void elements are assigned infinitesimal values rather than zero to minimize numerical errors and ensure stability throughout the optimization process \[[20](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0020), [74](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0074), [75](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0075)\]. The mesh for each model consists of 160 × 160 bilinear elements distributed along the x and y directions, with each element representing an area of 1 mm². The optimization was initially conducted at a single scale, focusing solely on the macroscale. Two volume fractions were considered: (a) a volume fraction of 0.5, corresponding to a 50% weight reduction, and (b) a volume fraction of 0.25, corresponding to a 75% weight reduction. The results of the macroscale topology optimization, along with the objective function histories, are presented in [Figure 5.27](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0027). Additionally, [Figure 5.28](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0028) illustrates the deformation and heat map of the design that achieved the maximum deformation, specifically the design corresponding to the 75% weight reduction.

![Figure 5.27 Normalized objective function of the single‐scale heat‐activated displacement actuator of (a) 50% weight reduction and (b) 75% weight reduction cases \[73\].](images/c05f027.png)

[**Figure 5.27**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0027) Normalized objective function of the single‐scale heat‐activated displacement actuator of (a) 50% weight reduction and (b) 75% weight reduction cases [\[73\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0073).

![Figure 5.28 Heat map and deformation of the displacement inverter design for 75% weight reduction \[73\].](images/c05f028.png)

[**Figure 5.28**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0028) Heat map and deformation of the displacement inverter design for 75% weight reduction [\[73\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0073).

It was necessary to formulate the microstructure using a specific finite element model in order to extend the inquiry to encompass multi‐scale optimization. Periodic boundary conditions have been taken into account in order to address the microstructure in accordance with the numerical homogenization theory [\[21\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0021). Consequently, a 100 × 100 element discretization of a finite element mesh was chosen at the microscale. The homogenization process and microstructure sensitivity analysis demand a substantial amount of processing power due to the large systems of equations that describe the behavior of the material at the microscale, which this mesh discretization allowed for a sufficient resolution of the microstructure while maintaining computational efficiency ([Figure 5.29](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0029)(a) and (b)).

The original design domain for the microstructure consists of a circular core subdomain with a radius equivalent to 50 elements. This central subdomain was initialized with a significantly smaller value compared to the remainder of the microstructure's design domain. This particular configuration was selected for two main purposes: first, to facilitate the optimization process using the optimality criteria while reducing computational expense \[[26](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0026)\]; second, to minimize numerical instability arising from cumulative errors \[[27](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0027), [75](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0075)\].

The multi‐scale optimization targeting a 50% weight reduction at both the macro‐ and microscales is designed to achieve a total volume fraction of 0.25 (equivalent to a 75% weight reduction), assuming that each element has a volume equal to that of the microscale. Consequently, significant volume reduction can be realized by minimizing weight at both the micro‐ and macrolevels. The results for the optimization of both the macro‐ and microscales are presented in [Figure 5.30](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0030).

![Figure 5.29 Multi‐scale design domain of the heat‐activated displacement actuator as (a) the macro domain, and (b) micro domain \[73\].](images/c05f029.png)

[**Figure 5.29**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0029) Multi‐scale design domain of the heat‐activated displacement actuator as (a) the macro domain, and (b) micro domain [\[73\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0073).

![Figure 5.30 Porous design of the heat‐activated displacement actuator \[73\] / with permission of Elsevier.](images/c05f030.png)

[**Figure 5.30**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0030) Porous design of the heat‐activated displacement actuator [\[73\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0073) / with permission of Elsevier.

The performance of the optimized single‐ and multi‐scale cases is presented in [Figure 5.31](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0031). From a physical perspective, designs with thicker membranes exhibit greater resistance to movement as compliant mechanisms due to their increased stiffness (i.e., reduced compliance), while designs with thinner linkages can achieve greater displacement for the same actuation field. This behavior is evident in the comparison of maximum displacement achieved for the 50% weight reduction versus the 75% weight reduction cases.

Furthermore, the concurrently optimized multi‐scale porous displacement actuator with a 75% weight reduction demonstrated superior performance compared to the single‐scale case with the same weight reduction, showing a 17.6% improvement over the 50% weight reduction case and a 6% improvement over the single‐scale 75% weight reduction case. The flexibility afforded by the microscale design domain allows the concurrent optimization algorithm to more effectively distribute materials for the porous heat‐activated displacement actuator, optimizing both the macro layout and the actuator's pose.

Let's examine the impact of heat convection on a single‐scale design domain, similar to that depicted in [Figure 5.26](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0026), considering weight reductions of 50% and 75%. Our investigation focuses on how varying heat convection coefficients affect the optimized structures. For this study, we set the ambient temperature to 0°C. The optimization process is conducted across a range of heat convection coefficients to thoroughly analyze their influence on the resulting designs. This approach allows us to observe how the optimal topology evolves as convective heat transfer becomes more prominent. It's worth noting that many experts in topology optimization advocate for the use of dimensionless studies. This recommendation stems from the advantages of dimensionless analysis in providing more generalizable results and facilitating easier comparison across different scales and scenarios \[[76](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0076), [77](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0077)\]. In topology optimization research, many scholars employ simplified material constants, often using unity values, to streamline studies and facilitate the development of objective functions and gradient optimizers. This approach, valuable for assessing adjoint analysis techniques, aligns with the broader use of dimensional analysis in engineering and scientific research. Dimensional analysis offers benefits such as problem simplification, identification of key parameters, development of scaling relationships, and experimental design for hypothesis testing. In our dimensionless study, we initially set a low convection rate, given that conduction and thermal expansion coefficients were normalized to 1 for the base material across all design cases. This approach allows for the isolation and examination of convection effects on the optimized structure, as even minor changes in surface boundary convection can significantly impact topology optimization outcomes \[[78](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0078)–[81](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0081)\].

![Figure 5.31 Performance of the 50% weight reduction solid, 75% weight reduction solid, and 75% weight reduction porous heat‐activated displacement actuator.](images/c05f031.png)

[**Figure 5.31**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0031) Performance of the 50% weight reduction solid, 75% weight reduction solid, and 75% weight reduction porous heat‐activated displacement actuator.

The findings summarized in Table [5.5](#c05.xhtml#c05-tbl-0005) indicate that the design domain adapts to increasing heat convection by developing extensions from the primary design body. Expanding the investigation to include multi‐scale optimization yielded the results presented in Table [5.6](#c05.xhtml#c05-tbl-0006). Similar to the single‐scale optimization outcomes, the macroscale design in the multi‐scale case responds to elevated heat convection by forming additional extensions to accommodate the increased thermal load. These results highlight the influence of heat convection on the structural resilience of the design. Notably, as heat convection intensifies, spike‐like formations emerge along the design boundaries, protruding from the main structure. These features become more pronounced with higher convection rates. The elevated convection‐driven heat transfer at the macroscale compels the structure to introduce perpendicular links, which in turn prompts the microstructure to adapt its configuration.

![Table 5.5 Single macroscale design of the heat‐activated displacement actuator with various heat convection coefficients \[73\].](images/c05t005a.png)

[**Table 5.5**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-tbl-0005) Single macroscale design of the heat‐activated displacement actuator with various heat convection coefficients [\[73\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0073).

![Table 5.6 Multi‐scale design of the heat‐activated displacement actuator with various heat convection coefficients \[73\].](images/c05t006a.png)

[**Table 5.6**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-tbl-0006) Multi‐scale design of the heat‐activated displacement actuator with various heat convection coefficients [\[73\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0073).

As shown in Table [5.6](#c05.xhtml#c05-tbl-0006), the microstructure responds by increasing its shear modulus of elasticity while reducing Young's modulus in the x and y directions. This adaptation enhances the design's resilience and elasticity against deformations induced by thermal actuation while also strengthening its resistance to twisting that could impair linear movements in the x direction—a key objective of the optimization. Additionally, the solid heat conductivity tensor indicates that the microstructure seeks to minimize heat transfer to the newly formed extensions, thereby preserving the thermal efficiency of the main design body.

 

##### *5.3.3.3.2 Topology optimization for heat‐activated grippers*

This section addresses the optimization of a heat‐activated gripper. As shown in [Figure 5.23](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0023), a heat flow is established from the left boundary to the right, while the upper and lower boundaries of the initial design domain are thermally insulated. Adopting a methodology similar to the numerical example discussed in Section 5.3.4.4.1, and excluding convection effects, we performed a multiphysics topology optimization of the heat‐activated gripper. The analysis is conducted on a single‐scale macro design domain, utilizing the same bilinear finite element discretization as in the prior study for both elastic and heat transfer physics.

The design domain consists of 80 × 80 elements, with a non‐design region for the gripper measuring 40 × 10 elements, forming an opening. Two volume fraction cases, 0.25 and 0.5 (corresponding to 75% and 50% weight reductions, respectively), were analyzed. The gripping output points were positioned above and below the opening, offset by 26 elements (as depicted in [Figure 5.32](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0032)), to provide greater flexibility for topology optimization in achieving an optimal design.

![Figure 5.32 Design domain of a heat‐activated gripper \[73\].](images/c05f032.png)

[**Figure 5.32**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0032) Design domain of a heat‐activated gripper [\[73\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0073).

Initially, a single‐scale optimization was performed, focusing exclusively on the macroscale. The optimization was then repeated for the two volume fraction cases. The first case employed a volume fraction of 0.5, achieving a 50% weight reduction, while the second case utilized a volume fraction of 0.25, resulting in a 75% weight reduction. The results of the single‐scale macroscale topology optimization, along with the evolution of the objective functions over time, are presented in [Figure 5.33](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0033).

![Figure 5.33 Normalized objective function of the single‐scale heat‐activated gripper of (a) 50% weight reduction and (b) 75% weight reduction cases \[73\].](images/c05f033.png)

[**Figure 5.33**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0033) Normalized objective function of the single‐scale heat‐activated gripper of (a) 50% weight reduction and (b) 75% weight reduction cases [\[73\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0073).

We extended the scope of this investigation to encompass the design of a heat‐activated porous gripper. Following a similar methodology to our previous approach, we initialized the microstructure design domain with a circular central sub‐design region of a 50‐element radius, as illustrated in [Figure 5.34](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0034). The primary goal was to perform multi‐scale optimization targeting a 50% weight reduction at both macro‐ and microscales while ensuring an overall volume fraction of 0.25, corresponding to a total weight reduction of 75%. This approach assumed that each element's volume was equivalent to that of the microscale. By simultaneously minimizing weights at both scales, a significant volume reduction was achieved. The optimization results for both scales are depicted in [Figure 5.35](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0035).

![Figure 5.34 Multi‐scale design domain of the heat‐activated gripper as (a) the macro domain, and (b) micro domain \[73\].](images/c05f034.png)

[**Figure 5.34**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0034) Multi‐scale design domain of the heat‐activated gripper as (a) the macro domain, and (b) micro domain [\[73\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0073).

![Figure 5.35 Porous design of the heat‐activated gripper design as (a) the macro domain, and (b) micro domain \[73\].](images/c05f035.png)

[**Figure 5.35**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0035) Porous design of the heat‐activated gripper design as (a) the macro domain, and (b) micro domain [\[73\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0073).

The single‐scale optimization design that aimed at a 50% weight reduction exhibited inferior performance compared to the design that optimized for a 75% weight reduction, as shown in [Figure 5.36](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0036). However, it is important to note that both single‐ and multi‐scale structures that optimized for a 75% weight reduction displayed comparable performance.

![Figure 5.36 Performance comparison of the 50% weight reduction solid, 75% weight reduction solid, and 75% weight reduction porous heat‐activated gripper.](images/c05f036.png)

[**Figure 5.36**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0036) Performance comparison of the 50% weight reduction solid, 75% weight reduction solid, and 75% weight reduction porous heat‐activated gripper.

![Table 5.7  A single macroscale heat‐activated gripper design that reduces weight by 75% for different heat convection coefficients.](images/c05t007a.png)

[Table 5.7](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-tbl-0007)  A single macroscale heat‐activated gripper design that reduces weight by 75% for different heat convection coefficients.

To further investigate the influence of convection on gripper design, we analyzed both the 75% and 50% weight reduction cases, with results presented in [Tables 5.7](#c05.xhtml#c05-tbl-0007) and [5.8](#c05.xhtml#c05-tbl-0008), respectively. Remarkably, the multi‐scale porous gripper that optimized for a 75% weight reduction demonstrated excellent performance, comparable to that of the single‐scale design achieving the same weight reduction.

During single‐scale optimization, it has been noted that as heat transfer to the surroundings increases (reflected by a higher heat convection coefficient), the gripper design demonstrates greater flexibility. However, while the mechanical contact between the point of action and the mechanical support is maintained, the design progressively becomes less effective for its intended function. This phenomenon is more pronounced in cases of 75% weight reduction compared to 50% weight reduction, particularly when heat convection is relatively low. This behavior is illustrated in [Tables 5.7](#c05.xhtml#c05-tbl-0007) and [5.8](#c05.xhtml#c05-tbl-0008). The larger surface area associated with the 50% weight reduction serves as a thermal energy reservoir, contributing to this effect. A similar trend is observed in the multi‐scale optimized cases, as shown in [Table 5.9](#c05.xhtml#c05-tbl-0009). These results highlight the influence of heat convection on the gripper's performance, where increased heat dissipation to the surroundings diminishes its functionality. The thermal energy reservoir created by the larger surface area further amplifies this impact. These findings emphasize the importance of carefully managing and balancing heat convection effects during the optimization process to ensure the gripper's practicality and performance.

![Table 5.8  A single macroscale heat‐activated gripper design that reduces weight by 50% for different heat convection coefficients.](images/c05t008a.png)

[Table 5.8](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-tbl-0008)  A single macroscale heat‐activated gripper design that reduces weight by 50% for different heat convection coefficients.

![Table 5.9  Heat‐activated gripper design on many scales with different heat convection coefficients.](images/c05t009a.png)

[Table 5.9](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-tbl-0009)  Heat‐activated gripper design on many scales with different heat convection coefficients.

To examine the dependence of the macrostructure on microstructural systems, multiple microstructures were introduced for concurrent optimization alongside the macrostructure. This analysis was conducted on a heat‐activated gripper by dividing its macro design domain into distinct sections, with each section assigned a unique micro design domain. While the size, initial design variables, and spatial configurations remained consistent, each micro design domain was assigned an individual finite element system. Initially, the macro domain was divided into four equal sections, each with its own micro design domain, as depicted in [Figure 5.37](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0037). The multi‐scale design maintained a volume fraction of 0.25, corresponding to a 75% weight reduction, and the resulting design is shown in [Figure 5.38](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0038). Additionally, the macro domain was further divided into eight sections, each with a designated micro design domain, as illustrated in [Figure 5.39](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0039), with the final design presented in [Figure 5.40](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0040).

![Figure 5.37 The heat‐activated gripper's multi‐scale design domain consists of four pieces, each with a dedicated microstructure.](images/c05f037.png)

[**Figure 5.37**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0037) The heat‐activated gripper's multi‐scale design domain consists of four pieces, each with a dedicated microstructure.

![Figure 5.38 Optimization results of (a) four‐sectioned heat‐activated gripper optimization results, and (b) the design defect due microstructure mismatch.](images/c05f038.png)

[**Figure 5.38**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0038) Optimization results of (a) four‐sectioned heat‐activated gripper optimization results, and (b) the design defect due microstructure mismatch.

![Figure 5.39 Multi‐scale design domain for the heat‐activated gripper of eight sections with a dedicated microstructure for each.](images/c05f039.png)

[**Figure 5.39**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0039) Multi‐scale design domain for the heat‐activated gripper of eight sections with a dedicated microstructure for each.

![Figure 5.40 Optimization results of (a) the eight sections of the heat‐activated gripper, and (b) the design defect due microstructure mismatch.](images/c05f040.png)

[**Figure 5.40**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0040) Optimization results of (a) the eight sections of the heat‐activated gripper, and (b) the design defect due microstructure mismatch.

As illustrated in [Figure 5.41](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0041), the design's performance markedly increased with the addition of more sections and a tailored microstructure. This enhancement was especially notable in the eight‐section case, where performance improved by an impressive 31.3% compared to the single macroscale optimized design with a 50% weight reduction (refer to case \[a\] in [Figure 5.33](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0033)). These results underscore that enhancing the microstructural diversity within the macroscale domain allows concurrently optimized multi‐scale designs to achieve remarkable adaptability.

The material properties of the periodic microstructure were evaluated using asymptotic homogenization theory. However, the core assumptions of homogenization theory—scale separation and periodicity—do not inherently address the connectivity between adjacent microstructures during the optimization of multi‐microstructure systems. Insufficient microstructural connections, particularly at the boundaries of adjoining sections, are a primary source of connectivity issues, compounded by challenges in manufacturability (as illustrated in [Figures 5.38](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0038)(b) and [5.40](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0040)(b)). Moreover, weak connectivity can lead to difficulties in load transfer and inaccuracies in property estimation when applying the homogenization approach. Therefore, it is crucial to examine the influence of boundary conditions and connectivity between microstructures to achieve practical and manufacturable designs suitable for additive manufacturing. This issue has been explored by several researchers. For example, Du et al. [\[82\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0082) investigated the introduction of a connectivity index to control the connection of the adjacent microstructures. Liu et al. [\[83\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0083) investigated the introduction of predefined connectivity regions for microstructures. In this research, we adopted Neves et al.'s [\[84\]](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0084) method by implementing a connection boundary as a non‐design domain to ensure better connectivity on the microstructure level. Also, this method showed attaining very good results in terms of solving the connectivity issues of microstructure and, therefore, improving the manufacturing ability \[[85](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0085), [86](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0086)\]. The optimization process is repeated using a connective boundary with a width of four elements for both the four‐section and eight‐section cases, as depicted in [Figure 5.42](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0042)(a) and (b), respectively. For the multi‐scale cases, a volume fraction of 0.25 is maintained, corresponding to a 75% weight reduction. The resulting optimization outcomes are presented in [Figures 5.43](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0043) and [5.44](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0044).

 ![Figure 5.41 Performance comparison of heat‐activated gripper designs.](images/c05f041.png)

[**Figure 5.41**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0041) Performance comparison of heat‐activated gripper designs.

![Figure 5.42 Multi‐scale design domain of the heat‐activated gripper with a non‐design domain for connectivity of (a) (a) four‐sectioned heat‐activated gripper, and (b) eight‐sectioned heat‐activated gripper.](images/c05f042a.png)

[**Figure 5.42**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0042) Multi‐scale design domain of the heat‐activated gripper with a non‐design domain for connectivity of (a) (a) four‐sectioned heat‐activated gripper, and (b) eight‐sectioned heat‐activated gripper.

 ![Figure 5.43 Heat‐activated gripper optimization results for four parts with microstructure connectivity.](images/c05f043.png)

[**Figure 5.43**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0043) Heat‐activated gripper optimization results for four parts with microstructure connectivity.

 ![Figure 5.44 Heat‐activated gripper optimization results for eight parts with microstructure connectivity.](images/c05f044.png)

[**Figure 5.44**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0044) Heat‐activated gripper optimization results for eight parts with microstructure connectivity.

The effectiveness of concurrently optimized multi‐scale designs improves markedly as the heterogeneity of the microstructure within the macroscale domain increases \[[3](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0003), [20](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0020)\]. Nevertheless, in multi‐scale and multi‐sector design domains that include connected non‐design regions, their performance is significantly reduced compared to scenarios without connectivity constraints, as illustrated in [Figure 5.45](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0045). This reduction arises from the restrictions on microstructural movements imposed by the fixed framework. These limitations curtail the adaptability of the microstructure and obstruct the fine‐tuning of design parameters, ultimately leading to diminished performance. These results highlight the critical need to address connectivity constraints to achieve optimal outcomes in multi‐scale design domains.

![Figure 5.45 Heat‐activated gripper multi‐sectioned design with and without connectivity non‐design domains, with and without four and eight sections, and performance comparison.](images/c05f045.png)

[**Figure 5.45**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0045) Heat‐activated gripper multi‐sectioned design with and without connectivity non‐design domains, with and without four and eight sections, and performance comparison.

# Chapter 6  
Advanced Software Utilization for Designing and Analyzing Porous Structures

 

## 6.1 INTRODUCTION

In this chapter, we provide an overview of commercial tools commonly used for designing porous structures and introduce educational codes to illustrate the principles of porous structure design. The chapter is divided into two main sections. The first section presents several widely used software platforms that support the modeling and optimization of porous architectures for industrial applications. These tools were selected based on practical experience and technical familiarity, rather than as endorsements or commercial promotions. The goal is to provide insights into the features and capabilities of these software platforms, enhancing the understanding of current design methodologies and their application in additive manufacturing. The second section offers demonstrations of educational codes for designing porous structures through topology optimization. These examples are intended for educational purposes only and are designed to provide foundational knowledge of the underlying concepts. It is important to clarify that, while every effort has been made to ensure the accuracy and reliability of the educational codes provided, the author does not guarantee that the codes are free of errors or bugs. The author shall not be held liable for any claims, damages, or issues arising from the use of these codes, whether during or after their execution. Users are encouraged to use these codes responsibly and at their own risk.

 

## 6.2 COMMERCIAL SOFTWARE

In this section, we review several commercial software packages commonly employed for designing porous structures. The current approach to designing porous structures for the vast majority of commercial software predominantly relies on infilling the geometry with predesigned microstructures, with the mechanical behavior often approximated using empirical interpolation functions based on the microstructure's size and thickness. While this method provides a straightforward solution, it oversimplifies the complex interactions within the material, limiting the precision of the mechanical response prediction. Software vendors have recognized the need to improve reliability by minimizing the risks associated with generating microstructures through optimization techniques, such as inverse homogenization. However, despite these advancements, inverse homogenization has yet to be fully integrated as a direct design tool into most commercial software platforms. This omission significantly restricts the potential for more innovative, performance‐driven designs. Although some software offers the flexibility to develop custom microstructures via computer language interfaces, enabling users to write code, this remains a workaround rather than a fully realized solution.

 

### 6.2.1 Altair Inspire

Altair Inspire is a simulation software that has become a valuable tool for creating and optimizing lightweight structures, particularly through lattice optimization techniques. By combining various lattice geometries, it enables the creation of designs that are lighter, stronger, and more energy‐efficient by adapting porous structures. In Altair Inspire, lattice optimization transforms the elements of a three‐dimensional (3D) mesh of solid structure components into interconnected lattice beams. The size and thickness of these beams are determined using a gray area approximation within the solid isotropic material with penalization (SIMP) \[[1](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0001)–[3](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0003)\] method. By interpolating the design variables of each element and its surrounding elements, the thickness of the lattice beams is defined.

However, the lattice design itself is preconfigured, with lattice types selected from a library of predefined options embedded in the software. Therefore, it is more accurate to describe this process as a hybrid non‐parametric optimization design approach.

 

### 6.2.2 GENESIS Software

In some similarity with Altair Inspire, using the GENESIS software can optimize lattice structures by employing topometry optimization, a method that enables the adjustment of lattice member dimensions based on specific design criteria. GENESIS streamlines this process through its use of the homogenization method. The adaptation of asymptotic homogenization allows for fast simulation without the need to explicitly model the individual lattice elements. By selecting key parameters such as lattice cell type, unit cell dimensions, and cross‐sectional thickness, users can efficiently define the structural characteristics of the lattice within finite element analysis simulations. The software offers a wide library of prebuilt lattice configurations, including cubic, body‐centered cubic, and double pyramid geometries, providing flexibility in selecting the lattice pattern. The optimization process involves minimizing some properties and functions such as mass while constraining displacements.

 

### 6.2.3 nTopology

nTopology is a non‐parametric optimization‐based design software that offers an approach to creating porous structures, particularly tailored to additive manufacturing applications. With its advanced computational design tools, nTopology allows to select lattice structures that are controlled to introduce porosity for the structure, to allow light weighting while optimizing structural performance as stiffness.

The software supports a wide range of lattice configurations including triply periodic minimal surfaces (TPMS), such as gyroid and Diamond. TPMS structures are particularly useful in optimizing material efficiency and mechanical performance, as they exhibit continuous, smooth surfaces that can distribute stresses evenly and improve structural integrity without adding excess weight.

Another key aspect of nTopology is its implicit modeling approach. With implicit modeling, geometric operations are defined using mathematical functions, making it easier to create complex shapes like lattices that would otherwise be labor‐intensive or even impossible to model explicitly.

 

### 6.2.4 SpaceClaim

Ansys SpaceClaim is a versatile 3D modeling software developed to design porous structures. Once the desired unit cell type is selected, SpaceClaim allows these cells to be populated within a predefined design domain. The software provides some flexibility to control parameters such as cell size, orientation, and distribution, enabling optimization of the properties of the porous material.

 

### 6.2.5 PTC Creo

PTC Creo is a computer‐aided design software that provides a set of tools for the design and analysis of products for mechanical engineering. One of the features of Creo is its lattice design functionality, which supports the creation of various types of porous structures. These include beam‐based lattices, 2.5D lattices \[[4](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0004), [5](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0005)\], formula‐driven lattices \[[6](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0006)–[11](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0011)\], custom lattices, and stochastic lattices \[[12](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0012)–[14](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0014)\], each offering unique advantages for different engineering applications. Creo's lattice generation tools allow us to optimize structures for strength, weight, thermal conductivity, and manufacturability.

 

## 6.3 CODING‐BASED COMMERCIAL SOFTWARE DESIGN OF POROUS STRUCTURES

Several software packages allow users to customize features by utilizing programming languages or even introducing new functionalities through their own programming platforms, such as APD for ANSYS and ANSA coding for Beta software. Additionally, widely used high‐level programming languages like Java, VBA, TCL, MATLAB, and Python are supported by these software packages. These languages, with their extensive libraries and large community support, enable the rapid development of cost‐effective, high‐performance custom programs. Such programs can enhance the design of porous structures and even facilitate the creation of porous structures in software that does not natively support such designs. For example, the author developed a topology optimization‐based porous structure design using MATLAB to control COMSOL software, enabling the design of elastic failure‐resistant microstructures for various theories of elastic and dynamic failures, as depicted in [Figure 6.1](#c06.xhtml#c06-fig-0001).

Moreover, it is possible to program commercial software that does not natively support programming languages. For example, Nastran has been shown to be adaptable for use with C++, enabling the development of innovative porous designs, such as porous laminated plates, to withstand static and dynamic loads (as depicted in [Figure 6.2](#c06.xhtml#c06-fig-0002)) [\[15\]](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0015).

 

### 6.3.1 Software Multiphysics Topology Optimization

The concurrent multi‐scale optimization steps for the multiphysics problem outlined in Section 5‐2‐8 are implemented in a MATLAB code, which is provided in the GitHub respiratory ([https://github.com/musaddiqalali/Multiscal-multiphysic-topology-optimization](https://github.com/musaddiqalali/Multiscal-multiphysic-topology-optimization)). This code employs the SIMP method for topology optimization to effectively solve the problem. The MATLAB code for topology optimization and homogenization, based on the work by Andreassen et al. \[[16](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0016), [17](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0017)\], serves as the foundation for this implementation. The code is structured into seven distinct modules, each organized into separate MATLAB files to provide flexibility and customization for the user. These modules include:

![Figure 6.1 COMOSL‐MATLAB microstructure design examples.](images/c06f001.png)

[**Figure 6.1**](#c06.xhtml#R_c06-fig-0001) COMOSL‐MATLAB microstructure design examples.

1.  **Input Data**: Handles the initial parameters and setup;
2.  **Finite Element Preparations**: Prepares the finite element method (FEM) framework;
3.  **Normalization Factor Calculation**: Computes necessary normalization factors;
4.  **Asymptotic Homogenization**: Applies homogenization techniques to model material properties;
5.  **Goal Programming Multi‐Objective Function**: Constructs the multi‐objective optimization function;
6.  **ConcurrentMulti‐scale Optimization**: Executes the optimization process across multiple scales.

![Figure 6.2 Porous plate design using Nastran‐C++ program. Shimoda et al., (2023) / Elsevier.](images/c06f002.png)

[**Figure 6.2**](#c06.xhtml#R_c06-fig-0002) Porous plate design using Nastran‐C++ program. Shimoda et al., (2023) / Elsevier.

**These modules are distributed across seven MATLAB functions**:

*   Concurrent\_MOO.m,
*   get\_Initials.m,
*   Concurrent\_solver.m,
*   Homogenization\_full.m,
*   elementMatVec.m,
*   Objective\_Calculator.m,
*   OC\_2D.m.

The main program, Concurrent\_MOO.m, orchestrates the entire optimization process, including data generation and execution of the core optimization steps. The other functions are called within this main program to perform specific tasks as part of the overall process. The flow of the program is illustrated in [Figure 6.3](#c06.xhtml#c06-fig-0003), which provides a visual representation of how the functions interact.

The main program is contained in the primary MATLAB file, (Concurrent\_MOO.m), and begins at line 4, as illustrated in [Figure 6.4](#c06.xhtml#c06-fig-0004). At this point, the microscale input parameters are defined, including (Micro\_nelx) and (Micro\_nely), which represent the number of elements in the x and y directions, respectively.

The microscale volume fraction constraint *v*[m] is defined by (Micro\_volfrac), while (penal) represents the power of the SIMP power‐law function. The (Micro\_rmin) parameter specifies the radius for the mesh independence filter, which will be further explained in the Optimality Criteria section. Similarly, the macroscale initial design domain and spatial configurations are defined starting at line 6. The macroscale input parameters include (Macro\_nelx) and (Macro\_nely), which denote the number of elements in the x and y directions, respectively. The volume fraction constraint for the macroscale is specified by (Macro\_volfrac), which is defined at line 7. Additionally, the element aspect ratios for the macroscale spatial configuration are represented by (*dx*) and (*dy*).

In line 9, the maximum number of iterations for the optimization process, (maxIter), is specified. In our analysis, two models (i.e., the heat and the mechanical physics) are constructed to calculate the heat and mechanical compliances. As a result, two separate finite element systems are employed. Since the SIMP method is based on an Eulerian framework, where the element weight, represented by the design variable **x**, is gradually reduced rather than eliminating undesired elements during the topology optimization process, the finite element framework can be prepared in advance of the optimization. Both the heat and mechanical analyses utilize a structured bilinear element with a uniform length of unity, as illustrated in [Figure 6.5](#c06.xhtml#c06-fig-0005).

![Figure 6.3 MATLAB code flow for multiphysics concurrent topology optimization \[18\].](images/c06f003.png)

[**Figure 6.3**](#c06.xhtml#R_c06-fig-0003) MATLAB code flow for multiphysics concurrent topology optimization [\[18\]](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0018).

![Figure 6.4 The input data section of MATLAB main code (Concurrent\_MOO.m) \[18\] / Springer Nature.](images/c06f004.png)

[**Figure 6.4**](#c06.xhtml#R_c06-fig-0004) The input data section of MATLAB main code (Concurrent\_MOO.m) [\[18\]](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0018) / Springer Nature.

![Figure 6.5 Bilinear quadratic element used \[18\].](images/c06f005.png)

[**Figure 6.5**](#c06.xhtml#R_c06-fig-0005) Bilinear quadratic element used [\[18\]](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0018).

![Figure 6.6 Mechanical finite element section of MATLAB main code of the main function Concurrent\_MMO.m \[18\] / Springer Nature.](images/c06f006.png)

[**Figure 6.6**](#c06.xhtml#R_c06-fig-0006) Mechanical finite element section of MATLAB main code of the main function Concurrent\_MMO.m [\[18\]](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0018) / Springer Nature.

For the mechanical compliance analysis, the finite element preparation steps are outlined in lines 11–22 (as shown in [Figure 6.6](#c06.xhtml#c06-fig-0006)). The initial degree of freedom (DOF) index for each element is determined using the matrix (Macro\_nodenrs\_mech), which is stored in a vector format called (Macro\_edofVec\_mech). This vector is then used to calculate the indices of the eight DOF for each element, which are stored in the DOF matrix (Macro\_edofMat\_mech). This matrix facilitates the efficient construction of the stiffness matrix during the optimization loop. For the example shown in [Figure 6.7](#c06.xhtml#c06-fig-0007), which consists of 25 elements, the matrix (Macro\_edofMat\_mech) is

separators=""><mtable><mtr><mtd><mn>3</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>4</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>11</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>12</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>9</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>10</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>1</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>2</mn></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mn>5</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>6</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>13</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>14</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>11</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>12</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>3</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>4</mn></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mn>7</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>8</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>15</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>16</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>13</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>14</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>5</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>6</mn></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mo>.</mo></mtd><mtd><mo>.</mo></mtd><mtd><mo>.</mo></mtd><mtd><mo>.</mo></mtd><mtd><mo>.</mo></mtd><mtd><mo>.</mo></mtd><mtd><mo>.</mo></mtd><mtd><mo>.</mo></mtd></mtr></mtable></mfenced>
